# Airbus wins on value as NGSA leads fps by three years; Boeing settles at 47% of narrowbodies, and no veto stops a decision

BOEING PROPRIETARY. Boeing Product Development war game on the narrowbody/widebody game-theory dashboard, played by ExCo and Board agents.

Airbus won on value (ΔPV +$23.37B, against Boeing's +$0.66B). Rolls-Royce had the best yield (118.68%, ΔPV +$20.55B). CFM/GE lost most (−$38.29B); Pratt & Whitney (P&W) ended at −$2.93B. Why: Boeing launched fps in 2030 on the 10-year ramp-up, where its Board had recommended the 7-year, so NGSA (in service 2037) leads fps (2040) by three years, and Boeing settles at 47% of narrowbody deliveries from 2049. In 2035 P&W and Rolls-Royce formed a Joint Venture, on conditions both their Boards had set in 2030; it cost CFM/GE $7.09B. Twenty AI agents played four sealed rounds (2030, 2035, 2045 and 2050) on your dashboard, each company as an executive committee (ExCo) of CEO, CFO and operating head under a Board agent. No veto stopped a decision, and the Boards approved all nine orders put to them. The record shows the limits came earlier: each Board named in advance the orders it would veto, and no CEO ordered one. Whether those limits changed any choice is not observed: every final order was the CEO's opening lean.

## Summary

| Player | Final ΔPV $B | Yield | Objectives met |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Boeing | +0.66 | 100.51% | 0 of 2 |
| Airbus | +23.37 | 115.58% | 1 of 2 |
| CFM/GE | −38.29 | 76.07% | 1 of 2 |
| Pratt & Whitney | −2.93 | 97.34% | 1 of 2 |
| Rolls-Royce | +20.55 | 118.68% | 2 of 2 |

**Boeing took the 10-year ramp-up against its Board's advice, and NGSA leads by three years.**
On the dashboard the two ramps were $0.46B apart in Boeing's expected case, inside the $1B margin its rules treat as a tie, so the CEO chose on his stated preferences.
- The Boeing Board recommended the 7-year ramp for its better worst case (−$1.50B against −$1.96B if Airbus re-engined the A350 beside NGSA), but said it would approve either.
- The CEO agent, profiled on Kelly Ortberg, chose the 10-year: a bill $4.68B smaller ($60.32B against $65.00B) and about $6.0B a year of spend against $9.3B. He logged the $0.46B he gave up as a premium paid for that preference. His CFO (Jay Malave) and operating head (Stephanie Pope) recommended the same, and the Board approved his reasons.
- Malave's own worst-column test preferred the 7-year, as the Board did. He set it aside on "balance sheet first", recommended the 10-year, and "would not veto the 7-year".
- fps enters service in 2040, three years after NGSA. Boeing falls from 40% of narrowbody deliveries to 37% in 2039 while NGSA is alone. It then gains 1 point a year, from 38% in 2040 to 47% in 2049. On the dashboard the first in service keeps its lead for good.
- Airbus launched NGSA with no A350 Re-engine and no Delay Tactics, the case that came true. There the 7-year ramp was worth +$1.12B to Boeing against +$0.66B, so the $0.46B premium was the real cost. The CEO and the Board had also recorded the share cost when choosing: Boeing below 40% for some years, and its incumbency objective missed.

**Airbus finishes far ahead: its gain comes earlier and is discounted less, and its debt penalty is far smaller.**
- In PV the narrowbody gain is +$39.13B for Airbus and +$20.30B for Boeing. Airbus's starts in 2037, at an 8% discount rate; Boeing's starts in 2040, at 10.5%.
- The fps bill ($14.91B in PV) and the debt penalty ($4.73B) leave Boeing +$0.66B. NGSA's bill ($15.23B in PV) and debt penalty ($0.53B) leave Airbus +$23.37B.
- In nominal terms Boeing's operating profit ends the higher. From 2048 it earns more than Airbus each year ($16.57B against $16.23B in 2048, $16.86B against $15.94B from 2049), and over 2026-60 its gain over the status quo is the larger ($200.71B against $191.74B).

**The Joint Venture formed in 2035 on conditions both Boards had set in 2030.**
No message passed between the companies. Each Board had set, in its private guidance, a matching condition for the public record to meet: the partner's commitment, or a live airframe carrying the partner's engine. Rolls-Royce's published line named only P&W's commitment, and P&W published none. NGSA's selection of all three engine makers met the airframe clause for both.
- In 2030 the RTX Board (the Board of P&W's parent) ruled out a commitment until one of those held, and the Rolls-Royce Board set the mirror condition. Both CFOs opposed a 2030 commitment in their memos.
- In 2035 both CEOs committed on sealed orders, and both Boards approved: on their grids the commitment was never worth less than holding. The RTX Board had recommended it.
- P&W went from −$8.44B to −$2.93B, Rolls-Royce from +$18.26B to +$20.55B, and CFM/GE from −$31.20B to −$38.29B.
- The Joint Venture moved 4.8 points of narrowbody engines from CFM/GE to P&W in every year from 2037. On the dashboard's engine rules a Joint Venture puts the two partners level, and the extra share comes from CFM/GE. Rolls-Royce's UltraFan already had the larger share, so only P&W rose.
- Rolls-Royce's share and engine value did not change. Its gain is its R&D halving from $8B to its $4B share, less a $1.71B write-off on UltraFan spend beyond that share.

**Engine selections set the narrowbody engine split, and CFM/GE's share tracks Boeing's.**
Boeing put fps on CFM alone. Airbus opened NGSA to all three makers, on its Board's, CFO's and operating head's advice: CFM plus a second maker for supply security, and a selection that could never clash with Boeing's.
- CFM/GE falls from 76% of narrowbody engines to 51.7% in 2037 and a low of 50.5% in 2039. It recovers to 56.8% from 2049 as Boeing's CFM-only fps gains share. It keeps a majority in every year, narrowly.
- P&W and Rolls-Royce share the rest equally: 24.1% each in 2037, 21.6% from 2049. Rolls-Royce enters from 0%. P&W is above its status-quo 24% only in 2037-41.
- CFM/GE ends −$38.29B: $32.29B of engine value lost against the status quo (the narrowbody loss net of the GEnx widebody gain), plus $4B of ducted-engine R&D and a $2B strain charge for three live programmes.
- P&W and Rolls-Royce launched only once NGSA selected them, so no engine was built without an airframe.

**Nobody re-engined a widebody, and nobody used Delay Tactics.**
Both airframers held, each on its own rules. In 2045 and 2050 neither Re-engine cleared its own company's $1B launch bar even if the rival held, and both CEOs said the hold did not rest on the rival.
- Boeing: its rule allows one development at a time, and fps was in development until 2040. In 2045 and 2050 its Board would have vetoed the 787 Re-engine on its business case.
- Airbus: the A350 Re-engine fell short of the $1B bar, and its CFO and operating head each opposed it with a binding veto in their memos: Toepfer on his added-move test, Wagner because no widebody engine would be ready.
- Rolls-Royce would spend nothing on a new widebody engine without a Re-engine on the record. With Wagner's readiness test, that is the one mutual wait in the record (observed).
- Delay Tactics: the Airbus Board ruled out the bottleneck on integrity. In 2030 it ruled out poaching too: with no fps yet on the record, one move risked the $48.32B fine. In 2035 poaching was worth +$1.65B, but it took peak spend $0.07B over the Board's $5.2B cash line, and Faury kept the team's stricter condition, which on its own keeps poaching off.
- Measured ex post, the 2030 holds cost Boeing $2.20B and Airbus $3.30B (regret table).

**No veto stopped a decision: the Boards set their limits in advance, and no CEO crossed them.**
The CFOs' and operating heads' binding vetoes ruled options out before the CEO chose, never his opening lean. Details under How the companies decided.
- Nine orders went to a Board (seven in 2030, two in 2035), and the Boards approved all nine. The veto checks on the CEOs' decisions raised no binding veto or red-line flag, so nothing was revised or reverted.
- Each Board's guidance named, with numbers, the orders it would veto. None of them was ordered: among them a 737 rate increase beside fps, Delay Tactics, an A350 Re-engine beside NGSA, and a 787 Re-engine in 2045.

**Objectives: Rolls-Royce met both of its own, Boeing neither; five of ten were met.**
- Boeing missed both: 50% of narrowbodies in 2040-50 (38%, 43%, 47%) and holding 40% (38% in 2040). Its true low, 37% in 2039, falls between the tested years.
- Airbus lost its 60% share target (53% in 2050) but protected the A320 family (60% until NGSA).
- CFM/GE kept narrowbody dominance, narrowly (51.1% in its worst tested year), and missed its Open Fan objective (never launched).
- P&W met one: credibility, its engine on NGSA through the Joint Venture. It missed the other, a ΔPV of at least zero (−$2.93B).
- Rolls-Royce met both: narrowbody entry (21.6% in 2050) and widebody dominance (55.2% at worst).

## How the companies decided

The chain never needed its later steps: no binding veto at the veto check, red-line flag, revision, Board veto or reversion in 20 company-rounds. Each company ran the same chain every round. The Board gave guidance. The CEO framed the choice, and the CFO and operating head tested the options independently. The CEO decided; the CFO and operating head then ran a veto check on that decision, and the CEO could revise once. Any order other than hold then went to the Board, with one more revision and a reversion to the default if a Board veto stood. Two kinds of veto appear below. In their test memos the CFO and operating head could mark any option a veto, some binding; the CEOs treated the binding ones as ruling those options out before choosing. Only the veto check on the CEO's actual decision, or a Board review, could send a decision back, and neither did. The record shows what came earlier: each Board's guidance, and the tests the CEO wrote into his opening framing. Whether they changed any choice is not observed. Names below are the agents profiled on those executives, not the people.

**The Boards set the limits in advance, with numbers.**
- Each Board's guidance listed the orders it would veto and why, with the item's value in each case. No CEO submitted one of them.
- The CEOs answered each Board recommendation in writing: 167 answers, of which one says "Not taken".
- They wrote the Boards' tests into their own reasons. Ortberg, on the 787 Re-engine in 2030: "The Board's Downside test also bars the Re-engine".

**The ExCos agreed at the decision; their binding vetoes fell on the options left out.**
- In all 40 test memos, the CFO's and the operating head's recommended orders were the CEO's final orders. In all 20 company-rounds, the final orders were the CEO's opening lean.
- The memos marked 58 vetoes binding (50 CFO, 8 operating head, all Wagner's), all on options the CEO did not choose. The other 80 entries in their veto lists were advice, standing conditions or explicit non-vetoes.
- Two failed CFO tests fell on chosen orders. Malave's worst-column test preferred the 7-year ramp, and he set it aside. The volumes-first test of Rahul Ghai, CFM/GE's CFO, failed the Embraer partnership; Larry Culp, the CEO, answered it with a $0 premium declaration, as the team rule allows.
- 61 of the CFOs' 297 tests returned a fail, and 20 of the operating heads' 213.
- Some seats had little record of their own, and said so. Pope (Boeing): "My record is thin". Lars Wagner (Airbus): "My tests are role-derived; confidence Very low".

**Boeing's ramp: the one open divergence from a Board.**
- The Board: "We therefore recommend the ramp with the higher worst column", the 7-year, and "If you choose the 10-year, state why in the rationale."
- Ortberg chose the 10-year, as he had leaned from the start. Entering service alongside a 2037 NGSA "would time our airplane to theirs, which I will not do."
- Malave's own worst-column test preferred the 7-year, as the Board did. He set it aside: "Fully restoring the health of our balance sheet comes first", and "I would not veto the 7-year." Pope: "The 7-year also passes every test of mine."
- The Board approved: "We recommended the 7-year ramp; the CEO answered with reasons, which is not a veto ground." It recorded the incumbency objective as not met, with Boeing below 40% for some years.
- At the same review the Board corrected its own guidance. It had parked the 787 Re-engine to 2035, but on the 10-year ramp a 2035 Re-engine overlaps fps by 5 years, so that window was "closed by arithmetic".

**The Joint Venture: matching private conditions, one public trigger.**
- 2030, the RTX Board (the Board of P&W's parent): "No Joint Venture commit until Rolls-Royce's commitment is on the record or a live airframe carries a Rolls-Royce code". The Rolls-Royce Board: a commitment "fails partner alignment" until the same holds for P&W. Both were private guidance.
- 2035: NGSA's selection of all three makers met the airframe clause for both. Neil Mitchill, P&W's CFO, had opposed a commitment in his 2030 memo for booking unproven upside: "That condition is met, so the objection is cleared." Helen McCabe, Rolls-Royce's CFO, co-signed the commitment in her memo.
- The RTX Board recommended the commitment in 2035. The Rolls-Royce Board "would approve a commit, but we do not press it". Its CEO's red line allowed one once a live airframe's engine selection included P&W, the Board's own partner test.
- Both Boards approved: on their grids the commitment was never below holding.

**Airbus: the cash line and the team's stricter condition kept Delay Tactics out in 2035.**
- 2030: the Board's guidance ruled out the bottleneck on integrity, and poaching as "naked": with no fps on the record, one move could cost $15.74B through the $48.32B fine.
- 2035: poaching passed the value test (+$1.65B) and the fps-on-the-record condition. The Board still ruled it out: "Its cash, not its value, decides it this round." NGSA was spending $5.07B a year against a $5.2B line, and one move took the peak to $5.27B. If the CEO had not recorded setting aside the team's stricter condition, it added, poaching "fails on integrity as well".
- Thomas Toepfer, the CFO: "an override is arithmetically open, but the Board's own line still vetoes it". The team's stricter condition, that the move must change who enters service first, cannot be met on this dashboard: "On that condition alone, poaching stays off." Toepfer and Wagner also barred the bottleneck on integrity.
- Guillaume Faury, the CEO, did not set that condition aside, and "the cash line decides it". He logged the 2030 gap to the best plan on his grid, $3.30B, as "the cost of the red lines, not a premium".

**The widebody: both airframers held on their own rules; the one mutual wait was between Airbus and Rolls-Royce.**
- Boeing held the 787 Re-engine on different grounds each round: its one-development-at-a-time rule and the Board's downside test in 2030, its 2-year overlap limit in 2035, and in 2045 and 2050 the Board's $1B business case plus Ortberg's tie-break toward doing less and paying debt first. Ortberg in 2045: "I'm not holding because of Airbus either."
- The Airbus Board on the A350 Re-engine in 2045: "The Board does not press for the launch and will not stand in its way." Toepfer failed it on his added-move test. Wagner failed it on readiness: "The engine leads the airframe." Faury held in both rounds: "The decision rests on our own tests, not on our read of Boeing."
- No override was available, since the margin was under $1B. Airbus's rules let the CEO overrule one ExCo veto per game; that override, open only for poaching in 2035, closed unused. Toepfer in 2050: "Waiting bought the same case, smaller."
- Wagner's readiness test waited on a widebody engine, and Rolls-Royce would launch UltraFan WB only with a Re-engine on the record (observed).
- Rolls-Royce also held the Trent 1000 upgrade, which still carried a $5B strain charge while the Joint Venture ran. Tufan Erginbilgic, the CEO, in 2050: "the 2035 widebody plan assumed the strain would lift ... It did not." The misread cost nothing: on the Board's 2045 estimate, without the strain the upgrade was still worth about −$1.48B (and −$1.73B on its 2050 estimate).

**CFM/GE: a package within its Board's conditions, and a declined objective.**
- The GE Aerospace Board's guidance had set the conditions under which it would approve all three 2030 items (the ducted engine, the Embraer partnership and the GEnx upgrade), and Culp's package met them. The package was the best plan on CFM/GE's grid in four of six cases, so no premium was paid.
- The Board ruled out the Open Fan in every round, as a downside with no airframer to fly on: −$8.00B to −$57.60B against holding in 2030, −$19.17B in 2050. In 2050: "we make no end-of-game exception for the Open Fan objective."
- On the advice of Ghai, the CFO ("Book about −$50B"), Culp, the CEO, expected −$50.41B in 2030. The value after the round was −$31.20B, and −$38.29B once the Joint Venture formed.

Where the game ended, as a board cell: Airbus Launch NGSA + Do Nothing A350; Boeing fps 10yr Solo + Do Nothing 787; at fps 2040, NGSA 2037.

### The airframer stage game each round (engine makers as played; nobody moves later)

| Round | Pure equilibria (ΔPV Boeing / Airbus) | What was played |
|---|---|---|
| Round 1 (2030) | Boeing fps 10-yr + 787: hold / Airbus NGSA + A350 Re-engine + Delay Tactics (one move) (−3.40 / +26.67); Boeing fps 10-yr + 787 Re-engine / Airbus NGSA + A350: hold + Delay Tactics (one move) (+1.42 / +24.08) | Boeing fps 10-yr + 787: hold / Airbus NGSA + A350: hold (+0.66 / +23.37) |
| Round 2 (2035) | Boeing fps: hold + 787: hold / Airbus NGSA: hold + A350 Re-engine + Delay Tactics (one move) (−2.24 / +26.56); Boeing fps: hold + 787 Re-engine / Airbus NGSA: hold + A350: hold + Delay Tactics (one move) (−0.13 / +24.54) | Boeing fps: hold + 787: hold / Airbus NGSA: hold + A350: hold (+0.66 / +23.37) |
| Round 3 (2045) | Boeing fps: hold + 787: hold / Airbus NGSA: hold + A350 Re-engine (+0.34 / +24.26); Boeing fps: hold + 787 Re-engine / Airbus NGSA: hold + A350: hold (+0.96 / +23.26) | Boeing fps: hold + 787: hold / Airbus NGSA: hold + A350: hold (+0.66 / +23.37) |
| Round 4 (2050) | Boeing fps: hold + 787: hold / Airbus NGSA: hold + A350 Re-engine (+0.57 / +23.94); Boeing fps: hold + 787 Re-engine / Airbus NGSA: hold + A350: hold (+0.71 / +23.33) | Boeing fps: hold + 787: hold / Airbus NGSA: hold + A350: hold (+0.66 / +23.37) |

**The played outcome is on none of the snapshot's equilibrium lists.**
- Your snapshot's four Pure Nash all pair fps on the 10-year ramp with NGSA plus one Delay Tactics move, with one airframer re-engining.
- The game's cell, with no Delay Tactics and no Re-engine, is neither Pure nor Near-Nash: not at the snapshot's dates (Airbus +$25.05B, Boeing −$0.06B), and not at the game's own fps date of 2040 (+$23.37B, +$0.66B).
- Moving fps from 2041 to 2040 cuts the Near-Nash from 12 to 8, and every remaining one has Delay Tactics. One Delay Tactics move is worth $1.45B to Airbus at 2041, inside the dashboard's Near-Nash band of 1% of value ($1.5B), and $1.57B at 2040, outside it.

**The stage game is Chicken on the widebody through 2050.**
- In every round the two pure equilibria have one airframer re-engine and the other hold. Both survive to 2050 on this dashboard; on the earlier version only Airbus re-engining was left by 2050.
- 2045: Boeing gains $0.29B by re-engining if Airbus holds, and loses $1.26B if Airbus re-engines too. Airbus gains $0.89B, or loses $0.50B.
- 2050: Boeing gains $0.04B or loses $1.00B; Airbus gains $0.57B or loses $0.42B.
- Both holding was within $1B of each side's best reply in 2045 and 2050, the only Near-Nash cell in those rounds. In 2030 and 2035 it was not: Airbus's best reply was worth $3.30B and $3.19B more.

**Regret: Airbus gave up most, $3.30B in 2030, in a Delay Tactics move and the A350 Re-engine.**
- Boeing: $2.20B in 2030, falling to $0.04B in 2050, all from the 787 Re-engine. It was kept out by the one-development-at-a-time rule and the Board's downside test in 2030, by the 2-year overlap limit in 2035, and in 2045 and 2050 by the Board's $1B business case (and its downside test in 2045) and Ortberg's tie-break toward doing less.
- With Boeing's other orders as played, the 7-year ramp was worth $0.46B more, its logged premium. Regret counts the best full set of orders, a 787 Re-engine with the 10-year ramp (+$2.86B against +$2.39B with the 7-year), so the ramp does not appear in it.
- Airbus: $3.30B in 2030 and $3.19B in 2035, from one Delay Tactics move and the A350 Re-engine together; either alone was worth about $1.5B to $1.7B. Then $0.89B and $0.57B for the A350 Re-engine.
- Rolls-Royce: $1.71B in 2030, exactly its fold-in write-off. Holding UltraFan NB in 2030 and joining in 2035 would have brought the same Joint Venture without it. Had no Joint Venture formed, holding would have left it at −$0.56B, so the conditional launch was right when made.
- P&W's GTF2 had spent $1.67B by 2035, under its $2B Joint Venture share, so P&W wrote off nothing. Rolls-Royce had spent $5.71B against a $4B share.

**Calibration: the airframers were exact on the case that came true; the engine makers missed by up to $19.21B in 2030.**
- Airbus expected +$23.37B in 2030 and got +$23.37B. Boeing expected −$1.96B, in the case where Airbus also re-engined the A350, which the Airbus Board had ruled out in private guidance Boeing could not see. Its figure for the case that came true, +$0.66B, was exact.
- The airframers' own logged premiums and the value their fixed rules cost match the game master's regret to within $0.01B in every round.
- The engine makers missed badly in 2030. CFM/GE expected −$50.41B and got −$31.20B; P&W +$3.15B against −$8.44B; Rolls-Royce +$3.37B against +$18.26B. The actual selection (fps on CFM alone, NGSA on all three) was not a case in P&W's or Rolls-Royce's grid.
- In 2035 each put "rivals hold" first and under-weighted the other's Joint Venture commitment: P&W expected −$10.14B and got −$2.93B, Rolls-Royce +$15.99B against +$20.55B. From 2045 every company was within $1.31B, and all but CFM/GE within $0.05B.

**Only Boeing logged premiums: $0.81B in all, within its $2B cap.**
- Boeing declared $0.46B in 2030 for the 10-year ramp, then $0.30B and $0.05B in 2045 and 2050 for holding the 787 Re-engine. The last two were holds its Board said it would have vetoed anyway. By the Board's own rule, which measures premiums against the best plan that passes its tests, they need not have been logged.
- Boeing logged separately what its fixed rules cost: $2.20B in 2030 and $0.65B in 2035.
- Every other company declared no premium in any round. Airbus logged its gaps as the cost of its red lines and binding tests (2030-35), and as within the $1B by which its rules let the default trail the best plan (2045-50). CFM/GE declined to pay $37.34B in 2035 for the Open Fan objective.
- No company cancelled a programme.

**All 20 company-rounds kept to their own information.**
- An audit of every tool call found all 20 company-rounds clean: no company reached another's material, the dashboard or the game master's files.
- Each company's chain ran as its own workflow, in parallel, in every round, and nothing passed between companies.

## Round 1: decision year 2030

**2030: both new narrowbodies launch, and Boeing takes the 10-year ramp-up**

- Airbus launched NGSA, open to all three engine makers, for entry into service in 2037. Its Board, CFO and operating head advised CFM plus a second maker, for supply security. Its Board's guidance ruled out an A350 Re-engine beside it, and every Delay Tactics move.
- Boeing lifted its rule against a first-round launch: by its CEO's reading the 777X and MAX 7/10 were done and debt repair was on track. It launched fps on the 10-year ramp-up, on CFM alone, where its Board had recommended the 7-year. No rate increase and no 787 Re-engine.
- P&W and Rolls-Royce ordered launches conditional on selection for NGSA, and Airbus selected both. Both Boards ruled out a Joint Venture commitment until the partner's was on the record or a live airframe carried the partner's engine.
- CFM/GE launched the ducted engine, the Embraer partnership and the GEnx upgrade. Its Board's guidance had set the conditions under which it would approve all three, and Culp's package met them.
- The Boards approved all seven orders put to them.

### Orders (as adjudicated)

| Player | Orders | Public statement |
|---|---|---|
| Boeing | Launch fps, 10-year ramp-up (engines requested: CFM) | When I started, I said we'd do a new airplane when the market and the technology and we're ready. We've spent these years earning that. The 777X and the 737-7 and -10 are delivering. Our production lines are stable on the KPIs. Our balance sheet is healthier. I think we've turned this ship. I don't think the work is done. Today we are launching a new single-aisle airplane. It is a Boeing airplane, and we will own the design. We are taking the ten-year path, with entry into service in 2040. That is a planning date with headroom built in, not a stretch target. It is so much more important that we do this right than fast. We have tested the plan against a harder case, including disruption in the supply chain or our engineering workforce around entry into service, and it still stands. If the schedule moves, you'll hear it from us early, with the plan to fix it, and we'll reset once. A few things will not change. We will certify before we build at rate. We won't take on fixed-price development. We'll share risk with our suppliers on terms that work for both of us, and we'll keep the supply chain funded and healthy. We'll fund this from our operations and our debt capacity. Paying down debt stays our priority, and we intend to stay solidly investment grade. We are not changing production rates today. The 737 stays where its KPIs have it, and we are not starting a second development alongside this one. Doing less, and doing it better, is what got us here. This decision rests on our own readiness, not on anyone else's timeline. The financials will follow our production performance. We have a lot of work in front of us, and we're going to do it one step at a time. |
| Airbus | Launch NGSA (engines requested: RR, P&W and CFM) | Our core principles of safety, quality, integrity, compliance and security will remain at the heart of all we do. Today Airbus launches its next-generation single-aisle aircraft to prepare the Airbus of the 2030s. We are launching it on its technology clock. Entry into service is planned for the second half of the 2030s, once the technologies have matured, and not before. The A320neo family remains the backbone of our single-aisle business. Its production ramp-up continues in a disciplined and controlled way, and the people and suppliers who deliver it are protected. We have invited all three engine makers to propose engines for the new aircraft. Our aim is CFM and at least one other maker on the aircraft, to give customers choice and to secure supply. We will also strengthen the European supply chain behind this programme. Securing work packages and critical materials such as titanium and rare earths remains a priority. On the widebody side, the A350 family, including the A350F, remains our focus. We will fund this investment within our financial framework: disciplined investment in our future portfolio, and shareholder returns while preserving financial flexibility. On trade, we will keep working with industry partners on both sides of the Atlantic for a zero-for-zero tariff regime in civil aerospace. |
| CFM/GE | Launch ducted engine; Partner with Embraer; GEnx upgrade | Safety, quality, delivery and cost, always in that order. That doesn't change this year. So let me tell you what we know and what we don't. What we know: nobody has launched a new narrowbody yet. When one comes, we don't have a birthright on that next order. So CFM, together with our partners at Safran, is launching a new ducted engine now. That way a new CFM engine is there for whichever new airframe comes first. We don't expect it ready before 2036. We'll report against that gate every year, on test, not on slides. It is not our next-generation engine. That's still RISE. We're all in on that technology, and we're keeping it whole. When that architecture enters service is really not for us to say. The airframers and their airline customers will decide that. CFM is also teaming with Embraer. Any airframer that wants a CFM engine can have one, and nobody gets locked out. On the widebody, GE is upgrading the GEnx for our 787 customers. What we don't know: when the airframers move, and what they pick. So we plan on the low case, and we keep the balance sheet and our capital returns whole. You won't see us pointing fingers; you'll see us solving problems. We get paid in dollars, not in percentages. It's that simple, ain't that hard. |
| Pratt & Whitney | Launch GTF2 if selected | Pratt & Whitney has no new engine programme to announce today, and our share of narrowbody engine deliveries has held at 24% for four years. That is not where we want to be. We know we have to earn the dependable-engine reputation every day, so the GTF fleet keeps first call on our parts, our shops and our engineers. Control what you can control. Our commitment is this. In any round in which an airframer names Pratt & Whitney on a new single-aisle, as sole source or alongside another engine, we will launch our next-generation geared turbofan, GTF2. It will be ready six years from launch, so no earlier than 2036, with time on wing and full-life parts proven before entry into service. We have learned our lesson from the prior iterations of the GTF. We will not buy a selection: standard terms, no launch-style discounts. We're not in the early entry phase, the launch phase. We have made no Joint Venture commitment. Any partnership we ever enter would share risk and investment, never our gear or core technology. We will not speak for any airframer's plans, dates or rates. When we've seen it and when we feel good about it, we invest. |
| Rolls-Royce | Launch UltraFan NB if selected | So, let me be clear. Rolls-Royce will launch UltraFan for a new narrowbody in the round an airframer selects it, and only then. Entry into service no earlier than seven years from launch, with the full test programme and nothing compressed, and with support in place through partner shops before entry into service. We do not want to underestimate the risks. Partnership is our preference: we are open to Pratt & Whitney on aligned terms, and we are not committing today. Widebody is our franchise. No new widebody engine spend without a Re-engine, and durability work on the Trent fleet goes on. We won't do anything not profitable. I will say that categorically. |

### How each company decided (private during the game)

**Boeing** (Kelly Ortberg, Jay Malave, Stephanie Pope; Boeing Board)

| Step | What happened |
|---|---|
| Board guidance | Boeing Board: risk appetite: 4.5 of 5, easing slowly: at most one Solo fps launch, and only with the H1 ruling and Pope's readiness results. Our limits: no Board item below -$1B in any of the five plausible columns, and no plan more than $2B below Do Nothing in S5. Nothing that leans on … |
| Frame | Kelly Ortberg: initial lean: Orders I lean to: - fps: launch (10-year ramp-up); - fps engine code: 3 (CFM); - 737 rate: hold; - 787 Re-engine: hold. That is one Solo launch, standing alone. Why: - It beats Do Nothing by $2.86-4.30B in every column. That passes debt-first and doing less, better. - It sits … |
| Test (CFO) | Jay Malave recommends: Launch fps, 10-year ramp-up (engines requested: CFM). Options failing a test: Buffer test (slip leg), plan H: every fps launch via Embraer row; Meet and beat: ramp, launch (10-year ramp-up) (B) against launch (7-year ramp-up) (C) (ask 4); Rate step: 737 Rate Increase (ask 6); 787 Re-engine beside fps: strain and downside (ask 5); via Embraer outside the grid (ask 7). Vetoes: Any plan with fps: launch via Embraer (all four rows: with holds, + 787 Re-engine, + 737 rate up, + both) (binding); 787 Re-engine: launch in Round 1 (beside either fps ramp, or alone) (not binding); 737 rate: increase beside any fps launch (not binding) |
| Test (operating head) | Stephanie Pope recommends: Launch fps, 10-year ramp-up (engines requested: CFM). Options failing a test: Capacity follows demand: 737 Rate Increase (inference from one remark [BX-1329]). Vetoes: 737 rate: increase (any combination) (not binding); fps launch (7-year ramp-up) + 787 Re-engine launch (option G) (not binding); fps launch (10-year ramp-up) + 787 Re-engine launch (option F) (not binding); fps launch via Embraer (option H) (not binding) |
| Decide | Kelly Ortberg: Launch fps, 10-year ramp-up (engines requested: CFM) |
| Veto check (CFO) | Jay Malave concurs |
| Veto check (operating head) | Stephanie Pope concurs |
| Board review | Boeing Board: approve; approved fps |

CFO tests:

| Test | Threshold | Result | Numbers |
|---|---|---|---|
| Buffer test (slip leg), plan B: fps launch (10-year ramp-up) + 737 rate hold + 787 Re-engine hold | Risk-case column S5 (NGSA + Airbus Delay Tactics) must be no more than $2B below Do Nothing in the same column. Do Nothing S5 is -3.64, so the floor is -5.64. Run as the baseline, not as a sensitivity. | pass | Row 'fps 10-yr + 787: hold', column S5 = -0.78. Against Do Nothing (-3.64) that is +2.86, and 4.86 above the -5.64 floor. Full row S1-S5: +3.50 / +0.66 / +0.88 / -1.96 / -0.78. Worst column is S4 at -1.96, against Do Nothing's worst of -6.26. |
| Buffer test (slip leg), plan C: fps launch (7-year ramp-up) + 737 rate hold + 787 Re-engine hold | S5 no more than $2B below Do Nothing's -3.64 (floor -5.64). | pass | Row 'fps 7-yr + 787: hold', column S5 = -0.83. Against Do Nothing that is +2.81, and 4.81 above the floor. Full row: +2.20 / +1.12 / -0.42 / -1.50 / -0.83. Worst column is S4 at -1.50. |
| Buffer test (slip leg), every other row without Embraer (plans D, E, F, G, I and the rate/Re-engine combinations) | S5 at or above -5.64. | pass | S5 values, with the gap to Do Nothing in brackets: E 'fps 10-yr + 737 rate up' -1.52 (+2.12); 'fps 7-yr + 737 rate up' -1.57 (+2.07); F 'fps 10-yr + 787 Re-engine' +1.42 (+5.06); G 'fps 7-yr + 787 Re-engine' +0.45 (+4.09); 'fps 10-yr + rate + Re-engine' +0.68 (+4.32); 'fps 7-yr + rate + Re-engine' -0.30 (+3.34); D 'hold + rate' -4.38 (-0.74); I 'hold + Re-engine' -0.34 (+3.30); 'hold + rate + Re-engine' -1.08 (+2.56). All clear the floor, so my veto does not reach them and my objections to them are advice only. |
| Buffer test (slip leg), plan H: every fps launch via Embraer row | S5 at or above -5.64. | fail | S5 values, with the gap to Do Nothing in brackets: 'via Embraer + 787: hold' -18.83 (-15.19); '+ 787 Re-engine' -17.47 (-13.83); '+ 737 rate up' -19.58 (-15.94); '+ rate + Re-engine' -18.21 (-14.57). That is 11.83 to 13.94 below the floor. Under the team rule this is my binding veto. |
| Fundable when the 777X turns (Board gate; ask 2) | fps is fundable once the 777X is cash-positive (from 2029). A 2030 launch passes unless the brief shows otherwise. Fund it from operations and debt capacity, with no new equity (inference). | pass | 777X cash is near neutral in 2028 and positive from 2029 [BX-0547], with first delivery in 2027 [BX-0550]. Launch year 2030 is not before 2029. The brief reports no live quality, FAA, supply-chain or certification problem. Funding, assuming an even spread over development (inference; the board books the bill at EIS): 10-yr about $6.03B a year over 2030-39, against the brief's airplane operating profit of $6.7B a year (90%); 7-yr about $9.29B a year over 2030-36 (139%, so about $2.6B a year borrowed); via Embraer about $10.0B a year. On the 10-year ramp there is no new equity: operations plus debt capacity, since tax, interest and sustaining capex leave it tight. The board does not model the rating. It books each full bill as debt at EIS, taking debt/EV from 35% ($45B on $130B) to 81% (10-yr), 85% (7-yr) or 112% (Embraer), and the netted penalty is its price for that. I can support the investment-grade rating on the 10-year phasing standing alone, but not with a second bill running in the same years. Condition: barring a 777X cash path that missed my 2025 guide, or a sustained fall in 737/787 deliveries or margins. |
| Balance sheet first: debt penalty (ask 3) | Grid ΔPV already nets each plan's discounted penalty (α 0.30 × debt × (debt/EV)²), so never subtract it again. Between plans within ε ($1B) on the expected column, prefer the one that adds the smaller penalty (inference). | pass | The penalty is netted in every grid row (rules payoff; dashboard_game.md §2). The right set is dashboard_game.md §2: undiscounted 10-yr $19.1B, 7-yr $22.0B, via Embraer $52.5B, Re-engine alone $0.6B. These reproduce from the formula on $45B of debt and a $130B EV. For example, (45+60.32) × 0.30 × (105.32/130)² = 20.74, less 1.62 today, gives 19.1. The 16.3/21.7 set fits older bills of $55.25B/$64.47B (my own card carries them too), so it is stale. Discounted at 10.5% to 2026 at EIS (my arithmetic, not a grid figure): 10-yr about $4.7B (2040), 7-yr about $7.3B (2037), via Embraer about $13.0B (2040), Re-engine alone about $0.24B (2035), Rate Increase alone about $0.19B (2032). These are not additive for combined plans. The 10-year is the less-debt plan: its bill is $4.68B smaller, its penalty $2.9B smaller undiscounted (about $2.6B discounted), and it lands three years later. In the expected column S4, B (-1.96) and C (-1.50) sit within ε, so this test prefers B. |
| Meet and beat: ramp, launch (10-year ramp-up) (B) against launch (7-year ramp-up) (C) (ask 4) | Among plans within ε ($1B) on the expected column, prefer the one with the higher worst column (inference). | fail | Advice, not a veto. The expected column is S4 on Ortberg's provisional read; I have no Airbus evidence of my own. In S4, C scores -1.50 and B -1.96, 0.46 apart and inside ε. Worst columns are C -1.50 and B -1.96, so the rule prefers the 7-year by 0.46. B minus C by column: +1.30 / -0.46 / +1.30 / -0.46 / +0.05. B never trails C by more than 0.46, while C trails B by 1.30 in S1 and S3. Mean of the five columns: B +0.46, C +0.11. I set the rule aside on balance sheet first and log 0.46 as a premium. On the honest buffer: the 10-year is the schedule baseline with room in it. At launch, publish entry into service in 2040, no earlier. State the risk case as a condition: S5, 5 pp of share lost for five years from EIS, -0.78 on the grid. Hold any earlier internal date back as upside. On the 7-year, publishing 2037 means a nominal date with no buffer: the 777X lesson [BX-0550] (inference). |
| Cash path of each bill (ask 2) | When each bill lands, and whether two land together (heavy use, then neutral, then positive) [BX-0547]. | pass | Board bookings, nominal (with PV at 10.5% to 2026 in brackets): Rate Increase $2.94B in 2032 ($1.62B); Re-engine $5B in 2035 ($2.04B); 7-yr $65.00B in 2037 ($21.67B); 10-yr $60.32B in 2040 ($14.91B); via Embraer $100B in 2040 ($24.71B). Real spend if spread evenly (inference; real programmes are heaviest mid-development): 10-yr about $6.0B a year over 2030-39; 7-yr about $9.3B a year over 2030-36; via Embraer about $10.0B a year over 2030-39; Re-engine about $1.0B a year over 2030-34; the Rate Increase in 2030-32. Plan B carries one bill, in 2040: heavy use through the 2030s, then neutral and positive after EIS, as on the 777X. In no candidate do two bills land in the same year. But in F the fps and Re-engine spend run side by side in 2030-34, about $7.0B a year against $6.7B of operating profit, and in G about $10.3B a year. |
| Full cost of a slip or cancel | Cancel write-off = bill × years elapsed ÷ development years, and the debt penalty stays. H5: never cancel. Cost any slip once, in full (concessions, rework, learning curve, carrying cost). | pass | No cancel is in play. For scale, a cancel in 2035 would write off $30.16B on the 10-yr (60.32 × 5/10) or $46.43B on the 7-yr (65.00 × 5/7), plus the penalty. By 2035 the 7-year has sunk 71% of its bill and the 10-year 50%. A Boeing EIS slip is not on the board; S5 prices only a 5 pp share loss for five years from EIS. A real slip adds concessions, rework, learning curve and carrying cost [B-2349], and the 10-year ramp has more room to absorb one. Reset once, not serially. |
| Rate step: 737 Rate Increase (ask 6) | Only after stability, jointly agreed with the FAA [BX-0554]. On the board, $2.94B in 2032 buys +5 pp in 2032-36 (every year only if no new narrowbody is ever launched). It must add value. | fail | Advice, not a veto: the KPI gate is Pope's seat. Gate: the brief reports no live quality, FAA or supply-chain problem. Value with fps launched is -0.74 in every column: B 3.50 / 0.66 / 0.88 / -1.96 / -0.78 becomes E 2.76 / -0.08 / 0.14 / -2.70 / -1.52, and C becomes 1.46 / 0.38 / -1.17 / -2.25 / -1.57. With fps on hold it is +2.30 in S1 and S3 and -0.74 in S2, S4 and S5. Cash is $2.94B in 2032 (PV $1.62B), with a formula penalty of about $0.34B undiscounted. 2030 is the only round it is offered, so declining is final. Hold. |
| 787 Re-engine beside fps: strain and downside (ask 5) | Count the $5B bill at EIS and any strain overlap with fps as cost. H4 allows at most 2 years of overlap. Advice only, unless the plan fails the buffer test. | fail | Advice, not a veto. On the board's measure, fps development runs EIS-7 to EIS (2033-40 on the 10-yr) and the Re-engine runs 2030-35, so they overlap 2 years. Strain is $3B × 2/5 = $1.2B undiscounted, already netted. With the 7-yr (2030-37) the overlap is 5 years and $3.0B, which breaches H4. Item value against B: +2.20 / +2.20 / -3.58 / -3.58 / +2.20; against C: +1.27 / +1.27 / -4.51 / -4.51 / +1.28. Row F passes my buffer test (S5 +1.42), so I have no veto. But a second programme while we deleverage adds about $1.0B a year in 2030-34 on top of about $6.0B a year of fps spend, and -3.58 in two plausible columns is a downside I would not take. Hold; revisit in 2035 only if the A350 has not re-engined and the overlap is within H4. |
| via Embraer outside the grid (ask 7) | Anything off the grid (risk transfer, partner capital) would have to close a gap of $13.75B or more to Do Nothing, and $15.19B in S5. | fail | The board books $100B to Boeing in 2040 with a $52.5B undiscounted penalty, against $60.32B and $19.1B for the Solo 10-yr with the same 2040 EIS. That is $39.68B more bill and $33.4B more penalty, or about $9.80B + $8.24B = $18.04B in PV. This matches the grid gap to B in S1 (-14.55 against +3.50 = 18.05). The board prices no partner capital or risk transfer, so nothing narrows the gap. It fails my buffer test and the balance sheet. |
| Premium in dollars (H8) | Report every doctrine or objective premium in $B; the total stays within $2B. Name the condition that would break the number. | pass | Measured on my recommendation (B) against the best plan that respects the hard rules and the Board's tests (Solo rows with no Re-engine or Rate Increase): S1 0 (B best at +3.50); S2 0.46 (C +1.12 against B +0.66); S3 0 (B +0.88); S4 0.46 (C -1.50 against B -1.96); S5 0 (B -0.78 against C -0.83). The doctrine premium is 0.46 in the expected column S4, on the grounds of balance sheet first [BX-0556] and schedule buffer [BX-0553], well within $2B. No objective premium: no plan reaches 50% by 2040. I note that on Ortberg's read the 10-yr dips below 40% for a few years in S2 and S4, where C holds incumbency at no cost; taking C for that objective would cost 1.30 in S1 and S3. Re-engine value forgone under H3 is a hard-rule cost, not a premium: 2.20 in S1, S2 and S5, nil in S3 and S4. Condition: the premium holds barring an fps schedule slip, which the board does not price. |

Operating head tests:

| Test | Threshold | Result | Numbers |
|---|---|---|---|
| On-time, predictable delivery (record [BX-1331], applied to airplanes by inference) | Can the plan's EIS be delivered as stated? Read the risk-case column S5 (NGSA plus Delay Tactics) against Do Nothing, and state how much of the plan's value depends on the nominal date. A plan passes if it keeps a positive item value in S5 and stays above the -5.64 floor. | pass | 10-year Solo: S5 -0.78, which is +2.86 against Do Nothing (-3.64) and 4.86 above the floor. $1.44B is lost from S2 (+0.66) to S5 (-0.78), or 33% of its S2 item value of +4.30. 7-year Solo: S5 -0.83 (+2.81 against Do Nothing). It loses $1.95B from S2 (+1.12) to S5, or 41% of its +4.76. Doctrine's slip prior for a clean sheet (profile.md §3, inference) is a central +3 years (range +2 to +5). A 7-year ramp launched in 2030 slips under it from 2037 to 2040, the 10-year ramp's nominal date. The 10-year therefore publishes a date we can keep. Via Embraer: S5 -18.83, which is 13.19 below the floor. It fails. |
| Two-front strain and engineering capacity (doctrine) | A Solo fps and a 787 Re-engine overlap at most 2 years (H4). Strain = $3B x overlap / 5, with the fps counted from EIS-7 to EIS and the Re-engine from EIS-5 to EIS. | pass | Recommended plan (fps alone, 787 Re-engine hold): 0 years of overlap and $0 of strain on either ramp. Reference rows: (a) 10-year plus Re-engine. The fps counts 2033-40 and the Re-engine 2030-35, so the overlap is 2 years (2033-35) and the strain $1.2B undiscounted. That sits at the H4 limit on the board, but on the real calendar both programmes are in development side by side for 2030-35, which is against one development at a time [B-0341]. The Re-engine item falls from +3.30 (fps hold, S1) to +2.20 beside the 10-year, so 1.10 of strain and extra debt is netted in. (b) 7-year plus Re-engine: 5 years of overlap (2030-35) and $3.0B of strain. This fails H4. |
| Ramp choice, 7- or 10-year (inference) | Which ramp can operations deliver predictably with the workforce and suppliers in the brief? State the strain and EIS of each. | pass | 10-year: EIS 2040, strain 0. The $60.32B bill spreads to about $6.03B a year over 2030-40 (linear, as the rules' cancel write-off implies; inference). 7-year: EIS 2037, strain 0. Its $65.00B is about $9.29B a year over 2030-37, a 54% heavier annual engineering and cash load. Both can be delivered standing alone. The 10-year carries the headroom to absorb hiccups the way the 2024 restart was planned [BX-1275, B-2295]: its nominal date equals the 7-year ramp's date under the central slip. Grid: the 10-year is ahead by 1.30 in S1 and S3 and by 0.05 in S5; the 7-year is ahead by 0.46 in S2 and S4 (inside epsilon) and has the higher worst column (-1.50 against -1.96). My advice is the 10-year. The 7-year also passes every test of mine. |
| Invest or partner on IP (record [BX-1330, BX-1333], applied by inference) | Solo fps where Boeing owns the airframe IP. Go via Embraer only if the partner adds capital or capacity Boeing lacks. | pass | Solo passes; via Embraer fails. Embraer adds no capacity: the same 2040 EIS as the 10-year ramp, and no strain to halve because no second programme runs. It adds no capital either. Boeing's bill is $100B against $60.32B (+$39.68B), and the undiscounted debt penalty is $52.5B against $19.1B. The Embraer item value is -14.55 / -13.75 / -14.56 / -13.75 / -15.19 (S1-S5), and the route shares airframe IP we should keep. |
| KPI gate for the 737 Rate Increase: my veto (doctrine) | No Rate Increase while a quality, FAA or supply-chain problem is live. Steps of 5, at least 6 months apart, KPI-gated. Supplier readiness is a hard gate. | pass | The 2030 brief reports no live quality, FAA, certification or supply-chain problem. On the board, the 737 and 787 delivered steadily: 800 NB (40.0%) and 100 WB (58.8%) a year, flat 2026-29. The board carries no KPI data: the 2-3 stable months at rate [BX-1271] and 47 a month cleared in May 2026 [BG-0069] are company record passed by the CEO, not my record and not in the brief. A step ordered now lands in 2032, well over 6 months after any prior step. The gate passes, so the KPI veto is not invoked and does not bind. Supplier caveat (doctrine): beyond 47 the supply chain binds [B-2340], and an fps ramp would load the same base. |
| Supplier readiness for the fps ramp (doctrine) | No live supply-chain problem; fragile suppliers kept funded and hot; the plan survives the risk case (unattributed bottleneck or talent poaching) with value above Do Nothing. | pass | The brief reports no supply-chain problem in 2030. Spirit inside since December 2025 [BG-0064] is company record via the CEO, not mine. The brief names no fragile supplier, so I name none. Doctrine: keep fragile suppliers above Boeing's rate and funded [B-2330, BX-1243, B-2264], with cost-plus or back-to-back terms. The risk case S5 costs 5 pp for 5 years from fps EIS: $1.44B (10-year) and $1.95B (7-year) against S2, with item value still +2.86 / +2.81. Our answer to a bottleneck: embed staff, buffer, requalify, fund, acquire. Never cancel (H5), and attribute nothing to Airbus before exposure. |
| Boeing-ready: MAX 7/10, 777X, no production-certification concurrency (doctrine; company record via CEO) | MAX 7/10 certified and delivering; 777X delivering; no certification trouble open (teams.md §9: treat as done unless a certification inject is live). The fps plan builds no production airplanes before certification. | pass | The brief reports no certification or FAA problem in 2030. MAX 7/10 certification in 2026 [BX-1873] and the 777X first delivery in 2027 [BX-0550] are not in the brief. I accept them under the §9 script. They are company record, not mine. No production-certification concurrency [BX-1263, B-2228]: neither ramp requires it on the board. It is a plan requirement, and the 10-year ramp leaves the more room to certify before building at rate. |
| Capacity follows demand: 737 Rate Increase (inference from one remark [BX-1329]) | Back the Rate Increase only where it adds value against the same plan without it, in the expected column. | fail | This result applies to the Rate Increase only; the recommended plan holds the rate. With fps launched it is -0.74 in every column on either ramp (10-year 2.76 against 3.50; 7-year 1.46 against 2.20). With fps on hold it is +2.30 in S1 and S3 and -0.74 in S2, S4 and S5. The CEO's expected column is S4 (S2 close), so the item is -0.74 there. Cost $2.94B in 2032. It does not earn the capacity: hold. |
| Working capital and cash conversion (record [BX-1327, BX-1332], applied by inference) | For each option: when the bill lands and what it does to cash, as a number, with a sensitivity per unit of change. | pass | 10-year: $60.32B booked at EIS 2040 (about $6.03B a year over 2030-40); undiscounted debt penalty $19.1B. 7-year: $65.00B at 2037 (about $9.29B a year); $22.0B. Via Embraer: $100B at 2040 (about $10.0B a year); $52.5B. Re-engine: $5B at 2035 (about $1.0B a year over 2030-35); $0.6B alone. Rate Increase: $2.94B in 2032. Sensitivity: each year of EIS pulled forward costs about $1.56B of bill ($4.68B over 3 years) and about $1.0B of undiscounted penalty ($2.9B over 3 years). Debt is $45B on an EV of $130B. Penalty and strain are already netted in the grid, so do not subtract them twice. The 10-year Solo is the best cash conversion of the launches. Malave's veto decides the slip leg. |

CFM is always available. | pass | Code 3 (CFM) is always available by EIS, as a LEAP derivative at worst. If NGSA also takes code 3, the later airframe moves to code 7, with no payoff change. Another maker counts only with a public engine-ready date on or before EIS: GTF2 6 years after launch, UltraFan NB 7, the Joint Venture 6 after forming. Rounds fall in 2030 and 2035, so only a 2030 engine launch makes 2037. A 2035 launch is ready 2041-42 and misses even 2040, unless a Joint Venture folds in a further-along solo programme. Durability: require a matured engine before EIS [BX-1298, BX-1325]. CFM's load across both airframers' rates [BX-1264] is not on the board. |

Board review:

| Board item | Value | Decision | Ground | Alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|---:|
| fps | launch (10-year ramp-up) | approve | Every test passes; engine code 3 (CFM) rides with the launch. (1) Unresolved objection: Malave's only binding veto (every launch via Embraer row, S5 -17.47 to -19.58) is out of the orders. Pope ran her KPI veto check and did not invoke it. Both veto checks concur with no red-line breach, and Pope's one flag (7-yr + Re-engine, H4) is struck. (2) Business case: the submitted row 'fps 10-yr + 787: hold' (+3.50 / +0.66 / +0.88 / -1.96 / -0.78) minus Do Nothing (0.00 / -3.64 / -2.62 / -6.26 / -3.64) gives an item value of +3.50 / +4.30 / +3.50 / +4.30 / +2.86. That is +4.30 in the expected column S4, above the $1B epsilon. (3) Rating first: S5 is -0.78, which is +2.86 against Do Nothing and 4.86 above the -5.64 floor. (4) Downside: the lowest item value is +2.86, so no column of the five plausible ones is below -$1B. (5) Boeing ready. Market: passes. Technology: EIS 2040 is not before 2035. Balance sheet: Malave's buffer test passes, and 'Fundable when the 777X turns' passes (777X cash-positive from 2029, launch in 2030). The written Round-1 ruling lifts H1 with evidence: 777X first delivery in 2027, MAX 7/10 certified in 2026, 777X cash positive from 2029, debt repair on the path set in July 2026. H3 is held. (6) Safety and production readiness: the brief reports no live quality, FAA or certification problem. Pope's KPI, supplier, strain and Boeing-ready tests all pass. (7) One development at a time: 787 Re-engine is on hold, so overlap is 0 years. (8) Premium: 0.46 in S4 (and S2) against the 7-yr Solo, at most $2B and logged with its reason. Our ramp recommendation (launch (7-year ramp-up), higher worst column) was answered with reasons. A higher-scoring alternative is never a veto ground. | — |

- Decision rationale: ExCo deliberation, Round 1 (2030), team rule of teams.md §9: I propose and decide; Malave's buffer test and the KPI doctrine can block.

1. Vetoes in force. Malave holds one binding veto: every fps launch via Embraer row fails his slip leg. In S5 they score -18.83, -17.47, -19.58 and -18.21, against a floor of -5.64 (Do Nothing -3.64). Embraer is out and I do not contest it. Pope ran the KPI check on a Rate Increase and did not invoke her veto: the brief shows no live quality, FAA or supply-chain problem. Pope's H4 flag on fps 7-yr + Re-engine (5 years of overlap) is correct; that row is struck. Neither colleague vetoes the 10-year Solo, so overrides is empty.

2. My four questions.
- KPIs stable? Yes. The brief shows no live problem, with 800 narrowbody and 100 widebody deliveries a year, flat over 2026-29. 47 a month cleared in May 2026 [BG-0069] is company record, not in the brief.
- Market, technology and Boeing ready?
  - Market: waiting is not free. Do Nothing scores -3.64 / -2.62 / -6.26 if Airbus moves.
  - Technology: H2 cannot bind; EIS 2040 is well after 2035.
  - Boeing: see the H1 ruling below.
- If certification takes longer? Read S5 first. The 10-year scores -0.78 there, +2.86 against Do Nothing and 4.86 above the floor.
- Doing less, better? The 10-year Solo beats Do Nothing by +3.50 / +4.30 / +3.50 / +4.30 / +2.86 in S1-S5, so it is above ε in every column. It carries the smallest bill of the launches.

3. H1 ruling (no fps in Round 1): lifted for 2030. Its 2026 grounds were unfinished developments and unrepaired debt [B-2326, BX-1305], and they no longer hold:
- the 777X is delivering from 2027 [BX-0550];
- the MAX 7/10 anti-ice fix certified in 2026 [BX-1873, BX-1299];
- 777X cash was near neutral in 2028 and positive from 2029 [BX-0547], so Malave's 'Fundable when the 777X turns' test passes for a 2030 launch;
- debt repair ran on the path I set at Farnborough, about two more years with a launch around 2029-30 [BG-0068, B-2327];
- Spirit is inside [BG-0064].
The post-2025 outcomes rest on the teams.md §9 script and Board-file record, not on my own words, and the brief reports no certification trouble. My FAA-process gate [BX-1309] therefore passes.

4. H3 ruling, first half (no 787 Re-engine in Round 1): held on a re-founded ground. The literal 2026 ground (the 777X first) has lapsed. The principle has not: one major development at a time [B-0341, B-1526, B-1393], finish what is in hand first [B-2326], do less and do it better [BX-1239]. From 2030 the program in hand is the fps. The Board's Downside test also bars the Re-engine: -3.58 in S3 and S4 beside the 10-year. H3's second half, H4, H5, H6 and H8 apply as written.

5. The ramp, my main call. In my expected column S4 (doctrine's reading of Airbus, profile §7; my own confidence on Airbus is Low), C (7-year) scores -1.50 and B (10-year) -1.96. That is 0.46 apart, inside ε, so doctrine breaks the tie. I take the 10-year:
- Doing less [BX-1239]: the bill is $4.68B smaller ($60.32B against $65.00B). The undiscounted debt penalty is $2.9B lower ($19.1B against $22.0B; Malave confirms the 16.3/21.7 set on my card is stale), about $2.6B lower discounted.
- Headroom [BX-1275, B-2295, BX-1241]: about $6.0B a year of spend against about $9.3B. The 7-year would need about $2.6B a year borrowed above airplane operating profit. Malave supports the rating only on the 10-year phasing.
- Honest date: Pope's clean-sheet slip prior (central +3 years, doctrine) puts a 7-year launch at 2040 anyway. So 2040 is a date we can meet and maybe beat [BX-0553].
- Robustness: B leads C by 1.30 in S1 and S3 and by 0.05 in S5, and trails by only 0.46 in S2 and S4. Its five-column mean is +0.46 against +0.11.
- Airbus does not set the date [B-2313]. The 7-year's one structural edge is entering service in 2037, the same year as an NGSA launched in 2030. Choosing it for that edge would time our airplane to theirs, which I will not do.
I log 0.46 as a doctrine premium, within the $2B cap. The Board recommended the 7-year for its higher worst column. I take that seriously, but the 10-year's worst column (-1.96) is still $4.30B above Do Nothing's in the same column. Doctrine (profile §11.4) tilts the same way as my record here.

6. Rate Increase: hold. Beside any fps launch its item value is -0.74 in every column (B 3.50 / 0.66 / 0.88 / -1.96 / -0.78 becomes 2.76 / -0.08 / 0.14 / -2.70 / -1.52). It fails the value condition and the Board's business case. Declining in 2030 is final, but it costs nothing in any column beside the fps. Airbus played no part in this reasoning (H6).

7. Embraer: out on Malave's binding veto and Pope's IP test. It costs $39.68B more bill for the same 2040 EIS.

8. Board items and rows.
- fps launch (10-year ramp-up) (with code 3 riding with it). Submitted row: +3.50 / +0.66 / +0.88 / -1.96 / -0.78. With the item at its default (Do Nothing): 0.00 / -3.64 / -2.62 / -6.26 / -3.64. Item value: +3.50 / +4.30 / +3.50 / +4.30 / +2.86. It is above $1B in the expected column, never below -$1B, and S5 clears the floor by 4.86.
- No other Board item: the rate and the Re-engine are held.
- Premium 0.46 (S4, doctrine). Hard-rule cost from H3: 2.20 in S1, S2 and S5, nil in S3 and S4.

Where doctrine, not my record, drove a choice: the expected column (profile §7); the re-founded H3 ground (company record on one development at a time); engine code 3 (Low on my record; doctrine and inference); the supplier-readiness and strain tests (Pope's seat is doctrine-played).
- Expected scenario: S4 (Airbus launches NGSA and an A350 Re-engine), with S2 a close second. This is doctrine's reading of Airbus (profile §7); my own record on Airbus is Low. The decision does not hang on it: the 10-year Solo beats Do Nothing by more than ε in all five columns, and B and C are within ε in both S2 and S4.. Best grid plan there: In S4, the best grid row is fps 7-yr + 787: hold at -1.50. It is also the best plan that respects the hard rules and the Board's tests. My plan, fps 10-yr + 787: hold (rate hold), scores -1.96 there: 0.46 behind, inside ε, and logged as a doctrine premium. In S2 the best grid row is fps 10-yr + 787 Re-engine at +2.86, which H3 (my Round-1 ruling) and the Board's Downside test exclude; among H3-legal rows the 7-year leads at +1.12, against +0.66 for the 10-year.. Premium +0.46 $B: This is a doctrine premium of $0.46B in the expected column S4, and the same in S2: launch (10-year ramp-up) (-1.96) against launch (7-year ramp-up) (-1.50), measured against the best plan that respects the hard rules and the Board's tests.

Grounds:
- doing less and doing it better [BX-1239]: a bill $4.68B smaller and a debt penalty $2.9B lower undiscounted;
- right than fast, with headroom [BX-1241, BX-1275]: about $6.0B a year of spend against $9.3B;
- balance sheet first [BX-1305, BX-0556];
- a 2040 date that holds under doctrine's clean-sheet slip prior [BX-0553].

The premium is nil in S1, S3 and S5, where the 10-year is ahead. There is no objective premium, since no plan reaches 50% by 2040.

Logged separately as a hard-rule cost, not a premium: the 787 Re-engine forgone under my H3 ruling, 2.20 in S1, S2 and S5 and nil in S3 and S4. The total doctrine plus objective premium is 0.46, within the $2B cap (H8). The figure holds only if the fps schedule does not slip, which the board does not price.
- Predictions: My record gives me little on Airbus (Low), so these lean on doctrine.

Airbus:
- Most likely in 2030: an NGSA launch (S2 or S4), with roughly even odds of an A350 Re-engine alongside it.
- Any covert Delay Tactics would only show around our 2040 EIS. I attribute nothing before exposure.

Engines:
- CFM stays our available engine by 2040 under code 3. Any rival maker's new narrowbody engine counts only with a public ready date on or before 2040.

Boeing on the board, under my orders:
- Narrowbody share holds at 40% through the 2030s unless NGSA enters service in 2037. In that case it dips to about 37% by 2040, then recovers toward 47% over the 2040s once the fps is in service.
- Widebody share holds at 58.8% unless the A350 re-engines (from 2035), in which case it erodes.
- ΔPV by column: +3.50 / +0.66 / +0.88 / -1.96 / -0.78.

Round 2 (2035):
- I expect to revisit the 787 Re-engine only if the A350 has not re-engined and the overlap stays within H4.
- I expect no cancel of the fps under any column (H5).
- Expected ΔPV −1.96 $B; board after the round +0.66 $B

- Other moves: Conservative EIS disclosure at launch (public); No fixed-price development; risk-sharing supplier terms (public); Certify before building at rate (public); Fund from operations and debt capacity; keep deleveraging (public); Keep fragile suppliers funded and hot (private); Engine request: code 3 (CFM), durability first (private); Independent estimate check and early-warning rule (private); 787 Re-engine parked to 2035 on conditions; widebody growth from Charleston (private)

A350 $0, strain $0, Delay Tactics $0. Peak $5.07B in each year 2030-36. Headroom: $0.13B (2.5%) to $5.2B, or $0.15B to the unrounded $5.23B. How thin that is, any one of these breaks the line: (a) a total overrun above $0.88B (2.5% of the bill) spread evenly; (b) any single year 2.5% above the average, since even phasing is my assumption and not a schedule; (c) a euro below about $1.07 (5.07 / 4.753 = 1.068); (d) one Delay Tactics move against a 7-year fps (S3): $0.14B a year, peak $5.22B; (e) the debt penalty (about $1.2B before discounting) if it were treated as cash and spread: $0.17B a year, peak $5.25B. The debt penalty is a board charge already in the grid ΔPV, not programme cash. Nothing else sits on the line in 2030-36, because the €5bn buyback runs 2026-29. |

That is $1.47B over the line. At the $30.07B bill it is $5.90B, still a fail. Preview: an A350 Re-engine launched in any year from 2032 to 2036 overlaps NGSA development by 5 down to 1 years. Strain is always $0.60B per overlap year, so the peak is always $6.67B. A 2035 launch overlaps 2035-36 with $1.2B of strain: $6.67B in 2035 and 2036, and $6.07B even without strain. It meets the 2-year overlap limit but fails peak cash. The first launch year that passes is 2037, after NGSA's last cash year (2036). In game rounds that is 2045 (EIS 2050), and it would then still need to pass rule 3 and the robustness test. Your reading is confirmed. |

| Board items | Every order above €300m is a Board item; within $1B prefer fewer | pass | Plan A has one item: the NGSA launch ($35.52B, above €800m, so a Qualified Majority), with the engine code riding with it. Plan C would carry two launches. Plan E would add one or two more items (each $1B move is above €300m and an abnormal risk). A has the fewest. |
| Net cash, rating and shareholder returns (the Board's test, answering ask 4) | Net cash maintained; 30-50% payout and €5bn buyback fundable; no equity raise, no dividend cut; state support upside only | pass | Opening net cash is €12.2bn, about $13.4B. Before 2030, on a flat FY2025 base: FCF of $5.2B covers the €3.20 dividend (about $2.8B, scaled from €2,372m paid at €3.00) and the €5bn buyback ($1.8B a year, 2026-29), leaving about $0.6B a year. The buyback ends before NGSA cash starts. 2030-36 stress case (FCF flat at $5.23B, the full bill incremental, no customer advances, risk-sharing or state money): FCF after NGSA is $0.15B a year. At a 30% payout ($1.7B), net cash falls $1.6B a year to about +$2.4B at end-2036. Breakeven payout is about 36% of FY2025 earnings. Holding €3.20 ($2.8B), net cash falls $2.6B a year to about -$5.0B (net debt) by 2036. Keeping €3.20 with net cash at or above zero needs average FCF of about $5.9B (€5.4bn), 14% above FY2025. The public 2029 outlook (EBIT Adjusted €12-13bn, cash conversion about 1) points above that, but it is not in the case. No equity raise is needed in any case. The A+/A1 rating thresholds are not in my record; the stress-case net debt at €3.20 is the headroom flag. The debt penalty (about $1.2B before discounting) is a board charge already in the grid. Refundable advances are upside only and outside the case. |
| Cancel | Cancel only an A350 Re-engine after a 787 Re-engine enters service first and its remaining spend loses at least $1B; never cancel NGSA | not applicable | Nothing has been launched, so there is nothing to cancel. |

Board review:

| Board item | Value | Decision | Ground | Alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| NGSA | launch (2030, EIS 2037; NGSA engine code 7 rides with it) | approve | The Board approves this item. It is above EUR 800m, so the Board read it as a Qualified Majority item [AG-0049, AG-0011], and the papers set out both the finance case and the industrial case. Every test that touches the item passes on the round1.md grid. (1) Unresolved objection. Toepfer and Wagner both concur. Neither flags a red line, there is no override and the revision is null [AX-0027, AG-0026]. (2) Technology clock. The launch is in 2030, the last year of the 2028-2030 window. Entry into service is 2037, after the 2035 technology-ready year [A-0225, A-0409, AG-0063]. (3) Downside. The item value against hold + hold is +45.82 / +26.67 / +22.27 / +26.67 / +45.82 / +26.67 across S1 to S6. That is +26.67 in the CEO's expected scenario, S2, and a gain in every plausible column. The submitted row stays positive even in its worst column, S3 at +17.28 [AX-0007, AG-0029, A-0364]. (4) Liquidity and rating. At $35.52B / 7, spend is $5.07B a year in 2030-36 against the $5.2B line, a pass with $0.13B (2.5%) of headroom. The Board uses the dashboard bill in place of the $30.07B on its own card, as its card instructs [AX-0022, A-0402, AG-0062, AX-0104]. (5) One clean sheet. There is one launch, with 0 years of overlap and $0 of strain [A-0343]. (6) Safety and ramp. Wagner passes the plan on safety, quality and the A320 ramp shield, with the ring-fence adopted. The supply-crunch test does not apply [AX-0015, AG-0038, A-0331, A-0303]. (7) Returns kept fundable. The plan passes the liquidity test. The buyback ends in 2029, before NGSA cash starts. No equity raise is needed and state money is outside the case [AG-0058, AG-0059, AG-0034]. (8) Engine code. Code 7 keeps CFM plus a second maker. This is not a veto matter [A-0243, A-0360]. | launch |

- Decision rationale: **Decision.** Launch NGSA in 2030 with engine code 7. Hold the A350. No Delay Tactics.

This is plan A, the company default for the last year of the window. It is one Board item, the NGSA launch, on a Qualified Majority, with the engine code riding with it.

I applied the team rule in order, on the brief's grid, which matches both memos and the Board's figures.

**Step 1. Delete red-line breaches, whatever their PV.**
- Plan C (NGSA + A350 Re-engine) breaks rule 6. It is two launches in one round, with five overlap years and $3B of strain. The $1B exception fails at -2.15 in S5 and S6 [A-0343, A-0401]. The Board also vetoes it.
- Every Delay Tactics row is deleted:
  - rule 4: no fps is on the public record, so a move would be naked and draw the $48.32B fine, -15.74 against A in S1 and S5;
  - bottleneck and both also fail the integrity pillar [AX-0073, AX-0081];
  - poaching on the NGSA-hold rows also breaks rule 5;
  - the Board vetoes all three this round.
- The rows with NGSA held and the A350 Re-engine plus Delay Tactics fall on the same grounds.

**Step 2. Survivors that pass both tests.**
- Plan A passes every test from Toepfer and Wagner.
- Plan B (hold + hold, the order default) fails Toepfer's robustness test: -4.99 in S3, negative in five of six columns.
- Plan D (NGSA hold + A350 Re-engine) fails Toepfer's robustness test (-4.80 in S6) and Wagner's A350F and Spirit absorption test.
- Neither B nor D comes within $19B of A in any column, so no override question arises. A is the only survivor.

**Step 3. Choose.** A is the highest survivor in my expected scenario, S2, at +23.37. It is also the company default, so the $1B default test is met trivially. Doctrine premium: $0.

**What the red lines cost, for the record.** These gaps are the price of the red lines, not a premium:
- S1: $1.75B below plan C.
- S2: $3.30B below C plus one Delay Tactics move.
- S3: $3.77B below the same row.
- S4: $3.30B below the same row.
- S5: none. A is the best plan.
- S6: $1.57B below A plus one Delay Tactics move.

**Step 4. Tie-breaks.** Not needed: no two survivors are within $1B. A also meets all of them: no early entry into service (2037, after the 2035 technology-ready year); zero concurrency; one Board item.

**Expected scenario: S2** (fps on a 10-year ramp, entering service 2040).
- Boeing ties a new aircraft to technology maturity "not before '35" [A-0570], to finished development programmes and to a restored balance sheet [A-0726, A-0785]. A 10-year ramp fits all three.
- The profile's prior (section 7) puts fps no earlier than 2035, which points to S1. A is the same choice there and is worth more (+45.82).
- The worst plausible column is S3, at +17.28. A stays positive in every column, and NGSA's item value against hold is +22.27 to +45.82.
- A 737 Rate Increase is not a column. It changes no programme (profile section 5, row 7).

**Own clock.** I chose 2030 because it is the last year of the 2028-2030 window, with entry into service in 2037, "around 2037" and "the second half of the next decade" [AX-0020, A-0409, AX-0006]. Boeing's possible moves did not set the year. Whatever Boeing does in 2030, NGSA moves neither earlier nor later and is never cancelled in reaction (rules 2 and 7).

**The NGSA bill.** $35.52B (dashboard_game section 2) governs. The $30.07B on the earlier cards, my own included, cannot be traced in the current mapping. At $30.07B, headroom would rise to $0.90B and no result would change.

**Peak cash is thin.** Spend is $5.07B a year in 2030-36 against about $5.2B, headroom $0.13B (2.5%). Any one of these breaks the line:
- a total overrun above $0.88B;
- uneven phasing, with any year more than 2.5% above the average;
- a euro below about $1.07.

So I attach Audit Committee monitoring, with re-phasing, never acceleration, as the remedy (my supply-binds reflex [AX-0054]).

**Returns.** The payout stays inside the 30-50% policy with no equity raise. Holding the per-share dividend at EUR 3.20 needs FCF of about $5.9B. I am bringing that to the Board openly as a flag, not hiding it.

**A350 Re-engine.** Kept in reserve. Wagner's capacity test would pass a 2035 launch (two years of overlap, $1.2B of strain). Toepfer's peak-cash test fails any launch before 2037 ($6.67B a year). Peak cash is the Board's line, so the CFO's test governs. On present numbers the earliest launch is the 2045 round, and never after a 787 Re-engine (rule 3).

**Delay Tactics.** Off. I do not set aside the team's stricter condition that the move must change who enters service first. On this board a move shifts share, not entry into service, so the condition keeps it off. Toepfer's preview also shows a 2035 move breaking the cash line while NGSA spends.

**Engine code: 7.** Both colleagues and the Board recommend CFM plus a second maker. Only a P&W or RR engine launched this round can be ready by 2037. Code 7 selects both, CFM backstops, and code 7 is in no barred pair [A-0243, A-0360].

**Overrides.** None.

**Board-gate line: `NGSA: launch` (2030, entry into service 2037), Qualified Majority [AG-0049, AX-0026], engine code 7 riding with it.**
- Five pillars: pass. Wagner confirms safety and quality; the public line gives windows, not dates, and no covert move.
- Toepfer's tests: pass. Peak $5.07B a year in 2030-36 against $5.2B (headroom $0.13B). Robustness worst case S3 at +17.28. Payout inside policy, no equity raise, state support outside the case.
- Wagner's tests: pass on safety, quality, A320 ramp shield (with the ring-fence), production readiness and engineering capacity (zero overlap). Supply crunch: not applicable.
- Grid ΔPV, S1 to S6: +45.82 / +23.37 / +17.28 / +23.37 / +44.96 / +22.51.
- Item value against hold + hold: +45.82 / +26.67 / +22.27 / +26.67 / +45.82 / +26.67. It never loses $2B in any column.
- Expected scenario: S2: Boeing launches fps on a 10-year ramp (entry into service 2040), with no 787 Re-engine this round. This keeps the provisional reading from my framing. It fits Boeing's "not before '35" technology clock and balance-sheet preconditions [A-0570, A-0726, A-0785].

S1 (rivals hold, per the profile's prior that fps comes no earlier than 2035) is the close alternative. Plan A is the same choice there and is worth +45.82.

The worst plausible column is S3 (fps on a 7-year ramp, entering service 2037), at +17.28.

A 737 Rate Increase is likely [A-0820] but is not a column.. Best grid plan there: In S2 the best grid row is NGSA + A350 Re-engine + Delay Tactics (one move), at +26.67. It is deleted on company red lines (rule 6: two launches; rule 4: a naked move with no fps on the record) and on the Board's veto.

The best surviving plan is NGSA + A350: hold, at +23.37. That is $3.30B below the deleted row, a red-line cost and not a doctrine premium. It is also the company default and the order submitted.. Premium +0.00 $B: No doctrine premium is paid. The submitted plan is the company default and the best plan that survives the red lines and both colleagues' tests in the expected scenario.

The $3.30B gap in S2 to the unconstrained best row is the cost of red lines (rules 4 and 6, integrity), which are never traded for PV. It is not a premium inside the $1B or $3B allowances.

No PV is traded for the briefing objective.
- Predictions: Boeing, 2030:
- A 737 Rate Increase is likely (sold out, raising rates [A-0820]).
- On fps, S2 (10-year ramp) is my modal case, with a hold (S1) a close second. A 7-year launch (S3) is possible if Boeing reacts to our window [A-0035].
- No 787 Re-engine this round: its widebody focus is the 777X and 787 rate.

Engine makers:
- Our code 7 lets P&W's and RR's launch if selected orders trigger: GTF2 ready 2036 and UltraFan NB ready 2037, so either could be fitted at NGSA entry into service.
- A P&W-RR Joint Venture is possible.
- CFM is likely to pursue a ducted engine, with Open Fan not before 2045.

Market: no narrowbody share change before 2037 unless Boeing raises its rate, in which case Boeing gains +5 pp in 2032-36.

2035 bulletin: NGSA on the record (entry into service 2037), with Boeing's fps status known. In round 2:
- any Delay Tactics question would be reassessed under the Board's conditions, with Toepfer's preview already showing a 2035 move breaking the cash line;
- the engine code is revisited on published ready dates;
- the A350 Re-engine stays in reserve on cash.

Expected ΔPV (S2, nobody moves after this round): +23.37. Range +17.28 (S3) to +45.82 (S1).
- Expected ΔPV +23.37 $B; board after the round +23.37 $B

- Other moves: NGSA engine competition open to all three makers (public); Secure NGSA supply first, by owning or locking it (public); Ring-fence the A320 ramp from NGSA development (private); Open recruitment for the Airbus of the 2030s (public); Gated NGSA cash control with the Audit Committee (private); Capital allocation inside the policy (private); A350 Re-engine kept in reserve (private); Zero-for-zero trade advocacy (public)

| Test | Threshold | Result | Numbers |

No order in Culp's lean (option 4: -5.22 / -20.69 / -71.86 / -50.10 / -48.96 / -105.61) depends on an earlier date. |

New-NB entry 2037 (NGSA, or fps on a 7-year ramp) and 2040 (fps on a 10-year ramp, or via Embraer). Open Fan not before 2045. All sit at the board's development times. The statement is not yet written; I recheck every date in it at step 4. Ask (1): on the board, 2036 is the floor, and nothing earlier is acceptable. My record holds no ducted test programme. What is in real testing is the technology base: RISE has completed over 350 tests with an early durability focus [CX-0216]; RISE compact-core HPT blades are in dust testing [CX-0222]; open-fan noise has been tested with Airbus [CX-0013]; and the LEAP-1A HPT fix was turned on and turned off in test, then certified in December 2024 [CX-0009, CX-0225]. So 2036 is a test-gated floor, reported against every round, not a promise (inference). |

First new-NB entry 2037 for an NGSA (S2, S3, and the NGSA in S5 and S6) or a 7-year fps: the Ducted is 1 year ahead. Entry 2040 for a 10-year fps (S4) or fps via Embraer: the Ducted is 4 years ahead. The +10 pp counts from 2037 (S2, S3, S5, S6) or 2040 (S4). A 2035 launch would be ready only in 2041: 4 years after a 2037 entry and 1 year after a 2040 entry. Track record: my demonstration-type dates slipped (the RISE fly demo moved from mid-decade to 'this decade'; the LEAP-1B kit ran about a year late), so a 1-year margin is thin. A 1-year slip still meets 2037, and a longer slip still earns from its ready year. |
| Ready no later than the airframe: Open Fan (option 7, if launched; advice) | An Open Fan ready no later than the first new narrowbody's entry avoids the board's loss. Ready after it, the board takes 35 pp of NB share (20 pp with lobbying). First new-NB entry this round: 2037 (8 years before 2045) or 2040 (5 years before). It cannot be ready for either. Open Fan against hold: -8.00 / -57.60 / -35.40 / -41.46 / -51.84 / -16.07 (S1-S6). Open Fan + Lobby + GEnx against GEnx alone: -11.00 / -39.34 / -35.05 / -35.87 / -40.75 / -18.01. Culp's lean holds the Open Fan, so his plan does not fail this test. Ask (2), RISE status: real testing is under way [CX-0013, CX-0216, CX-0222]. The evidence shows no open durability failure, but it ends in October 2025. So the Ducted will not meet 20%. The 20% red line binds RISE as the next generation. For the Ducted it is answered by the defensive framing, not struck. I recheck the statement's wording at step 4. |
| Durability first | No plan trades durability or time on wing for fuel burn or schedule.
