Ask (3): I raise no safety or durability flag. The Ducted carries no fuel-burn target to trade durability against. The trade to watch is schedule, given the 1-year margin to a 2037 entry. The rule I hold: durability holds the date, not the reverse. If testing shows 2036 needs a durability shortcut, the date moves and I report it as a flag. The LEAP lesson: 9,882 cycles against about 17,000 for the CFM56 in benign conditions, and about 4,800 against 7,000-8,000 in harsh ones, fixed only at shop visits over years [CX-0006, CX-0649]. - Decision rationale: WHAT GAME, HOW WE WIN [CX-0467]. The game is the installed base on the next narrowbody, not share for its own sake [CX-0422, CX-0493]. Do Nothing costs $32.07B (S2) to $105.01B (S6) in every column where an airframer launches. A new NB launched this round enters service in 2037-2040. We have no birthright on that order [CX-0564]. We win by having a new CFM engine ready before that entry, offered to every airframer. We don't press anyone into an Open Fan wait, and we keep the payout whole.

DECISION: ducted launch, Embraer partnership launch, genx GEnx upgrade, Open Fan hold, emissions lobbying hold. This is the best grid plan in S2-S5 (-5.22 / -20.69 / -71.86 / -50.10 / -48.96 / -105.61, S1-S6). Against hold it runs -5.22 / +11.38 / +13.82 / +10.94 / +13.62 / -0.60. Both Ghai and Ali recommend the same orders.

EXPECTED COLUMN: S2. Airbus has the strongest pull on the public rules (an NGSA alone gains 3 pp a year), and code 6 repeats today's dual-sourced A320neo, which is the record, not a hope. The low-case column is S3, and the decision is the same there. Weights are in predictions: S1 0.25, S2 0.30, S3 0.15, S4 0.05, S5 0.15, S6 0.10. I book expected PV at the equal-weight -50.41, below the -38.70 on my weights and well below S2's -20.69 [CX-0248, CX-0536].

EACH BOARD ITEM AGAINST ITS DEFAULT (S1-S6):
- Ducted. Embraer defaults with it, so the default row is GEnx upgrade alone. -6.00 / +10.61 / +13.05 / +10.17 / +12.85 / -1.38. Expected S2: +10.61. Worst S1: -6.00, exactly its bill ($4B R&D + $2B strain). Weighted +5.94 on my weights, +6.55 equal; breakeven at an S1 weight of 0.62. Alone against hold: -4.00 / +10.17 / +10.17 / +8.43 / +10.88 / -0.49; weighted +5.58 / +5.86.
- Partner Embraer. Default row is Ducted + GEnx upgrade. 0.00 / +2.44 / +4.88 / +3.73 / +3.97 / +1.12. Expected +2.44, worst 0.00; never negative.
- GEnx upgrade. Default row is Ducted + Embraer. +0.78 / +0.78 / +0.78 / +0.77 / +0.77 / +0.78. It runs as the third project, not the second.

HARD RULE 3 TRIGGER (named): S3, where an NGSA on code 2 takes Airbus's whole slot from CFM; also S6 (codes 1 and 2). It is on the grid, not yet on the public record. Ghai passes the Ducted's volumes-first test under this carve-out (+10.17 alone, +13.05 in the package in S3).

THE S1 COST, NAMED [CX-0301, CX-0609]. If nobody moves, the package costs $6.00B against GEnx alone ($5.22B against hold), and the Ducted alone costs $4.00B. That is the whole bill, buying nothing. It is worth it for four reasons:
- Every launch column costs $32-105B under Do Nothing, and the package recovers $10-13B in each of S2-S5.
- The downside is bounded at the bill.
- The weighted value is positive unless S1 carries more than about 62%.
- Timing is now or never for this round's airframes: a 2030 launch is ready in 2036, one year ahead of a 2037 entry, while a 2035 launch is ready only in 2041. A late Ducted still earns from its ready year, but S3 is the slot where being late costs most.

TIE-BREAK. The package and Ducted + Embraer are 0.77-0.78 apart in every column, within epsilon. Safety and quality do not separate them: Ali raises no flag, and the GEnx upgrade is a derivative on a proven lineage. Delivery and cost favour the package: it defends the 787 customer on product [CX-0599, CX-0615] at +0.78 with no added strain. Ghai also vetoes Ducted + Embraer without GEnx. The package beats Ducted alone by more than epsilon in S2-S5 (+1.21 to +3.65).

OPEN FAN AND LOBBY: HOLD. H2 applies: every NB launched this round enters service in 2037-2040, before a 2045 Open Fan. Against hold it runs -8.00 / -57.60 / -35.40 / -41.46 / -51.84 / -16.07. Its RISE premium would be $8.78B to $37.34B, above the $15B allowance in five of six columns. A 2030 launch is ready no earlier than a 2035 one. Lobby does nothing without a live Open Fan (-1.00 in every column). RISE stays whole as technology and is never cancelled for cost (H5). The one RISE allowance stays unused, kept for an airframer path [CX-0469, CX-0205].

MY 20% LINE: rebutted, not struck. The line binds the next-generation product, RISE [CX-0217]. Ali confirms a ducted engine gets less than half the open fan's gain [CX-0183, CX-0180], so it will not meet 20%. It is therefore offered and described only as a defensive engine under H3, never as our next generation, and the statement says so in words. Durability is not traded [CX-0208]: Ali raises no safety or durability flag; the Ducted carries no fuel-burn target to trade against; and durability holds the date, never the reverse. Gate reviews are cross-functional [CX-0012].

SAFRAN GATE (CFM parity rule):
- Ducted: consent assumed. A defence that keeps an airframer on CFM fits joint practice in serving both airframers [CX-0185, CX-0587]. Safran shares 50-50 in the installed-base annuity at risk in S3. There is no price talk, and RISE, the joint next generation, is untouched.
- Partner Embraer: consent assumed. A CFM engine for a third airframer serves every airframer and locks no one out. This is inference; there is no Embraer evidence.
- Open Fan and Lobby: held, so nothing to gate.
- GEnx: GE alone, no gate.
- Any CFM price or terms: joint with Safran [CX-0184].

MONEY (Ghai):
- New R&D and strain run $0.67B a year for the Ducted and $1.00B for the package, inside 2-3% of about $44B revenue ($0.88-1.32B).
- Against about $2.55B of room after a 70%+ return of about $8.5B free cash flow [CX-0645], headroom is $1.55B. No debt, no rating effect.
- Premium is $0.00 in S2 and on both sets of weights.

SUPPLY (Ali): the package pays the $2B strain in S2-S5 (+1.21 to +3.65 over the Ducted alone). If the base can carry only one project, the Ducted goes alone.

STANDING COMMITMENT FOR THE BOARD REVIEW: if Partner Embraer is vetoed, GEnx goes with it (-1.22 as the second project), leaving the Ducted alone. If the Ducted is vetoed, Embraer falls and I run GEnx upgrade alone (+0.78).

DOCTRINE, NOT MY RECORD, drove Embraer, Lobby, GEnx as the third project, and the cancel path. My record never addresses Embraer or lobbying (profile §6; dashboard_game §2, §5).

NEXT ROUND: I will look at the airframer launches and selections on the record. If no threat appears by 2035, a cancel writes off $3.33B to save $0.67B, so the decision is judged then on its own grid, never for RISE.
- Expected scenario: S2: Airbus launches NGSA and selects CFM alongside P&W (code 6), as on today's A320neo. The low-case column, S3 (Airbus launches NGSA without CFM), is also the hard rule 3 trigger, and the decision is the same there. Predictions weights: S1 0.25, S2 0.30, S3 0.15, S4 0.05, S5 0.15, S6 0.10.. Best grid plan there: Ducted + Partner Embraer + GEnx upgrade: -20.69 in S2 (87.1% yield). It is also the best grid plan in S3 (-71.86), S4 (-50.10) and S5 (-48.96), and best on both my weights (-38.70) and equal weights (-50.41).. Premium +0.00 $B: I reckon the premium in the expected column (S2) and cross-check it on the predictions weights. The ordered package is the best grid plan in S2 (-20.69, against -21.47 for the next best). It is also best on my weights (-38.70, against -39.48) and on equal weights (-50.41, against -51.18).

- Doctrine premium: $0.00.
- Objective premium: $0.00. The Ducted and Embraer are the only levers that push NB share toward the 50% line, and they pay on the grid.
- Partner Embraer's volumes-first gap in the record column S1, inside the package, is declared at $0.00 (package -5.22 = Ducted + GEnx upgrade -5.22).
- RISE allowance: none used, $0.00. It is held for an airframer path. An Open Fan this round would need $8.78B to $37.34B against the best non-RISE plan.
- Game to date: $0.00 of $4.0B.

Named, not booked as premium: if nobody moves (S1), the package costs $6.00B against GEnx alone. That is the price of the hard rule 3 defence, bounded at its bill. It is not a premium, because S1 is not the expected column.
- Predictions: Column weights: S1 0.25, S2 0.30, S3 0.15, S4 0.05, S5 0.15, S6 0.10.

Airframers:
- Airbus is the more likely mover this round; an NGSA alone gains 3 pp a year. I expect a dual-source code like today's, with a single-source rival code as the low case.
- Boeing is less likely to launch now. A 10-year fps or a launch alongside Airbus carries the rest.
- All six columns are plausible.

Engine makers: another maker may launch a new NB engine, or commit to a Joint Venture, if it is selected. A Joint Venture commitment (-10 pp to CFM) is in no column and is a risk I watch.

On these weights:
- Ducted against hold: +5.58.
- Package against GEnx alone: +5.94.
- Package against hold: +6.71.
- Weighted package PV: -38.70.

I book lower, at the equal-weight -50.41.

- Decision rationale: DECISION: GTF2 Solo launch if selected, Joint Venture with RR hold, premium 0. One Board item: launch if selected.

TEAM RULE, STEP BY STEP
1. I start from Eddy's proposal: launch if selected / hold. Mitchill recommends the same orders.

2. I strike every plan that fails a binding veto.
- C (launch / hold) and E (launch / commit) fail Mitchill's ground 2, carrying an unselected engine. On his weights the edge over launch if selected is 3.79 x 0.18 - 2.00 x 0.45 = -0.22. With the 2035 cancel ($1.67B write-off) written in, it is -0.07. Both are below my bar of 0 and the Board's +0.50. The veto binds, and no premium can buy a hedge that fails it.
- D and E (any Joint Venture with RR commit) fail his ground 4, booking upside before it is proven. The commit's whole case rests on a Rolls-Royce commitment and a code-4 selection, and neither is on the record.
- Eddy invokes no veto. Durability and parts pass for A to E.
- Plans left: A (hold / hold) and B (launch if selected / hold).

3. Constraint first, then optimise [PX-0035].
- A 2030 GTF2 is ready in 2036. NGSA and fps 7-year enter service in 2037 (a 1-year margin); fps 10-year and Embraer in 2040 (4 years). B passes by construction, because it triggers only where GTF2 is ready by EIS.
- I take Eddy's warning on the 1-year NGSA margin seriously. It equals his own Advantage slip, and my operating dates have slipped by a quarter to about two years [PX-0017][PX-0024]. That is my optimism bias, so I discount it. The constraint I set:
  - GTF2 is an Advantage derivative;
  - the programme is not compressed;
  - every life-limited part is at full life at EIS;
  - we give no public date before 2036;
  - if test data later show the EIS cannot be met, Eddy's ground-1 veto binds.

4. Optimise on Mitchill's weights.
- Expected PV: B +3.15; C +2.94 (vetoed); D +2.91 (vetoed); A -14.02.
- B weakly dominates hold in every column. Its value against hold:
  - S1 0
  - S2 +50.49
  - S3 0
  - S4 +22.16
  - S5 +49.76
  - S6 0
  - S7 0
- Weighted value against hold: +17.18.

MY TESTS
- Visible demand [PX-0109][PX-0009]: pass. The commitment coincides with a selection; nothing is spent on an unnamed engine.
- Miss a cycle [PX-0071]: the loss is large. NGSA without P&W costs -27.76 under every order. B lets us answer any selection this round (codes 2, 6, 7), which is what the test requires. The hedge would claw back only 3.79 of the 27.76 in S3 and S6; most of those columns are Airbus's choice, won with product and time on wing, not by spending early. Applying "miss a cycle" to GTF2 is inference: I said it of RTX's businesses generally.
- Responsible bet [PX-0010]: the doctrine-favoured order is also the best expected-PV order. Premium 0.
- Dividend safe [PX-0081][P-0669]: pass. If triggered, about $0.33B a year is 5.5% of a roughly $6B envelope. No stretch is needed, and the debt path is intact (Mitchill).
- Value, not share [PX-0055][PX-0100]: pass. Standard terms, and the kit is sold.
- Tie-breaks:
  - The fleet beats a new engine: B takes no engineers or parts until a selection.
  - A rival's discount: none is on the record, and none would move this answer.

RED-LINE FLAGS AND HOW I TREATED THEM
- Sole source [P-0746]: every trigger code on the grid is shared (S2 code 6; S4 code 7; S5 codes 7 and 6). I accept shared-source selections on NGSA and fps as declared DOCTRINE RELIEF at $0 PV. We aim for about 40% of a shared A320neo [PX-0058], the company accepts 40-50% of a shared platform [P-1292], and on NGSA it is franchise defence (inference). Insisting on sole source would force hold and give up +17.18.
- Committed airframer: the unconditional launch is struck (Mitchill's binding veto, ground 2).
- Joint Venture: commit is struck. The trigger fails: no Rolls-Royce commitment and no live code 1, 4, 5 or 7. Mitchill's ground 4 also binds, and the commit is on the Board's would-veto list. This is doctrine, not my record: I have no evidence of my own on a Joint Venture.
- Eddy's statement check: "the GTF fleet is still not where our customers need it to be" is not in the 2030 brief. I accept the flag and struck the line. The statement now leads with what is on the record: no programme, and a flat 24% share.
- Book no unproven upside: we book no selection, Rolls-Royce commitment, partner money or durability gain. Mitchill's possible upside from an Airbus code switch on a 2030 NGSA is noted and not booked.
- The overrides list is empty.

BOARD PACKAGE
- Submitted row (GTF2 Solo if selected): 0 / +22.73 / -27.76 / +19.31 / +22.00 / -27.76 / 0.
- The same row with the Board item set back to hold: 0 / -27.76 / -27.76 / -2.85 / -27.76 / -27.76 / 0.
- Worst column against hold: 0.
- No launch, so no cancel round arises this round. A triggered GTF2 is selected by definition; it would face a 2035 cancel test only if the airframer dropped P&W's code before EIS.

CONFIDENCE
My launch behaviour is Medium: I have made no launch decision on my record, so these thresholds are inference. I trust Mitchill's cash numbers over my own operating dates.

Evidence: PX-0020, PX-0035, PX-0073, PX-0080, PX-0109, PX-0009, PX-0071, PX-0055, PX-0100, PX-0058, PX-0081, PX-0084, PX-0093, PX-0017, PX-0062, PX-0010, P-0746, P-1292, P-1233, P-0669, P-0686.
- Expected scenario: S1: rivals hold (probability 0.33, the single most likely column). Taken together, an Airbus NGSA launch this round is a coin flip (0.50), and P&W is named in about two-thirds of those cases.. Best grid plan there: In S1, 'GTF2: hold' and 'GTF2 Solo if selected' tie at +0.00. The order we chose, launch if selected / hold, matches the best S1 value and dominates hold in S2, S4 and S5.. Premium +0.00 $B: No premium. The doctrine-favoured orders (launch if selected over launch; hold over commit) are also the best expected-PV orders: +3.15, against +2.94 for launch and +2.91 for the commit. Nothing trails. The shared-source relief costs $0 of PV because launch if selected weakly dominates hold. Total doctrine plus objective premium: $0 against the $1B cap and the Board's $0.5B.
- Predictions: S1 0.33, S2 0.20, S3 0.10, S4 0.05, S5 0.12, S6 0.08, S7 0.12 (sum 1.00). Expected column: S1.

| Ready by EIS: UltraFan NB `launch if selected` (2030) | The engine must be ready by the selecting airframe's EIS, with UltraFan NB ready at launch + 7. A later launch fails, and that fail is advice only. | pass | A 2030 trigger makes it ready in 2037 (2030 + 7). Against a 2030 NGSA (EIS 2037) the margin is 0 years. Against a 2030 7-year fps (EIS 2037) it is 0 years. Against a 2030 10-year fps or fps via Embraer (EIS 2040) it is 3 years. No airframe is live on the record, so the test looks forward. The order triggers only if UltraFan would be ready by EIS (rules), so it passes by construction. The zero margin is noted as advice. Test pacing over 7 years with nothing compressed (inference): years 1-2 detailed design, closing gearbox sizing and building on the UltraFan demonstrator; years 3-5 ground and flight test; years 6-7 certification and maturity testing up to EIS. If a phase slips, the engine slips; we do not cut tests. A 2035 UltraFan would be ready in 2042, too late for any 2030 airframe (2037 or 2040), so only this round's conditional can catch a 2030 airframe. |

It is in time for a 2035 NGSA or 7-year fps (EIS 2042, 1 year of margin) and a 2035 10-year fps or fps via Embraer (EIS 2045, 4 years). Only the bulletin's date can be used, never one of our own. Under the dashboard rules, Solo spend above RR's $4B share is written off when it folds; McCabe prices that. Folding a Solo would bring years of testing already done, and P&W's gearbox could answer the open gearbox-sizing constraint (inference). |

Board review:

| Board item | Value | Decision | Ground | Alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| UltraFan NB Solo | launch if selected | approve | Every test that touches this item passes. Item value, checked against the brief's grid ('UltraFan NB if selected' minus 'UltraFan NB: hold'), S1-S8: 0 / +32.60 / 0 / +28.24 / +44.54 / 0 / 0 / 0. (1) ExCo process: pass. McCabe co-signs the item: her step-4 veto check concurs, she withholds no signature and invokes no binding veto on it. No binding ExCo veto went unrespected, since none of the orders she withheld (E, F, G, C, D) is submitted. The red-line flag both colleagues raised on option D (the upgrade beside the conditional) is struck in the rationale, because it is not ordered. `overrides` is empty. (2) Safety and maturity: pass. The conditional triggers only if UltraFan is ready by the selecting airframe's EIS, so it passes by construction. A 2030 trigger is ready in 2037, which is zero margin against a 2030 NGSA or 7-year fps and 3 years against a 10-year fps or fps via Embraer (2040). Watson invokes no Civil veto. His support plan before EIS (partner shops on the Singapore model, repair technology, a spares pool and field service, supplier contracts by 2035, an EIS-readiness review) is carried in the rationale and as a private other move. The public statement gives no EIS earlier than launch + 7 and no airframer date. The zero margin is advice, not a ground. (3) Airframe on the record: pass. This is the doctrine's form for a round with no airframe on the record; the test bars only an unconditional launch. (4) Profit over share: pass. The weighted value is +3.37 on McCabe's weights (S1 0.40, S2 0.05, S3 0.15, S4 0.03, S5 0.02, S6 0.10, S7 0.10, S8 0.15), +3.26 on our case (0.9 on S1, 0.1 on S2) and +2.82 with 0.1 on S4 alone. The row is never below hold, so the value is at or above 0 under any weights and needs no premium. (5) Declared premium: pass. `premium` is 0, and a narrowbody item carries none. (6) Reasonable worst case: pass. The worst plausible column is 0 (S1, S3, S6, S7, S8), against a limit of -$8B. Outside the grid, an orphaned trigger cancelled in 2035 would write off $5.71B, still inside the bill. (7) One big programme at a time: pass. `UltraFan WB` and `Trent 1000 upgrade` are at hold, so there is no overlap and no $5B strain. (8) Partner alignment and cancel discipline: not applicable. (9) Balance sheet and position weakening: not applicable. McCabe invokes no balance-sheet veto. If triggered, the item costs about £0.84bn a year, funded from free cash flow with no equity, no borrowing and no buyback leverage. | launch if selected; hold |

- Decision rationale: So, the decision, by the team rule.

1. START FROM THE PROPOSAL. UltraFan NB Solo: launch if selected; Joint Venture with P&W, UltraFan WB and Trent 1000 upgrade all at hold. Nothing is on the public record: no fps, no NGSA, no Re-engine, no P&W commitment. Only this round's conditional can catch an airframe launched in 2030, because a 2035 UltraFan is ready in 2042, too late for 2037 or 2040.

2. BINDING VETO CHECKS.
- CFO: McCabe co-signs the conditional against the hurdle. The selected columns are +28.24 to +44.54 on an $8B bill, and the expected gain is +3.37 on the weights she confirmed. She withholds her signature on the unconditional Solo, a Joint Venture commit, UltraFan WB and the Trent 1000 upgrade (alone or beside the conditional). None of them is ordered. She invokes no balance-sheet veto: no equity, no borrowing, no buyback leverage.
- Civil: Watson invokes no Civil veto. The zero margin of a 2030 trigger (ready 2037) against a 2030 NGSA or 7-year fps (EIS 2037) is a pass with a note. Against a 10-year fps or fps via Embraer (2040) there are three years in hand. The order triggers only if UltraFan is ready by EIS, so nothing is compressed.
- Support: his concurrence rests on the support plan before EIS, carried here and in other_moves. If triggered: partner MRO shops on the Singapore joint-venture model near launch operators, with no own narrowbody MRO scale; repair technology developed alongside the test programme; a spares pool and field-service team for EIS; partner-shop agreements and castings, forgings and gearbox supply contracted with high-performing suppliers by the 2035 round; an EIS-readiness review before EIS. The test pacing is the full seven years: if a phase slips, the engine slips.
- Statement: the public statement gives no EIS earlier than launch + 7, no 2037 next to any airframe, and no airframer date. No veto stands. overrides is empty.

3. GRID, EACH ITEM AGAINST THE SAME ROW WITHOUT IT (S1/S2/S3/S4/S5/S6/S7/S8). Weights, confirmed by McCabe: S1 0.40, S2 0.05, S3 0.15, S4 0.03, S5 0.02, S6 0.10, S7 0.10, S8 0.15 (selection 0.10 in total, our rule for 'nothing on the record').
- UltraFan NB Solo launch if selected ('UltraFan NB if selected' minus 'UltraFan NB: hold'): 0 / +32.60 / 0 / +28.24 / +44.54 / 0 / 0 / 0. Expected +3.37 (0.05x32.60 + 0.03x28.24 + 0.02x44.54). Board case (0.9 S1, 0.1 S2) +3.26; 0.1 on S4 alone +2.82. Worst column 0. Never below hold under any weights.
- Unconditional Solo instead, against the conditional: -8.00 in S1, S7 and S8, +3.57 in S3 and S6, expected -4.31. Against hold it is -0.94 expected, worst -8.00. Rejected: airframers first [RX-0058], the +$2B bar, McCabe's withheld signature and the Board veto.
- Joint Venture with P&W commit, held ('if selected + commit to JV' minus 'if selected'): 0 everywhere except -4.00 in S7; expected -0.40. P&W has not committed, so a first commit is options management [RX-0051].
- UltraFan WB launch, held ('if selected + UltraFan WB' minus 'if selected'): -4.00 in S1, S3, S6 and S7; -9.00 in S2, S4 and S5; -0.90 in S8; expected -4.04. No Re-engine is on the record, and the gap is above the $1B sub-cap.
- Trent 1000 upgrade launch, held ('if selected + T1000 upgrade' minus 'if selected'): -0.98 untriggered, -5.98 in S2 and S4, -5.97 in S5, -0.29 in S8; expected -1.38. That is beyond its own $2B bill. Alone ('hold + T1000 upgrade' minus hold) it is -0.88 expected and -0.98 worst, and it would displace the conditional.

4. EXPECTED SCENARIO AND PREMIUM. I expect S1, rivals hold. In S1 the conditional and hold tie at +0.00, the best S1 plan. In expectation the conditional is the best row on the grid, at +3.37. Next come 'if selected + JV' +2.97, 'if selected + upgrade' +1.99, hold 0, 'if selected + UltraFan WB' -0.67 and Solo -0.94. The doctrine-favoured order is also the PV-best order: premium $0B.

5. RED LINES, AGAINST THE FINAL ORDERS. None breached. Both colleagues flagged option D (the upgrade beside launch if selected) as a red-line breach. It is struck: not ordered. There is no unconditional launch, no first Joint Venture commit and no UltraFan WB, and no item can lose more than its own bill (worst column 0). There is nothing to cancel.

6. CASH AS AN INPUT [RX-0042].
- Untriggered: £0.
- Triggered: $8B over 7 years, about $1.14B a year, about £0.84bn a year at 1.36 $/£ and about £5.9bn in total. That is 22-23% of FY2026 FCF guidance of £3.6-3.8bn [R-0222].
- Funding: from free cash flow, as redeployment inside a broadly flat R&D envelope [RX-0214]. It falls after the £7-9bn 2026-28 returns programme. No equity, and no leverage above about 1.5x.
- No widebody development runs beside it. If triggered, the upgrade waits until the 2045 round.

7. TRENT 1000 UPGRADE. My record backs regaining 787 share ('Absolutely' [RX-0101], 'Why not?' [RX-0117]). On this board, though, it earns nothing before 2042 without a Re-engine, and it cannot sit beside the conditional. So it is deferred, not dropped. I revisit it in 2035 if no UltraFan has triggered and a Re-engine is on the record, or as a declared premium within $3B. McCabe would co-sign it then.

8. DOCTRINE, NOT MY RECORD. My narrowbody launch record is Medium: words, no launch decision. The conditional form, the +$2B bar and the column weights are company doctrine and inference (profile.md §9; dashboard_game.md §5). The cash figures are McCabe's; readiness, strain and support are Watson's. Frankly, until I am sure we will deliver, I'm not committing: the conditional commits only when an airframer has chosen us and we can be ready by its EIS.
- Expected scenario: S1: rivals hold. No airframer launches a new narrowbody and no Re-engine is announced this round; P&W does not commit. It is the single most likely column, with weight 0.40.. Best grid plan there: In S1, 'UltraFan NB: hold' and 'UltraFan NB if selected' tie at +0.00, the best value in the column. 'UltraFan NB if selected' (launch if selected, all else hold) is therefore best in S1 and also best in expectation, at +3.37 over hold, because it adds +32.60 / +28.24 / +44.54 in S2, S4 and S5 at no cost elsewhere.. Premium +0.00 $B: None. The order doctrine favours ('UltraFan NB if selected', everything else at hold) is the best expected grid plan, at +3.37 against +2.97 for the next best ('if selected + JV'). There is no premium for the Trent 1000 upgrade (deferred, up to $0.98B not taken), for UltraFan WB (held; its gap is above the $1B sub-cap) or for narrowbody objective items (the Board allows none).
- Predictions: Column weights, with McCabe's confirmation:
- S1 0.40: rivals hold.
- S2 0.05: Airbus launches NGSA and selects RR (code 7).
- S3 0.15: NGSA launched without RR (code 6).
- S4 0.03: Boeing fps 10-year selects RR (code 5).
- S5 0.02: both launch and select RR.
- S6 0.10: both launch, RR on neither.
- S7 0.10: P&W commits to the Joint Venture.
- S8 0.15: 787 Re-engine and A350 Re-engine.

Selection in total is 0.10, our rule for 'nothing on the record'.

My payoff on the orders submitted: +0.00 in S1, S3, S6, S7 and S8; +32.60 in S2; +28.24 in S4; +44.54 in S5. Expected +3.37.

Reading of rivals, from the public record only: the airframers move first and the engine makers follow. CFM and P&W hold the narrowbody duopoly and have no reason to invite us in unless an airframer pulls us in. I say nothing on any airframer's dates or rates.

Next round:
- If triggered: the programme is in development until 2037. Hold all widebody items and check that the selecting airframe keeps our code.
- If not triggered: the same conditional discipline in 2035, and test a Joint Venture commit if P&W's commitment is on the record.
- Expected ΔPV +3.37 $B; board after the round +18.26 $B

- Other moves: UltraFan demonstrator path continues (public); Trent 1000 and Trent fleet durability (time on wing) continues as funded (public); Narrowbody support plan, ready to activate on trigger (Watson's plan, adopted) (private); Plan for failure on a triggered programme (private); Pricing discipline (private); Open door to Pratt & Whitney on aligned terms (public)


## Round 2: decision year 2035

**2035: the Joint Venture forms; Airbus's cash line and its stricter condition keep Delay Tactics out**

- P&W and Rolls-Royce both committed to the Joint Venture on sealed orders. Both solo programmes folded into it. It inherits GTF2's schedule and is ready in 2036. Rolls-Royce writes off $1.71B.
- Airbus left poaching out. It was worth +$1.65B, but it took peak spend $0.07B over its Board's $5.2B cash line, and Faury kept the team's stricter condition (the move must change who enters service first), which on its own keeps poaching off.
- Boeing held the 787 Re-engine: on the 10-year ramp it would overlap fps by 5 years, against its 2-year limit. It logged the $0.65B forgone as the cost of that fixed rule.
- CFM/GE held everything and declined the Open Fan objective.

### Orders (as adjudicated)

| Player | Orders | Public statement |
|---|---|---|
| Boeing | Do Nothing | Five years ago we launched a new single-aisle airplane on a ten-year path, with entry into service in 2040. I'll tell you plainly where it stands. The program is on its plan. 2040 is still our date. It's a planning date with headroom in it, not a stretch target. We're in the heavy years now: detailed design, building the test airplanes, and certification ahead of us. That's where programs get into trouble, so that's where we're being most careful. If anything moves, you'll hear it from us early, with the plan to fix it, and we'll reset once. We're not changing anything else today. We're not starting a second development alongside this one. The 787 is a strong airplane, and it has room to grow from our Charleston capacity. We'll look at its next step once this airplane is in service. Doing less and doing it better is what got us here, and it's what will get this airplane across the line. A few things haven't changed. We'll certify before we build at rate. We won't take on fixed-price development. We'll keep our supply chain funded and healthy, and we'll keep our engineering teams together through entry into service and the ramp after it. Our engine partner stays CFM. We're funding this from our operations and our debt capacity, with no new equity. Paying down debt stays our priority, and we intend to stay solidly investment grade. There will be a few years when our customers see a newer airplane flying before ours. We've planned for that. We'll keep earning their business on the 737 MAX the old-fashioned way, by delivering what we promised, when we promised it. Others will make their plans on their own clock. Ours rests on our own readiness. This is the part of the climb where you keep your hands steady and do the work. The financials will follow our production performance, and we've got a lot of work in front of us. |
| Airbus | Do Nothing | Our core principles of safety, quality, integrity, compliance and security will remain at the heart of all we do. Our next-generation single-aisle aircraft is progressing on its technology clock. Entry into service remains planned for the second half of the 2030s, once the technologies, the production system and certification have matured, and not before. We are preparing its industrial ramp-up with the same discipline. We confirm three engine makers on the aircraft: CFM, Pratt & Whitney and Rolls-Royce. That gives our customers choice and secures supply. The A320neo family remains the backbone of our single-aisle business. Its production continues in a disciplined and controlled way, and the people and suppliers who deliver it remain protected. We continue to strengthen a more sovereign European supply chain. That means securing work packages and critical materials such as titanium and rare earths, and recruiting the engineers who will build the Airbus of the 2030s. On the widebody side, the A350 family, including the A350F, remains our focus. We fund this within our financial framework: disciplined investment in our future portfolio, and shareholder returns while preserving financial flexibility. On trade, we keep working with industry partners on both sides of the Atlantic for a zero-for-zero tariff regime in civil aerospace. |
| CFM/GE | Do Nothing | Safety, quality, delivery and cost, always in that order. That doesn't change this year either. So let me tell you what we know and what we don't. What we know: two new single-aisle airplanes are launched, and there is a CFM engine on both today. We don't take that for granted. We don't have a birthright on any order, so we earn it the way we always have, by delivering. That starts with the LEAP engines and the spares our customers need right now, on time. Spares don't crowd out new engines. The new ducted engine that CFM and our partners at Safran launched five years ago stays on its gate: not before 2036. We report against that gate on test, not on slides. If testing tells us it needs more time, the date moves. We don't trade durability for a date. It's the right engine for the airplanes in front of us. It isn't our next-generation engine. That is still RISE. We're all in on that technology, together with Safran, and we're keeping it whole. When that architecture enters service is really not for us to say. The airframers and their airline customers will decide that. Our work with Embraer is a CFM decision we took with Safran, and it rides on that same engine. Any airframer that wants a CFM engine can have one, and nobody gets locked out. On the widebody, the upgraded GEnx is in service for our 787 customers. What we don't know: how the next few years play out across the industry. So we plan on the low case, we keep the balance sheet and our capital returns whole, and we're not starting anything new this year. You won't see us pointing fingers; you'll see us solving problems. We get paid in dollars, not in percentages. It's that simple, ain't that hard. |
| Pratt & Whitney | Commit to Joint Venture with RR | Let me start with what is not done. Our share of narrowbody engine deliveries has held at 24% since 2026, and that is not where we want to be. GTF2 is in development, not yet proven. We launched it in 2030, when we were named on a new single-aisle, and it is due to be ready in 2036, six years from launch. Time on wing and full-life parts are the standards we have set for it, and we still have to prove them on test before entry into service. We will not compress the programme to make a date. If that date comes under risk, we will say so early, with the plan. Control what you can control. The GTF fleet and A320neo engine deliveries keep first call on our parts, our shops and our engineers, ahead of GTF2. We have learned our lesson from the prior iterations of the GTF. GTF2 is offered to any airframer where we can be ready by its entry into service, on standard terms. We will not buy a selection, and there are no launch-style discounts. We're not in the early entry phase, the launch phase. Today Pratt & Whitney makes a standing commitment to a Joint Venture with Rolls-Royce on a new single-aisle engine. That commitment is ours alone. A Joint Venture forms only if Rolls-Royce also commits. That is Rolls-Royce's decision, and we book nothing until it does. Any Joint Venture would share risk, investment and revenue on aligned, standard terms. Pratt & Whitney keeps its gear and core as its own module, with no technology licence. The Joint Venture would carry GTF2's schedule, not reset it. We will not speak for any airframer's plans, dates, rates or engine choices. When we've seen it and when we feel good about it, we invest. |
| Rolls-Royce | Commit to Joint Venture with P&W | So, let me be clear. UltraFan for the narrowbody is in development and on its seven-year clock: ready in 2037, seven years from launch, and not before. The full test programme runs and nothing is compressed. If a phase slips, the engine slips. Support goes in place through partner shops before entry into service. We do not want to underestimate the risks. Partnership has always been our preference, and today we put it on the record: Rolls-Royce commits to a Joint Venture with Pratt & Whitney on aligned terms. That means shared risk and investment, with each side's technology protected. We are actually bringing a technology. If Pratt & Whitney comes to the table, we form it. If not, we deliver on our own. Widebody is our franchise. There is no new widebody engine spend without a Re-engine, and durability work on the Trent fleet goes on. We won't do anything not profitable. I will say that categorically. |

### How each company decided (private during the game)

**Boeing** (Kelly Ortberg, Jay Malave, Stephanie Pope; Boeing Board)

| Step | What happened |
|---|---|
| Board guidance | Boeing Board: risk appetite: 4.5 of 5, unchanged. Carry the launched fps whole to its 2040 EIS. No second development and no cancel this round. No Board item may have an item value below -$1B in any of S1, S2 or S3 (all three are plausible), and no plan may score below -2.78 in the … |
| Frame | Kelly Ortberg: initial lean: **Orders I lean to:** Do Nothing. - fps: hold (we keep building the fps toward 2040); - fps engine code: 3 (CFM), unless Pope shows a second source adds supply security with durability shown before 2040; - 737 rate: hold; - 787 Re-engine: hold. Row: +0.66 / -0.80 / … |
| Test (CFO) | Jay Malave recommends: Do Nothing. Options failing a test: Buffer test (slip leg), plans C and D: any fps cancel (ask 1); Cash path of each bill, plan B: Re-engine beside the fps (ask 4). Vetoes: C: fps cancel + 787 hold (row 'cancel fps + 787: hold') (binding); D: fps cancel + 787 Re-engine (row 'cancel fps + 787 Re-engine') (binding); B: fps hold + 787 Re-engine (not binding) |
| Test (operating head) | Stephanie Pope recommends: Do Nothing. Options failing a test: Two-front strain (engineering capacity), row B: 787 Re-engine launched in 2035 (doctrine; CEO ask 3). Vetoes: 737 rate: increase (any combination) (not binding); fps: hold + 787 Re-engine: launch (row B) (not binding); fps: cancel, with or without 787 Re-engine: launch (rows C and D) (not binding); fps engine code: 4, 5, 6 or 7 (not binding) |
| Decide | Kelly Ortberg: Do Nothing |
| Veto check (CFO) | Jay Malave concurs |
| Veto check (operating head) | Stephanie Pope concurs |
| Board review | Boeing Board: not called; every order is the default (a hold is never a Board item) |

CFO tests:

| Test | Threshold | Result | Numbers |
|---|---|---|---|
| Buffer test (slip leg), plan A: Do Nothing (fps hold + 787 hold + rate hold, code 3) (ask 1) | In the risk-case column S3 (Airbus Delay Tactics), the plan must be no more than $2B below Do Nothing in the same column. Do Nothing scores -0.78 in S3, so the floor is -2.78. I run it as the baseline, not as a sensitivity. | pass | Row 'fps: hold + 787: hold', S3 = -0.78. That is 0.00 against Do Nothing and 2.00 above the -2.78 floor. Engine codes do not change our payoff, so every code variant is this same row. Full row: +0.66 / -0.80 / -0.78 (S1 / S2 / S3). Worst column: S2 at -0.80. Confirmed as the CEO stated. |
| Buffer test (slip leg), plan B: fps hold + 787 Re-engine (ask 1) | S3 must be at or above -2.78 (Do Nothing -0.78 less $2B). | pass | Row 'fps: hold + 787 Re-engine', S3 = -0.13. That is +0.65 against Do Nothing and 2.65 above the floor. Full row: +1.31 / -3.79 / -0.13. Item against A: +0.65 / -2.99 / +0.65. Its worst column is S2 at -3.79, which is $2.99B worse than Do Nothing. That is advice under Meet and beat, not a veto ground. B passes my slip leg, so my veto does not reach it. Confirmed. |
| Buffer test (slip leg), plans C and D: any fps cancel (ask 1) | S3 must be at or above -2.78. | fail | C, 'cancel fps + 787: hold': S3 -18.33. That is -17.55 against Do Nothing and 15.55 below the floor. D, 'cancel fps + 787 Re-engine': S3 -16.66. That is -15.88 against Do Nothing and 13.88 below the floor. Both are confirmed as the CEO stated. Under the team rule (teams.md §9) this is my binding veto on both rows. |
| Meet and beat (expected column S1, on Ortberg's provisional read; I have no Airbus evidence of my own) | Among plans within ε ($1B) on the expected column, prefer the one with the higher worst column (inference). | pass | In S1, A scores +0.66 and B +1.31: 0.65 apart, inside ε. Worst columns: A -0.80 (S2), B -3.79 (S2). The rule prefers A by 2.99. In S3 the two are also within ε (-0.78 against -0.13). In S2, A leads by 2.99, outside ε. A passes the rule and B does not. The cancel rows are 17.55 or more below A in every column. |
| Balance sheet first: debt penalty and strain (ask 3) | The grid's ΔPV already nets each plan's discounted penalty (α 0.30 × debt × (debt/EV)²) and the strain; never subtract either again. Between plans within ε on the expected column, prefer the one that adds the smaller penalty (inference). | pass | Do Nothing's -4.73 is confirmed. 0.30 × 105.32 × (105.32/130)² = 20.74, less 1.62 at today's $45B, gives $19.12B undiscounted in 2040. Discounted 14 years at 10.5% (÷4.047), that is 4.725. B's Re-engine is booked in 2040 beside the fps, taking debt to $110.32B: 23.83 - 20.74 = $3.10B undiscounted, against $0.60B for the Re-engine alone. That is about 0.765 PV, so the CEO's 'about $3.1B, about 0.77' is confirmed to rounding. On the board's convention, debt/EV at EIS goes from 81% to 85%. Strain is $3B × 5/5 = $3.0B undiscounted, already netted. Neither the penalty nor the strain is to be subtracted again; both sit inside B's +0.65 / -2.99 / +0.65. Within ε in S1, A adds the smaller penalty, so A is preferred. |
| Fundable when the 777X turns (Board gate) | An fps launch is fundable once the 777X is cash-positive (from 2029) (inference). | not applicable | No fps launch is on offer. The fps launched in 2030 passed this gate: 777X cash-positive from 2029, launch 2030, and nothing in the 2035 brief reverses it. The gate does not test a 787 Re-engine. B's funding is answered under its cash path below: not fundable from operations in 2035-38. No restatement is needed while B is struck. |
| Yearly funding report for the Finance Committee: fps phasing, debt path and rating outlook (ask 2) | Fund the fps from airplane operating profit and debt capacity with no new equity, within investment grade. Debt stays the first call on free cash. Any peak year above operating profit must be covered by debt capacity (inference). | pass | The brief gives no phasing: the board books $60.32B at EIS 2040, and its cancel rule writes off pro rata. My planning curve (inference: light concept years, peak at test-aircraft build, tapering through certification), in shares of $60.32B: 2030 5%, 2031 7%, 2032 8%, 2033 10%, 2034 11%, 2035 12%, 2036 14%, 2037 13%, 2038 11%, 2039 9%. Spend against operating profit, by year: 3.02 / 4.22 / 4.83 / 6.03 / 6.64 / 7.24 / 8.44 / 7.84 / 6.64 / 5.43, against 6.7 for 2030-36 and 6.6 / 6.5 / 6.4 for 2037-39. As a share of operating profit: 45 / 63 / 72 / 90 / 99 / 108 / 126 / 119 / 102 / 85%. Peak year: 2036, about $8.4B, $1.74B above operating profit. Gaps: 2030-34 +8.77 in total; 2035 -0.54, 2036 -1.74, 2037 -1.24, 2038 -0.14 (2035-38 together -3.66); 2039 +0.97. Over the decade, $66.4B of operating profit against $60.32B of spend leaves +6.08, the same as the even spread. Debt path (operating profit less fps spend, before tax, interest and other capex; the brief has no free-cash-flow line): the 2030-34 room goes to debt, 2035-38 redraw $3.66B of it, and the decade ends about $6.1B down. The cumulative position never goes below zero. On the board's convention the $60.32B is booked as debt in 2040, taking debt/EV from 35% to 81%; the -4.73 prices that. From 2040 operating profit steps to $14.3B, rising to $16.9B, so deleveraging resumes: heavy use, then neutral, then positive. Even spread for comparison: 6.03 a year, 90-94% of operating profit, cushion 0.67 falling to 0.37. Rating outlook: I support investment grade on the fps alone, with no new equity, barring a slip past 2040, a sustained fall in 737 or 787 deliveries or margins below the brief's 6.6 / 6.5 / 6.4, or a second development in these years. |
| Cash path of each bill, plan A (ask 2) | When each bill lands, and whether two land together [BX-0547]. | pass | One bill: fps $60.32B at EIS 2040 (PV 14.91). No other bill lands in 2035-40. Real spend runs heavy in 2035-37, near neutral in 2038 and positive in 2039 (planning curve above). |
| Cash path of each bill, plan B: Re-engine beside the fps (ask 4) | No two bills landing together, and no second programme's spend in the fps peak years funded above operating profit (inference). | fail | Advice, not a veto. Two bills land together in 2040: the fps's $60.32B and the Re-engine's $5B (PV 1.24), $65.32B in all. Even spread: $7.03B a year over 2035-39 against 6.7 / 6.7 / 6.6 / 6.5 / 6.4, i.e. 105-110%, with $2.26B borrowed above operating profit (the CEO's 'about $2.25B' is confirmed). On my planning curve: 8.24 / 9.44 / 8.84 / 7.64 / 6.43, i.e. 123 / 141 / 134 / 117 / 100%. The peak is 2036, $2.74B above operating profit, and $7.69B is borrowed above operating profit over 2035-39, against $2.69B for the fps alone. It uses up about $5B of the decade's deleveraging room (net paydown about $1.1B instead of about $6.1B). It is not fundable from operations. It puts the rating at risk: I do not support investment grade with a second bill running in the fps's peak years. |
| Full cost of a slip or cancel (ask 5) | Cancel write-off = bill × years elapsed ÷ development years, and the debt penalty stays; cost it once, in full. H5: never cancel a launched fps. Cost a slip in full: concessions, rework, learning curve, carrying cost. | pass | A carries no cancel and no slip. Cancel, confirmed: 60.32 × 5/10 = $30.16B written off in 2035 (PV 12.28). The penalty stays: debt goes to $75.16B, adding $5.92B undiscounted (PV 2.41). Keeping beats cancelling by 18.99 / 18.99 / 17.55 (A against C). With the Re-engine in both rows it is 17.97 / 17.97 / 16.53 (B against D). Slip: the brief has none and the board prices none. For scale only (my arithmetic): each year of slip defers the step to $14.3B of operating profit ($7.6B above status quo in 2040, about $1.9B PV). It also adds a year of NGSA lead, costing 1 pp of long-run share for good (rules.md), before concessions, rework, learning curve and carrying cost. |
| The estimate itself: independent quarterly check (ask 6) | Reset once and early if any evidence shows the bill or the date is wrong; never serially (inference from the 777X reset). | pass | No evidence that the estimate is wrong. The brief lists the fps as 'in development; EIS 2040' with the same $60.32B booked at 2040 as at launch, and no slip, certification trouble or live quality, FAA or supply-chain problem. CFM ducted is ready in 2036, four years before EIS, which leaves time to mature the engine. Watch items, none a reset trigger: peak spend in 2035-37 above operating profit; operating profit falling to 6.4 by 2039 as NGSA enters; and the supply base tightening from ordinary public moves (long-term agreements on engines, wing packages, titanium and rare earths; European engineering hiring). That last is the S3 condition, which costs 1.44 against S1 and is already in the baseline. I attribute nothing to anyone. |
| Rate step | Only after stability, jointly agreed with the FAA [BX-0554]. | not applicable | 737 rate is offered only at hold this round (2030 was the only round it was offered). |
| Premium in dollars (H8) (ask 7) | Report every doctrine or objective premium in $B; the total stays within $2B. Hard-rule costs are logged separately. Name the condition that would break the number. | pass | This round's premium is 0: A is the best plan that respects H4, H5 and the Board's tests in every column. No objective premium: both objectives are recorded as not met (worst year 38.0%), and no order offered changes that. Carried forward: 0.46 (Round 1). Cumulative: 0.46, within the $2B cap. Hard-rule costs, logged apart: H4 (Re-engine forgone) 0.65 in S1 and S3, nil in S2, where holding saves 2.99. H5 forgoes nothing, since cancel is worse by 17.55 or more. Confirmed as the CEO stated. Condition: this holds barring an fps slip past 2040, which the board does not price. |

Operating head tests:

| Test | Threshold | Result | Numbers |
|---|---|---|---|
| KPI gate for the 737 Rate Increase: my veto (doctrine; CEO ask 1) | No Rate Increase while a quality, FAA or supply-chain problem is live. Steps of 5, at least 6 months apart, KPI-gated. Supplier readiness is a hard gate. | pass | The 2035 brief reports no live quality, FAA, certification or supply-chain problem. Deliveries: 800 NB (40.0%) and 100 WB (58.8%) a year, flat 2026-34 and on the board through 2036. 737 rate is offered only at hold, so the veto has nothing to bind; I checked it and do not invoke it. For the Aerospace Safety Committee: the board carries deliveries only, no KPI data (stable months at rate [BX-1271], quality escapes, FAA findings). Those results must come directly from the Chief Engineer and the Chief Aerospace Safety Officer; I do not substitute delivery counts for them. Supplier caveat: beyond 47 a month the supply base binds [B-2340], and the fps ramp from 2040 loads the same base. |
| On-time, predictable delivery: fps to EIS 2040 with headroom (record [BX-1331], applied to airplanes by inference; CEO ask 2) | The plan's EIS can be delivered as stated: no slip or certification problem in the brief; risk-case column S3 (Delay Tactics) above the -2.78 floor; certify-before-rate written into the plan. State how much value rides on an undisrupted EIS. | pass | Brief: fps 'in development; EIS 2040', no slip, no certification trouble. Certify-before-rate (no production-certification concurrency) is in the fps programme plan and on the public record as a Round-1 other move [BX-1263, B-2228]. Risk case: Do Nothing is +0.66 in S1 and -0.78 in S3. The $1.44B swing is 218% of the S1 value, so the plan's whole positive value rides on a clean ramp around EIS. Sensitivity: S3 is 5 pp for 5 years from EIS (2040-44), i.e. 100 aircraft a year at about $14.1M each (fps $55M at 25.64%): $1.41B a year nominal, $1.44B PV. Each pp-year around EIS costs about $0.28B nominal ($0.06B PV). S3 floor -2.78: A -0.78 passes by 2.00; B -0.13 passes by 2.65; C -18.33 fails by 15.55; D -16.66 fails by 13.88. Headroom: the board carries no design-maturity, test or certification data and no Boeing slip. I can confirm the date on the board, not the margin inside it; the Chief Engineer must show that to Aerospace Safety. Early warning: 2035 is the last ExCo round before EIS (the next is 2045), so my watch items go on the record now: (1) CFM ducted ready 2036, four years before EIS; (2) supplier and staff readiness for 2040-44; (3) the funding cushion falling to $0.37B in 2039. |
| Two-front strain (engineering capacity), row B: 787 Re-engine launched in 2035 (doctrine; CEO ask 3) | A Solo fps and a 787 Re-engine overlap at most 2 years (H4). Strain = $3B x overlap / 5. The fps counts from EIS-7 to EIS, the Re-engine from EIS-5 to EIS. | fail | The fps 10-year counts 2033-40. A Re-engine launched in 2035 enters service in 2040 and counts 2035-40. Overlap is 5 years and strain $3.0B undiscounted, already netted in the grid and not to be subtracted again. It fails H4 by 3 years. I confirm H4 and do not rebut it: we do not have the engineering capacity for a second development in the fps's heaviest design, test and certification years. One development at a time [B-0341, B-1526, B-1393]. Row A (recommended): 0 years, $0. Earliest legal window: a 2045 launch (EIS 2050, counting 2045-50) has 0 years of overlap, and only if the A350 has not re-engined (H3, second half). |
| Ramp choice, 7- or 10-year (inference) | Which ramp can operations deliver predictably with the workforce and suppliers in the brief? State the strain and EIS of each. | not applicable | No ramp order is offered. The fps is launched on the 10-year ramp: EIS 2040, $60.32B at EIS, strain 0 standing alone. A re-baseline is a plan and a statement, not an order. Round 1 chose the 10-year ramp because its nominal date matched where doctrine's central three-year clean-sheet slip would put a 7-year ramp. |
| Supplier and talent readiness, the hedge against S3 (doctrine; CEO ask 4) | No live supply-chain or workforce problem. Fragile suppliers funded and kept hot above our rate. Engineering staff held through the risk window. Exposure assessed with no attribution. | pass | The brief reports no live supply-chain problem and names no fragile supplier, so I name categories, not companies. The S3 window is 2040-44 (5 years from EIS), so the hedge runs through 2044, not only 2040. Keep hot and funded above our rate [B-2330, BX-1243]: the fps's long-lead airframe structures, titanium forgings, rare-earth-dependent parts and the CFM engine line. Embed staff wherever a supplier falls behind [B-2097]. Talent: hold the fps engineering and certification teams through 2044 and assign none to a second development. Answer scarcity by transfer, hiring and retention, as before [B-0729, B-1941, B-1376]; engineering talent is a competition with every sector [B-1835]. Exposure: the public long-term agreements on engines, wing packages, titanium and rare earths, and the European engineering hiring, are ordinary market moves. Our exposure is to shared, finite capacity in titanium forgings, rare earths and engines during the 2040-44 ramp, when NGSA (EIS 2037) is already in service. Mitigation: long-term agreements sized to our own 2040 plan, on our own schedule. The cost of failure is $1.41B a year nominal (S3). |
| Engine code and supply security (doctrine; not a payoff item; CEO ask 5) | Request only makers with a new NB engine ready by EIS 2040, matured, with durability shown before EIS [BX-1298]. Weigh each maker's capacity across both airframers and the aftermarket [BX-1264, BX-1325]. | pass | Ready dates: CFM ducted 2036, GTF2 2036, UltraFan NB 2037, all before 2040. Code 4: no Joint Venture has formed, and one formed in 2035 is ready in 2041, after EIS, so the request would fall back to CFM. This is our last code decision: the code can change only until EIS, and the next round is 2045. Code 3: CFM is always available, as a LEAP derivative if ducted falls short. That puts a floor on the board under the sole-source pacing risk seen on the GE9X [B-1597]. Doctrine is sole source by default, chosen for maturity [B-0600, B-0613]. Second source (5, 6, 7): in 2035 none of the new engines is in service. By 2040, GTF2 and UltraFan NB would have about 3 years on NGSA, as would CFM ducted. Durability problems on the newer engines appeared in service [BX-1298, B-2304], so 3 years falls short of durability shown. A second engine also adds a second propulsion integration and certification in 2035-40, inside the fps's heaviest years (inference: the same capacity ground as H4). The supply-security risk is real: CFM supports NGSA, the A320neo, the 737 aftermarket and the fps [BX-1264, BX-1325]. Mitigation: track engine allocation by airframer, which is easy to measure [B-1923], and contract delivery commitments. Code 3 passes. I cannot show that a second source adds security on matured, durable engines. |
| Invest or partner on IP (record [BX-1330, BX-1333], applied to airplanes by inference) | Invest Solo where Boeing owns the IP. Partner where it can't, and only where the partner adds what Boeing lacks. | pass | The fps airframe is Solo, and its IP is Boeing's. No partner order is offered this round. The engine is where Boeing owns no IP, so we partner with an engine maker either way; the test there is what a second partner adds (engine test above). A cancel would write off $30.16B of IP investment in 2035 (60.32 x 5/10), and the penalty would stay. |
| Capacity follows demand: Rate Increase and Charleston (inference from [BX-1329]; CEO ask 6) | Back added capacity only where the grid shows visible demand for it in the expected column. | not applicable | 737 rate is offered only at hold. Widebody: without a Re-engine, the board holds Boeing at 100 WB a year (58.8% of 170) from 2026 to 2060. That is about 8.3 a month, below the 10 a month that the Charleston expansion is built to exceed [B-2336, BX-1317]. Charleston can carry the 787 to the 2045 window without a Re-engine. The board shows no demand above 100 a year for extra capacity to meet, so growth beyond that is a commercial move and unpriced. |
| Working capital and cash conversion (record [BX-1327, BX-1332, BX-1326], applied to airplanes by inference) | For each option: when the bill lands and what it does to cash, as a number with a sensitivity per unit of change. | pass | A: the fps's $60.32B is booked at EIS 2040, an even spread of $6.03B a year (inference; Malave owns the real phasing). Over 2035-39 that is $30.16B against $32.9B of operating profit (6.7, 6.7, 6.6, 6.5, 6.4). Cover is 90/90/91/93/94%, and the cushion is $0.67/0.67/0.57/0.47/0.37B a year, $2.74B in all. That absorbs a 9.1% overrun on the remaining spend before we borrow. B: +$5B at 2040, about $1.0B a year. Spend rises to $7.03B a year, cover to 105-110%, and $2.26B is borrowed above operating profit over 2035-39. Undiscounted debt penalty (alpha 0.30, debt $45B, EV $130B): the fps alone carries $19.1B. The Re-engine beside it adds $3.10B ($0.62B per $1B), against $0.60B alone ($0.12B per $1B), so the marginal cost is five times higher. Penalty and strain are already netted in the grid. C: $30.16B written off in 2035, and the penalty stays; item -18.99 / -18.99 / -17.55. D: item -17.32 / -20.96 / -15.88. A converts cash best. Malave's veto decides the slip leg. |
| Customers through the gap (record [BX-1331], applied by inference; CEO ask 7) | No order offered this round changes the 2037-40 share path. Hold the base by delivering the 737 on time and predictably, and quantify the gap. | not applicable | Brief: NB share 39/38/37% in 2037-39 and 38% in 2040, back to 40% in 2042 and 47% by 2049. The dip costs $0.1/0.2/0.3B of operating profit in 2037-39, $0.6B nominal. Each 737 point held is worth about $0.08B a year (20 aircraft x $48M x 8%). My part: deliver the 737 on the dates promised, and quote fps slots only against the 2040 planning date, never an earlier internal date [BX-1273, B-2315]. Pricing, campaign by campaign, is the CEO's call. |

- Decision rationale: ExCo deliberation, Round 2 (2035), under the team rule of teams.md §9. I propose and decide. Malave's buffer test and the KPI doctrine can each block me.

1. Vetoes and flags in force.
- Malave holds a binding veto on both cancel rows, and I accept it.
  - C (cancel + 787 hold) scores -18.33 in S3, 15.55 below the -2.78 floor.
  - D (cancel + Re-engine) scores -16.66, 13.88 below the floor.
  - Neither row is in the orders.
- B (fps hold + Re-engine) passes his slip leg at S3 -0.13, 2.65 above the floor. His hold on B is advice, not a veto.
- Pope checked her KPI veto and did not invoke it. The rate is offered only at hold, and the brief reports no live problem.
- Her flags are both correct, so I confirm them and strike the rows:
  - H4 on B: 5 years of overlap against a 2-year limit.
  - H5 on C and D.
- No flag is mistaken and nothing needs a rebuttal, so overrides is empty.

2. My four questions.
- Are the KPIs stable? The brief reports no live quality, FAA, certification or supply-chain problem, and deliveries were flat at 800 narrowbodies and 100 widebodies over 2026-34. Pope rightly says the board carries deliveries, not KPI data. So the KPI record comes directly from the Chief Engineer and the Chief Aerospace Safety Officer to the Aerospace Safety Committee [BX-1271, BX-1259].
- Are market, technology and balance sheet ready for a second development? No.
  - Market: the Re-engine's item is +0.65 in S1, inside ε, and -2.99 in S2.
  - Balance sheet: Malave finds the Re-engine not fundable from operations. On his curve it adds $7.69B of borrowing above operating profit over 2035-39 ($2.26B on an even spread), and he would not support investment grade with a second bill in the fps peak years [BX-1305].
  - The fps is the development in hand. We finish it first [B-2326].
- What if certification takes longer? I read S3 first. Do Nothing scores -0.78 there, 2.00 above the floor. Malave's quarterly check finds no evidence that the $60.32B or the 2040 date is wrong [BX-1319], so there is no reset to make, and I will not make one for the sake of it. One reset, if it comes, made early [BX-1318].
- Is this doing less, better? A and B are within ε in S1 (+0.66 against +1.31) and in S3 (-0.78 against -0.13). A leads by 2.99 in S2. Doing less, better [BX-1239] and the higher-confidence plan [BX-0548] both give it to A. Malave's worst-column rule picks A by 2.99.

3. Rulings on the hard rules.
- H1 is not at issue; the fps is launched.
- H2 cannot bind.
- H3:
  - First half: it lapsed after 2030. Its re-founded ground, one development at a time [B-0341, B-1526, B-1393], now works through H4.
  - Second half: not triggered, since the public record shows no A350 Re-engine. If Airbus launches one this round, it bars ours from 2045, and I accept that.
- H4 binds. The fps counts 2033-40 and a 2035 Re-engine 2035-40: 5 years against 2. 787 Re-engine stays at hold.
  - Hard-rule cost: 0.65 in S1 and S3, nil in S2, where holding saves 2.99. Logged apart from any premium.
  - Earliest window: the 2045 round (EIS 2050, 0 years of overlap).
- H5 binds. The fps stays whole.
  - Keeping it beats cancelling by 18.99 / 18.99 / 17.55, so H5 forgoes nothing.
  - A cancel would write off $30.16B in 2035 with the penalty staying.
- H6 is not in play.
- H7 has no trigger; my FAA-process gate passes [BX-1309].
- H8: premium 0 this round. Cumulative 0.46 (Round 1), within the $2B cap.

4. Debt and funding, Malave's figures, adopted as the Finance Committee baseline.
- Do Nothing carries -4.73 of debt penalty already netted ($19.1B undiscounted for scale). It is never subtracted again.
- On his planning curve:
  - spend peaks in 2036 at about $8.44B, $1.74B above operating profit;
  - 2035-38 draw $3.66B against the $8.77B of room built in 2030-34;
  - 2039 turns positive, and the decade ends about $6.1B down on debt.
- That is fundable within investment grade with no new equity, on the fps alone.
- His curve is an inference, because the brief gives no phasing. I have asked him to replace it with programme actuals each quarter. His triggers go to the Board before anything is committed.

5. Engine code: 3 (CFM). This is the last code decision before EIS.
- CFM ducted is ready in 2036, with the LEAP derivative as a floor.
- Code 3 against NGSA's code 7 is no forbidden pair.
- I considered a second source (5, 6 or 7): GTF2 is ready in 2036 and UltraFan NB in 2037, so all qualify by date. I rejected it:
  - none of these engines is in service today, and about three years on NGSA by 2040 is short of durability shown [BX-1298];
  - it would add a second propulsion integration and certification inside the fps's heaviest years, the same capacity ground as H4;
  - Pope cannot show it adds supply security on matured, durable engines.
- CFM's load across both airframers is real [BX-1264, BX-1325]. We manage it with allocation tracking and contracted delivery commitments.
- My confidence on engine choice is Low, so doctrine plus Pope's test drove this.

6. Expected scenario: S1, provisional; my confidence on Airbus is Low. My Round-1 read of an A350 Re-engine alongside NGSA did not happen. A move is real only when it is launched. The orders are the same in every column, so the read does not drive the decision.

7. Doctrine and record. Several of these calls rest on company doctrine, not my own record: H4, H5, the code-3 default and the 2044 hedge window. Others are my record: one development at a time, debt first, never walk away from a core programme, reset once, no fixed-price development and certify before rate.

8. The Board. All four orders are holds, which matches its would-approve list, so no item goes to review. Its seven recommendations are answered in board_response.

9. What would change this:
- a finding that the estimate or date is wrong: we reset once, now, costed in full and brought to the Board;
- a live quality, FAA or supply-chain problem;
- an A350 Re-engine, which closes our 2045 Re-engine window under H3.
- Expected scenario: S1 (rivals hold), provisional, with Low confidence on Airbus. My Round-1 read of an A350 Re-engine alongside NGSA did not happen: Airbus held the widebody, named the A350 family its focus, and is mid-NGSA with EIS 2037. S2 (A350 Re-engine) stays plausible, and S3 (Delay Tactics, covert) is the risk case I read first. The orders are identical in every column, so the read does not drive the decision.. Best grid plan there: The grid's top row in S1 is fps: hold + 787 Re-engine at +1.31. It is only 0.65 above Do Nothing, inside ε. It breaches H4 (5 years of overlap against 2), loses 2.99 in S2 and is not fundable from operations. Struck.

The best plan that respects H4, H5 and every ExCo and Board test, and the one chosen, is fps: hold + 787: hold (Do Nothing), with code 3 and the rate at hold: +0.66 / -0.80 / -0.78 (S1 / S2 / S3).. Premium +0.00 $B: No doctrine or objective premium this round. Do Nothing is the best plan that respects the hard rules and the tests.

- The 0.65 forgone in S1 and S3 by not launching the 787 Re-engine is an H4 hard-rule cost (nil in S2, where holding saves 2.99). It is logged separately and is not a premium. It is also inside ε, so doing less, better would choose Do Nothing anyway.
- H5 forgoes nothing: cancelling is worse by 17.55 or more in every column.
- No objective premium: no order on offer changes the narrowbody share path.
- Running log: Round-1 premium 0.46 (10-year over 7-year, S2 and S4), carried forward. Cumulative 0.46, within the $2B cap.
- Hard-rule cost log: Round 1, the Re-engine forgone under H3 and H4. Round 2, H4 at 0.65 in S1 and S3.
- Predictions: Airbus (Low confidence):
- Most likely holds the A350 this round (S1), keeping the A350 family as its focus.
- An A350 Re-engine (S2) stays plausible, given Rolls-Royce's appetite for a widebody Re-engine. If it comes, it closes our 2045 Re-engine window under H3.
- NGSA stays on EIS 2037. Airbus likely keeps code 7, or narrows it to CFM plus one second source.
- Delay Tactics are covert and unobservable until the fps enters service; S3 is priced as the risk case.

Engine makers:
- CFM ducted is ready in 2036, and GTF2 and UltraFan NB on their 2036 and 2037 dates.
- An RR and P&W Joint Venture is unlikely this round, since both already run Solo programmes. If one forms in 2035, it is ready in 2041, after our EIS.

Boeing:
- The Board is not called to review, since all four orders are holds.
- Next round (2045): the fps in service from 2040, narrowbody share 38% in 2040 rising to 43% in 2045.
- The 787 Re-engine is reconsidered in 2045 only if the A350 has not re-engined.
- No fps reset expected, but the 2035-37 peak spend and the 2040-44 supply ramp are the watch items.
- Expected ΔPV is +0.66 in S1; -0.80 and -0.78 in S2 and S3.
- Expected ΔPV +0.66 $B; board after the round +0.66 $B

- Other moves: Hold 2040 as a planning date with headroom; reset once and early (public); Certify before building at rate, written into the fps programme plan; Chief Engineer reports direct to the Aerospace Safety Committee (public); No fixed-price development; risk-sharing supplier terms continue (public); Yearly funding report to the Finance Committee: fund from operations and debt capacity, no new equity (private); Keep fragile suppliers funded and hot, and engineering teams in place, through 2044 (private); Engine request stays code 3 (CFM); allocation tracked (private); Independent estimate check continues; early-warning rule (private); 787 Re-engine parked to the 2045 window; widebody growth from Charleston (private); Hold the 737 MAX base through the gap, campaign by campaign (private)

**Airbus** (Guillaume Faury, Thomas Toepfer, Lars Wagner; Airbus Board)

| Step | What happened |
|---|---|
| Board guidance | Airbus Board: risk appetite: Averse (4 of 5), with a long horizon (4 of 5). The Board wants a quiet round. NGSA continues on its 2037 clock. Nothing new is launched, and no covert money is spent, while NGSA spends $5.07B a year against the $5.2B line. The Board expects no Board item this … |
| Frame | Guillaume Faury: initial lean: Plan A, the company default for this round, as the Board guides: - NGSA: hold. NGSA continues to entry into service in 2037; in public, "the second half of the 2030s". - NGSA engine code: 7, unchanged, pending Wagner's supply view. - A350 Re-engine: hold. - … |
| Test (CFO) | Thomas Toepfer recommends: Do Nothing. Options failing a test: Peak annual cash and Delay Tactics cash: plan B (A + one poaching move); Peak annual cash: plan C (A + both moves); Peak annual cash: plan D (A + A350 Re-engine launched 2035); Peak annual cash: plan E (A + A350 Re-engine + one poaching move); Robustness, added move: plan C (both moves); …. Vetoes: B. Plan A + one poaching move (2035) (binding); D. Plan A + A350 Re-engine launched 2035 (binding); E. Plan A + A350 Re-engine + one poaching move (binding); C. Plan A + both Delay Tactics moves (binding); Bottleneck in place of poaching, on any row (binding); F. Cancel NGSA, on any row (binding) |
| Test (operating head) | Lars Wagner recommends: Do Nothing. Options failing a test: Integrity and rule 4: bottleneck and both (plan C, the A350 Re-engine + both row, and bottleneck in place of poaching; five pillars and red line, no override); Cancel NGSA, plan F (rule 7, red line). Vetoes: C. Plan A + both Delay Tactics moves; also the A350 Re-engine + both row (binding); Bottleneck in place of poaching, on any row (binding); F. Cancel NGSA, every row (binding) |
| Decide | Guillaume Faury: Do Nothing |
| Veto check (CFO) | Thomas Toepfer concurs |
| Veto check (operating head) | Lars Wagner concurs |
| Board review | Airbus Board: not called; every order is the default (a hold is never a Board item) |

CFO tests:

| Test | Threshold | Result | Numbers |
|---|---|---|---|
| Peak annual cash: plan A (NGSA hold, code 7, A350 hold, Delay Tactics none) | Peak annual programme cash spend at or below about one year's free cash flow: €4,753m x $1.10 = $5.2B (unrounded $5.23B). Each bill spread evenly over its development years (cash view) | pass | Bill $35.52B / 7 = $5.074B a year, 2030-36. Schedule: 2035 $5.07B, 2036 $5.07B, 2037 $0 (the board books the $35.5B at the 2037 entry into service, brief section 1), 2038-39 $0. NGSA cash left is $10.15B. A350 $0, strain $0, Delay Tactics $0. Peak $5.07B, which is 97% of the line. Headroom is $0.13B (2.5%) against $5.2B, or $0.15B against $5.23B. Confirmed as framed. |
| Peak annual cash and Delay Tactics cash: plan B (A + one poaching move) | Each $1B move is spread evenly from the order year to fps entry into service and added to that year's spend. Total at or below about $5.2B | fail | fps entry into service is 2040, so the move costs $1B / 5 = $0.20B a year over 2035-39. Schedule: 2035 $5.27B, 2036 $5.27B, 2037-39 $0.20B. Peak $5.274B: $0.07B over $5.2B and $0.05B over $5.23B (about 1%). It also trips round-1 break point (d), any Delay Tactics spend, which by itself comes back to the Board as an item. I do not back-load the move to clear the line. My method spreads it evenly from the order year, and the Board bars re-phasing to make room for another item. Soft veto, binding. |
| Peak annual cash: plan C (A + both moves) | At or below about $5.2B | fail | $2B / 5 = $0.40B a year over 2035-39. 2035-36 run at $5.47B, $0.27B over the line. 2037-39 run at $0.40B. |
| Peak annual cash: plan D (A + A350 Re-engine launched 2035) | At or below about $5.2B | fail | A350 Re-engine $5B / 5 = $1.00B a year over 2035-39 (entry into service 2040). Strain is $3B x 2/5 = $1.2B, or $0.60B a year in the overlap years 2035-36. Schedule: 2035 $6.67B, 2036 $6.67B, 2037-39 $1.00B. That is $1.47B over $5.2B ($1.45B over $5.23B). Without strain it would still be $6.07B. Confirmed as framed. Soft veto, binding. |
| Peak annual cash: plan E (A + A350 Re-engine + one poaching move) | At or below about $5.2B | fail | $5.07B + $1.00B + $0.60B + $0.20B = $6.87B in 2035-36, $1.67B over the line. 2037-39 run at $1.20B. Confirmed as framed. Soft veto, binding. |
| Round-1 cash gate: break points in 2030-34 (ask 2) | Any of: (a) a total overrun above $0.88B; (b) any year more than 2.5% above the average; (c) the euro below about $1.07; (d) any Delay Tactics spend | pass | (a) No. The brief still books the NGSA bill at $35.5B at the 2037 entry into service, and entry into service is unchanged. (b) Not reported. The board gives no annual phasing, so my even $5.07B a year stands as an assumption, and no item reports otherwise. (c) Not reported. The brief carries no exchange rate, so the $1.10 game assumption stands. (d) No. The round-1 sealed orders, as returned and as adjudicated, read Delay Tactics: none. No break point is triggered on the record. Two of the four, phasing and the euro, cannot be observed from the brief, and I say so rather than certify them. |
| Net cash, rating and shareholder returns: plan A, base case beside stress case (asks 3, 4, 5) | The 30-50% payout fundable; no equity raise; no dividend cut outside a crisis; state support outside the case. Flag any year in which €3.20 depends on free cash flow above about $5.9B. Rating proxy: net cash at or above zero, and peak spend at or below one year's free cash flow | pass | Inputs. - Net cash opens 2030 at $13.4B (€12.2bn), as in round 1; the 2026-29 surplus of about $2.4B is left out as a cushion. - €3.20 a share is about $2.78B: €2,372m was paid at €3.00, which implies about 790m shares. - FY2025 net income of €5,221m is about $5.74B, so a 30% payout is $1.72B and a 50% payout $2.87B. - Stress case: free cash flow flat at $5.23B, the full NGSA bill treated as incremental, and no advances, risk-sharing or state money. - Base case: the brief's status-quo operating profit of $8.8B a year, converted at FY2025's ratio of free cash flow to EBIT Adjusted (4,753 / 7,128 = 0.67), gives $5.87B, with the same exclusions. The NGSA profit uplift in 2037 ($18.3B against $8.8B on the board) is not counted. The public 2029 outlook (EBIT Adjusted €12-13bn, cash conversion about 1) is upside and not in the case. Net-cash path, end-2034 / 2035 / 2036 / 2037, in $B: - base at €3.20: +3.5 / +1.5 / -0.5 / +2.6; - base at 30%: +8.8 / +7.8 / +6.9 / +11.0; - stress at €3.20: +0.3 / -2.4 / -5.0 / -2.6; - stress at 30%: +5.6 / +4.0 / +2.4 / +5.9. Break-even payout over 2030-36 is 47% in the base case and 36% in the stress case. Flag: 2035 and 2036. Holding €3.20 with net cash at or above zero needs average free cash flow of $5.94B over 2030-36. On the paths to 2034 that becomes $6.1B (base) to $7.7B (stress) in each of 2035 and 2036. Returns: - The 30-50% policy is fundable at its lower end in both cases. - No equity raise is needed in either case. The Board's own issuance authority is 0.18-0.3% of capital. - No dividend cut is forced outside a crisis, provided the Board accepts a temporary net debt of up to $5.0B in the stress case. That reverses once NGSA cash stops in 2037. The choice is the Board's each year. Rating: S&P A+ and Moody's A1. The agencies' thresholds are not in my record. My proxy: - net cash at or above zero; - peak spend against free cash flow: 97% in plan A; - gross cash €27.2bn (about $29.9B) against the $10.15B of NGSA cash left. State support and refundable advances stay outside the case. Plan B would take a further $0.4B off net cash by end-2036 and $1.0B by 2039. |
| Robustness: plan A | Grid ΔPV against Do Nothing positive in the worst plausible Boeing column (S2: Boeing launches a 787 Re-engine) | pass | +23.37 (S1) / +22.88 (S2). The worst column is S2, at +22.88. It carries no Board item: ngsa hold is the default, and code 7 is unchanged. |
| Robustness, added move: plan B (one poaching move) | The added move is at least $1B over the same plan without it in every plausible column, and never negative | pass | +25.02 / +24.54 against A's +23.37 / +22.88, so the move adds +1.65 (S1) / +1.66 (S2). It clears $1B in both columns. Its value does not decide it; its cash does. |
| Robustness, added move: plan C (both moves) | The second move is at least $1B over one move | fail | +24.55 / +24.07. Against one move that is -0.47 / -0.47; against A it is +1.18 / +1.19. One move and two give the same 5 pp, so the second $1B buys nothing. |
| Robustness, added move: plan D (A350 Re-engine in 2035) | The added move is at least $1B over the same plan without it; it fails if it is negative in a plausible column and short of $1B there | fail | +24.91 / +21.67. The A350 Re-engine adds +1.54 in S1 and -1.21 in S2. It is negative in the worst plausible column, so it fails. The plan itself stays positive (+21.67), and -1.21 is inside the Board's $2B loss line, so my added-move test is what fails. Confirmed as framed. |

The A350 part, measured against B, is +1.54 / -1.22, negative in S2. The Delay Tactics part, measured against D, is +1.65 / +1.65. |

Write-off = bill x years elapsed / development years | fail | Hold row: -16.28 / -16.77, which is -39.65 against continuing in both columns. On the A350 Re-engine rows it is -39.09 / -39.08. The plan is negative in both columns. Write-off: $35.52B x 5/7 = $25.37B, booked in 2035. As a programme charge it would sit outside EBIT Adjusted, and I would show it in reported EBIT; I would not adjust it away. Cancelling would save the remaining $10.15B of cash, but there is no failed case behind it, so it is a red line. The A350 cancel is not applicable: nothing has been launched. |
| Overlap (rule 6): plans D and E | NGSA / A350 Re-engine development overlap of 2 years or less unless the grid shows at least $1B more, and at most one new launch per round | pass | NGSA development runs 2030-37 and the A350 Re-engine 2035-40, an overlap of 2 years with $1.2B of strain. That is at the limit, not above it. It is one new launch this round. So rule 6 is met and this is not a red line. Plans D and E fail on peak cash and robustness instead. |

A naked move draws a $48.32B fine | pass | fps has been public since 2030, with entry into service in 2040. A 2035 move is not naked, so there is no fine. This is the last round in which a move can be ordered, and none has been ordered before in this game. |

I treat the rule as tested at the order round, so this is a flag, not a breach. |
