- Decision rationale: Decision: plan A, the company default.
- NGSA: hold. NGSA continues to entry into service in 2037.
- NGSA engine code: 7, unchanged.
- A350 Re-engine: hold.
- Delay Tactics: none.

Grid: +23.37 in S1 (expected) and +22.88 in S2 (worst plausible). The plan is positive in both columns, carries no Board item and uses no override.

The team rule, step by step.

1. Delete red-line and pillar breaches.
- Cancel NGSA (every cancel row) is deleted under rule 7. It would be a reaction to Boeing with no failed case behind it: -39.65 against continuing in both columns (-39.09 / -39.08 on the A350 Re-engine rows), with a $25.37B write-off.
- Both moves (on every row) are deleted. They break rule 4, since both counts as two moves, and they fail the integrity pillar because both contains a bottleneck. They also lose $0.47B against one move.
- Bottleneck in place of poaching is deleted on integrity. Toepfer and Wagner both fail it as more than a first claim on our own capacity.

2. Survivors of both colleagues' tests.
- Only plan A passes Toepfer's tests and Wagner's tests together.
- Plan B (one poaching move, +25.02 / +24.54) fails Toepfer's peak-cash test: $5.27B in 2035-36 against the $5.2B line, a binding soft veto. It also trips round-1 break point (d).
- Plan D (A350 Re-engine, +24.91 / +21.67) fails peak cash at $6.67B and fails robustness at -1.21 in S2.
- Plan E (+26.56 / +23.32) fails peak cash at $6.87B, and its A350 part is -1.22 in S2.
- Wagner passes poaching and passes the A350 Re-engine on capacity at the limit, but recommends hold.

3. Choice. The highest-grid survivor in my expected column, S1, is plan A. No tie-break is needed.

4. Overrides considered and declined.
- Poaching clears $1B over plan A in both columns (+1.65 / +1.66), so an override of Toepfer's cash test is arithmetically open. I decline it for five reasons:
  - The Board applies its own $5.2B line and has said it would veto, and I have no override against the Board.
  - The team's stricter condition (the move must change who enters service first) cannot be met on this board, and I do not set it aside.
  - Toepfer flags that three of its five spend years (2037-39) would run with no Airbus programme in development, which is the spirit of rule 5.
  - Nothing in my record shows me using or favouring such a lever.
  - The money cannot be spent inside the line while NGSA spends.
- The A350 Re-engine trails plan A by $1.21B in S2, so it fails both the override rule and the Board's override review.
- No override is used; the budget of one per game stays intact.

5. What discipline costs on the grid.
- S1: $3.19B below row E and $1.65B below row B.
- S2: $1.66B below row B.
- These are costs of the binding cash test, the integrity pillar and the Board's vetoes. They are not a doctrine premium.

6. Delay Tactics are now closed for the game. fps enters service in 2040, and the next round is 2045. No move was ordered in either round, and nothing is said in public.

7. Against what I am paid to protect.
- Five pillars: plan A adds nothing that tests safety, quality or integrity [AX-0073, AX-0081].
- Ramp: there is no new concurrency on the shared NGSA/A350 teams while NGSA industrialises. The A320 ring-fence holds [AX-0010, AX-0054].
- The Airbus of the 2030s: NGSA is kept whole on its technology clock, entering service two years after the 2035 technology-ready year. Boeing's fps on the 10-year path moves nothing (rules 1, 2 and 7) [AX-0020, A-0409, A-0225].
- Board: the defaults are what it would approve.

8. Engine code. I keep 7 on Wagner's supply recommendation and the Board's. All three makers are ready by 2037, CFM backstops UltraFan NB (which has no margin), code 3 is avoided, and the code is not a veto ground.

9. Board-gate lines. There is no Board item. For the package:
- five pillars: pass;
- Toepfer: pass on every test, with peak $5.07B, headroom $0.13B, no break point triggered (phasing and the euro rate cannot be seen in the brief), +22.88 in the worst column, and returns fundable at the lower end of the policy with no equity raise;
- Wagner: pass on safety, quality, the A320 ramp shield and NGSA readiness; supply crunch not applicable;
- grid: +23.37 / +22.88.

10. What I take to the Board outside the orders.
- Toepfer's flag that the per-share dividend in 2035-36 depends on FCF of about $5.9B or more. The payout is set each year inside the 30-50% policy on actual FCF.
- Wagner's watch item on P&W new-build engines for the A320neo.
- The 2045 staged case for the A350 Re-engine, which now needs a widebody engine ready by its entry into service as well as the A350F and Spirit confirmation.
- Expected scenario: S1: rivals hold. Boeing says it is "not starting a second development alongside this one" and is not changing 737 rates, so I put a 787 Re-engine this round at about 20%. S2 (Boeing launches a 787 Re-engine) is the worst plausible column, and the case shows it: plan A stays at +22.88 there.. Best grid plan there: NGSA hold + A350 Re-engine + one Delay Tactics move (row E): +26.56 in S1 and +23.32 in S2. It fails Toepfer's peak-cash test ($6.87B in 2035-36), and its A350 part is -1.22 in S2. The Board vetoes both added items. The best row that every test and the Board pass is plan A: NGSA hold + A350 hold, +23.37 in S1 and +22.88 in S2. That is $3.19B below row E in S1. Row B (one poaching move, +25.02 / +24.54) fails the cash line and the Board's veto.. Premium +0.00 $B: No doctrine premium is paid. Plan A is the only option that passes Toepfer's and Wagner's tests together, and the Board would approve it. The $3.19B gap to row E in S1 and the $1.65-1.66B gap to row B are the price of binding tests: the $5.2B cash line, robustness in S2, integrity, and the Board's would-veto list. They are not a premium I chose to pay under the $3B allowance. No PV is traded for the briefing objective.
- Predictions: Boeing:
- About 80% that it holds this round: no 787 Re-engine and no Rate Increase, with fps kept at entry into service in 2040 on CFM. About 20% that it launches a 787 Re-engine (S2), which would close our A350 Re-engine under rule 3.
- I expect no public Boeing move aimed at NGSA, and no fps re-timing announced this round.

Engines:
- CFM ducted and P&W GTF2 ready in 2036 as stated.
- UltraFan NB ready in 2037 with no margin. I put a slip at about 25%; if it slips, RR is dropped and NGSA enters service in 2037 on CFM + PW.
- No RR-P&W Joint Venture formed this round.
- The CFM-Embraer partnership continues toward 2037, with no new effect on our grid.

Our board path if nobody moves:
- narrowbody share 61% in 2037, 63% in 2039, 62% in 2040, 57% in 2045 and 53% from 2049;
- ΔPV +23.37 in S1 and +22.88 in S2.

The 2045 round: the A350 Re-engine comes back as a staged case only if no 787 Re-engine is on the record and a widebody engine can be ready by its entry into service.
- Expected ΔPV +23.27 $B; board after the round +23.37 $B

- Other moves: NGSA engine selection confirmed: CFM, Pratt & Whitney and Rolls-Royce (code 7 unchanged) (public); Secure supply for the NGSA ramp after entry into service (public); A320 ring-fence and quarterly ramp report, with a P&W new-build watch item (private); NGSA readiness report to the Board's twice-yearly safety review (private); Audit Committee cash gate, 2035-37 (private); Capital allocation inside the policy (private); Open European engineering recruitment continues (public); A350 Re-engine kept in reserve: staged widebody case for the 2045 round (private); Zero-for-zero trade advocacy (public)

LEFT OUT: (a) A Joint Venture commitment is in no column. 'Dominate' would then be lost even in the record column. - A third reading I can't price: a Joint Venture engine ready 2041 (the date in the other maker's public move) governing fitting on NGSA. - What I can say: a 2035 formation needs both commitments in the same sealed round, and the one partner said it would revisit only once the other's commitment was on the record. It is not. What I can't say: which reading the board applies. (b) Codes lock. Confirmed: rounds 2045 and 2050 fall after NGSA's 2037 and fps's 2040 entry. What stays open: an airframe cancel before entry (this round only), a Joint Venture in any round, covert Delay Tactics on fps (Boeing -5 pp for 5 years from 2040, which hits our slot in S1), and widebody moves in later rounds against GEnx's +5 pp. (c) Upside not booked: NGSA moving to code 5 or 6 (CFM plus one, which fits Airbus's stated aim), or a rival ready date slipping past NGSA's 2037. What the Board expects from me is covered above: every alternative against hold column by column, the run-rate against the room, and the premium count. |

Operating head tests:

| Test | Threshold | Result | Numbers |
|---|---|---|---|
| Turn on, turn off (date veto ground 1): ready year of every order | No ready year earlier than the board's development time. Ducted: launch + 6. Open Fan: launch + 10 and not before 2045. Partner Embraer: counts 7 years after launch. GEnx: counts 3 years after launch and not before 2035. An order whose value depends on an earlier year fails (inference: my test standard applied to the board's development times). | pass | Ducted: launched 2030, ready 2036 (2030 + 6). Partner Embraer: launched 2030, counts from 2037 (2030 + 7), the year after the Ducted it needs. GEnx upgrade: launched 2030, in service 2033 (2030 + 3). The grid counts it from 2042 because no re-engine is on the record, and never before 2035. Open Fan: held. Launched in 2035 it would be ready in 2045 (2035 + 10, which is also the 2045 floor). The hold row (-31.20 / -58.50 / -78.31, S1-S3) is priced on these years and no earlier, and no order in Culp's lean depends on an earlier year. Ask (1): 2036 holds as the floor at the board's development time, and nothing earlier. On what has been turned on and turned off: my record holds no ducted core or hot-section result, because it ends in 2025. The test base under that floor is LEAP-1A HPT hardware, turned on and turned off and certified in December 2024 [CX-0009, CX-0225]; RISE, over 350 tests with an early durability focus [CX-0216]; and RISE compact-core HPT blades in dust testing [CX-0222]. So 2036 is a gate reported on test, not a promise (inference). |

No Ducted ready date before 2036 (launch + 6), no Embraer date before 2037 (launch + 7), no Open Fan entry before 2045, and no RISE fly-demo, ground-test or flight-test date. | pass | Dates in Culp's frame and statement plan: Ducted 2036 (a floor, launch + 6); Embraer 2037 (launch + 7); GEnx in service 2033, counting from 2042; NGSA entry 2037 and fps entry 2040 (the airframers' published dates, not ours to speak for); Open Fan 'not before 2045'. No RISE demo, ground-test or flight-test date appears, and the public record carries none (round-1 other move). All are at or after the board's development times, so the test passes. Ask (7): 2036 passes as a floor. 'On track for a 2036 floor, on test' claims progress my record cannot show. Word it 'not before 2036, reported against that gate on test', as in 2030. At step 4 I fail any Ducted date before 2036, any Embraer date before 2037, any Open Fan entry before 2045 and any RISE demo, ground-test or flight-test date [CX-0151, CX-0211]. Advice: give no Open Fan date at all; the entry is the airframers' call. Keep '2035 is the last launch that could be ready by 2050' in the objective_note, not in the statement. |
| Ready no later than the airframe: Ducted (advice) | Report the gap in years. A defensive Ducted ready after the airframe's entry still earns +10 pp from its ready year once a new NB is in service. Earlier is better, but a late date does not fail it. Not a veto ground (inference). | pass | Ducted ready 2036. NGSA entry 2037: 1 year ahead. fps entry 2040: 4 years ahead. On the board the +10 pp counts from the year the engine is ready, once a new narrowbody is in service, so from 2037; the brief's 2036 row is still 76.0%. A one-year slip to 2037 therefore costs nothing on the board. A slip to 2038 loses the 2037 year: 10 pp x 4,000 is about 400 engines, about $3.04B nominal, or about $1.24B PV at 2026. That is my arithmetic from the brief's 2037 row, which I reproduce at 20.44 against the brief's 20.4 (inference; renormalisation ignored). CFM is never dropped from NGSA, since the LEAP derivative is always available, so a slip costs share, not the slot. A cancel, by contrast, costs -15.30 / -16.67 / -17.65. Track record: my test-backed fixes landed on time, but my demonstration-type dates ran late. The LEAP-1B kit moved from 2025 to 1H 2026, and the RISE fly demo from mid-decade to 'this decade' [CX-0008, CX-0210, CX-0151, CX-0211]. Airbus weighs published ready dates, so any slip is reported early, in the next round. |

It counts 7 years after launch and needs a new CFM engine (inference: my date test applied to the board's rule). | pass | It counts from 2037 (2030 + 7): the same year as NGSA's entry and 3 years before fps's 2040. It needs a new CFM engine, the Ducted, which is ready in 2036, one year ahead. Ask (2): on the board it is on track for 2037. Physically it rides on the Ducted, so a Ducted slip past 2037 carries Embraer with it (inference); nothing else ties the two dates. My record holds no Embraer evidence. No evidence: following Culp and doctrine. |

Ready after it, the board takes 35 pp of NB share (20 pp with lobbying). Not a veto ground (inference). | fail | Launched in 2035, it is ready in 2045 (2035 + 10, and not before 2045). That is 8 years after NGSA's 2037 entry and 5 years after fps's 2040, so the 35 pp wait applies in every column (20 pp with Lobby). Item against hold, verified on the grid: -57.60 / -57.60 / -40.09. With Lobby: -37.34 / -37.35 / -35.99. Lobby beside it: +20.26 / +20.25 / +4.10. Culp holds it, so his plan does not fail this test. Ask (4): confirmed, it cannot be ready by 2037 or 2040. RISE on real testing: over 350 tests with an early durability focus [CX-0216]; compact-core HPT blades in dust testing, the earliest we have ever started that test [CX-0222]; and open-fan noise tested with Airbus, lower than today's LEAP [CX-0013]. The evidence shows no open durability failure, but it ends in October 2025. The ground and flight test was set for 'this decade' [CX-0211], and its outcome is not in my evidence. The public record carries no RISE demo, ground-test or flight-test date. |

This is a wording check unless a fuel-burn claim is made (inference). | pass | Neither the brief nor the frame makes a fuel-burn claim. The frame calls the Ducted a defence under H3, never our next generation, and keeps the 20% line on RISE. Ask (6): nothing has changed. 20% is 'practically impossible ... without the open fan', and a ducted engine has 'less than half' the gain of an open fan, which is rated at 'more than 20%' [CX-0180, CX-0183, CX-0001]. I recheck the statement's wording at step 4. |
| Durability first | No plan trades durability or time on wing for fuel burn or schedule (inference; Culp's line applied to my seat). | pass | The hold plan trades no durability for fuel burn or schedule. The frame commits that a date moves rather than a durability margin, and that covers the Ducted's run into 2036 [CX-0208]. Ask (3): I raise no safety or durability flag on the Ducted, the LEAP fleet or RISE hardware, because none is in the evidence. LEAP background, not a flag: in 2023 fleet leaders ran 9,882 cycles against about 17,000 for the CFM56 in benign conditions, and about 4,800 against 7,000-8,000 in harsh ones [CX-0006]. Ducted gate calls (ready year, durability, safety-critical parts) go to the one supply chain, engineering and quality team [CX-0633], with Safran's engineers on a CFM engine, as set in the round-1 private move. - Decision rationale: DECISION: hold on every field. No Board item, $0 of new R&D, $0 of new strain, premium $0.

THE FRAME [CX-0467]. In 2030 the game was to get a new CFM engine in place before the next narrowbody. That is done. fps selected CFM (code 3, entry 2040). NGSA selected all three makers (code 7, entry 2037). The game now is the installed base on airframes already launched [CX-0422], and it is won on delivery, not on new launches. Both airframes enter service before the 2045 round, so this is the last round in which either code can move. "We don't have a birthright on that next order" [CX-0564] holds until 2040.

THE GRID (brief §3). I checked it myself and Ghai confirmed every figure. Hold everything is -31.20 / -58.50 / -78.31 (S1 / S2 / S3): -46.08 on my weights (0.60 / 0.20 / 0.20) and -56.00 on equal weights. It is the best plan in every column. Each alternative against hold (S1 / S2 / S3; my weights / equal):
- Ducted cancel: -15.30 / -16.67 / -17.65 (-16.04 / -16.54). It writes off $3.33B. Ghai's correction: it saves $2.67B on the board ($0.67B of R&D plus the $2B strain handed back) against about $18-20B of engine value given up.
- Embraer cancel: -3.09 / -4.45 / -5.44 (-3.83 / -4.33).
- Open Fan: -57.60 / -57.60 / -40.09 (-54.10 / -51.76). In S1 that is the $8B bill plus about $49.6B of wait penalty.
- Open Fan + Lobby: -37.34 / -37.35 / -35.99 (-37.07 / -36.89).
- Lobby alone: about -1.00.
- Every combination with cancels is dominated.
Nothing on the menu is positive in any column, S1 included. Weights move the booking, not the decision.

MY TESTS.
- Additive and paid for the risk [CX-0301]: no order beats hold anywhere, so nothing is ordered.
- H3: the trigger is now on the record. GTF2 and UltraFan NB are launched and fitted on NGSA, so the Ducted stands as a defence by the book, and keeping it is worth +15.30 in the record column.
- H2: NGSA (2037) and fps (2040) both enter service before a 2045 Open Fan. Every route waits in every column, so there is no launch. The RISE allowance cannot cover it: the cheapest route costs $35.99B against $15B, and even the allowance plus the full $4.0B game cap is $17-19B short.
- H5: RISE continues whole as technology. Holding the Open Fan is not a RISE cancel.
- Strain: no new project, so no new strain. Supply load is the live risk, but it is unpriced (see Ali).
- 20% and durability lines: unaffected. The Ducted is never called the next generation, and no durability margin is traded for the 2036 floor.
- Tie-break: not needed, since no alternative comes within ε.

LOW CASE FIRST [CX-0248, CX-0536]. The expected column is S1, the selections on the record. I book low at the equal-weight -56.00 and treat S1's -31.20 as upside. Ghai agrees and would not go lower. We raise only when the next bulletin confirms the codes.

SAFRAN GATE (H7). Hold orders no CFM narrowbody move, so there is nothing to gate. Keeping the Ducted and Embraer continues decisions taken jointly in 2030. No reason exists why Safran would agree to a cancel: it would cut Safran's half of the same installed base. RISE continues as a 50-50 decision [CX-0166]. Any price, terms or narrowbody capacity commitment is joint [CX-0184]. GEnx is GE alone.

DOCTRINE, NOT MY RECORD, drove three parts. My record is silent on Embraer, so I followed doctrine (profile.md §6) and kept it, since a cancel runs against H1. My record has no lobbying evidence, so Lobby follows doctrine: only beside a live Open Fan. My record is silent on a Joint Venture and on Delay Tactics, so I followed profile.md §5 and §7: no public reaction and no price cut.

BIAS NAMED. My installed-base lens ('seed corn' [CX-0256], 'price of admission' [CX-0557]) pulls toward an Open Fan for the objective. Admission buys a seat, and here there is no seat to buy: both airframes are in service before 2045. My ramp optimism [CX-0590, CX-0592] is checked by publishing no Ducted rate and using the one year of board slack before 2038.

WHAT CHANGES MY MIND:
- A durability flag on the Ducted: the date moves; it is not cancelled.
- Supply that can't carry the Ducted's entry with LEAP on time: LEAP comes first, and the Ducted's ramp moves.
- A Joint Venture on the record: re-priced next round.
Investors asking does not move me [CX-0444].

No veto stands. Ghai's and Ali's vetoes all fall on orders I don't make, so there are no overrides.
- Expected scenario: S1 (rivals hold): the selections on the public record, fps on CFM (code 3) and NGSA on all three (code 7). It is the backlog we hold, not a hoped-for selection [CX-0248]. Airbus's stated aim of 'CFM and at least one other maker' argues against S2 (P&W alone). Boeing's selection of CFM and the 737's CFM-only history [CX-0632] argue against S3 (RR alone). Codes can still move until entry and we have no birthright [CX-0564], so 40% of my weight sits on the two switch columns. I book on equal weights to stay low.. Best grid plan there: Hold everything: -31.20 in S1 (yield 80.5%). It is also the best plan in S2 (-58.50) and S3 (-78.31), so it is the best plan on any weights: -46.08 on mine and -56.00 on equal weights. My submitted plan is this plan.. Premium +0.00 $B: $0.00 this round and $0.00 of the $4.0B game cap. RISE allowance: $0 of $15B, unused. The submitted plan (hold everything) is the best grid plan in every column, so no doctrine or objective premium arises.

The objective premium I decline to pay, in dollars, for 'Introduce the Open Fan' against the best non-RISE plan (hold):
- Cheapest route, Open Fan + Lobby: $37.34B / $37.35B / $35.99B (S1 / S2 / S3), or $37.07B on my weights and $36.89B on equal weights.
- Open Fan alone: $57.60B / $57.60B / $40.09B.
Either is far above the $2.0B round cap and the $15B RISE allowance, and still $17-19B short even with the allowance and the whole game cap combined. The RISE allowance also fails the airframer-path test, because every route waits on an airframe in every column.

'Dominate NB' raises no premium question. It is met in S1, and no order on the menu restores it in S2 or S3.
- Predictions: COLUMN WEIGHTS: S1 0.60, S2 0.20, S3 0.20. Hold is -46.08 on these weights and -56.00 on equal weights. I book -56.00.

S1 (rivals hold), 0.60. The record carries the selections. Airbus's stated aim is CFM plus at least one other maker, and Boeing picked CFM. I expect P&W and RR to keep their solos on their published dates (GTF2 2036, UltraFan NB 2037).

S2 (NGSA to P&W alone), 0.20. It contradicts Airbus's stated aim of CFM plus at least one other, but Airbus weighs A320 engine delivery performance in its revisit, and we don't control its read. It costs $27.30B against S1.

S3 (fps to RR alone), 0.20. Boeing gave no signal of a switch, but a code is a request until entry, and RR would be ready by 2040. It costs $47.11B against S1. It is the worst column.

RISKS OUTSIDE THE COLUMNS (named, not booked):
(a) A P&W-RR Joint Venture commitment. P&W says it has made none. RR said it would revisit in 2035 only if P&W's commitment were on the record. Both would need to commit in the same sealed round, so I put it at about 15-20% this round. On Ghai's reading it is worth +$7B to CFM if it folds in both solos on their dates, and -$14B if it stacks on top. On the stacked reading 'Dominate' fails even in S1 (about 46% in 2040).
(b) Rival ready dates slipping past NGSA's 2037 entry. That maker would be dropped and CFM would carry more of the slot. Upside I don't book.
(c) Our own Ducted slipping. A slip to 2037 costs nothing on the board; a slip to 2038 costs about $1.2B PV (Ali, inference). Embraer moves with it.
(d) Delay Tactics on the fps: Boeing -5 pp for 5 years from 2040, which cuts our fps slot in S1. It is covert, so we would see it only at game end. No reaction (doctrine).
(e) Raw materials: titanium forgings and rare earths for the Ducted's ramp. Unpriced and unquantified (Ali's flag).
(f) Codes lock after this round. The next bulletin tells us our column, and I raise the booking only then.
- Expected ΔPV −56.00 $B; board after the round −38.29 $B

- Other moves: LEAP delivery first: OE engines and spares on time on the A320neo and 737 (public); Ducted gate reported on test: not before 2036 (public); Supplier readiness at the 9 suppliers and 15 sites before the Ducted's 2036-37 production load (private); Private CFM offer to every airframer on standard terms, with Safran (private); RISE continues whole as technology, 50-50 with Safran (public); No reaction to rival or Joint Venture news (private)

The gear-and-core red-line flag ('joining RR's engine shares RR's, not yours') is rebutted in rationale §5: the order shares risk and investment; the published terms keep the gear and core as one P&W module, run by one P&W team with design authority through certification; there is no technology licence; and the configuration freeze applies. Eddy accepts the rebuttal. overrides is empty. - Return hurdle: pass. The item's value against hold is 0 (S1) / 0 (S2) / 0 (S3) / +5.51 (S4). - Decision rationale: THE DECISION: GTF2 Solo hold, Joint Venture with RR commit, premium 0, overrides empty.

1. Safety and durability first [PX-0035][PX-0073]
- GTF2 (launched 2030) is ready in 2036, against NGSA's 2037 entry into service (code 7, which includes P&W). It would also be ready for fps's 2040 entry into service if Boeing moves to code 7 (S3).
- If the Joint Venture forms this round (S4), its engine is the folded GTF2. It is ready in 2036, not at formation + 6 = 2041, and UltraFan NB's 2037 does not pull it later.
- Eddy passes both of his veto grounds on rows A and B. The fold neither compresses nor resets GTF2, and its configuration stays frozen through certification.
- I name my own bias here. My operating dates have slipped by a quarter to about two years [PX-0017][PX-0024][PX-0098]. So I take Eddy's number, not mine: the one-year margin is not shown at risk on the board, and nothing proves it safe. His ground-1 veto binds the moment test data say otherwise.

2. Starting point: Eddy's proposal
Eddy proposes hold and commit. Mitchill recommends the same orders. No binding veto stands from either of them.

3. GTF2: HOLD. The cancel test, written out
- A cancel writes off $2B x 5/6 = $1.67B in 2035 to save the last ~$0.33B. This is the last round a cancel exists, because GTF2 is ready in 2036 and the next round is 2045.
- Cancel row: -29.42 in every column. Against hold: -20.98 (S1) / +0.34 (S2) / -32.79 (S3) / -20.98 (S4). Weighted: -18.73.
- Break-even P(S2) is 0.98, or 0.83 beside a commit with all the remaining weight in S4.
- My lever rule cancels only once no live airframe can still use GTF2 in time. NGSA can, and an airframer that has named us is not harvested [PX-0064 read across, inference].
- The $0.34B gain in S2 is not worth hedging. A cancel would also turn credibility from met to not met.
- Not a responsible bet with a responsible payback [PX-0010].

4. Joint Venture: COMMIT
- The trigger is met on the public record: NGSA's code 7 includes Rolls-Royce.
- Against hold the commit reads 0 / 0 / 0 / +5.51. That is never below Do Nothing, weighted +0.55, for $0 of new R&D, because GTF2's $2B folds into P&W's share.
- My record on a Joint Venture is Low and holds no evidence of my own. This is doctrine plus the grid, decided on Mitchill's cash test and Eddy's technical test, as my fallback directs.
- I name my architecture loyalty bias ('the architecture of the future' [PX-0014]). It would keep me out. Here the Joint Venture does not replace the GTF; it carries it.
- For: 'the strength of this partnership is critical' [PX-0038], and partners carried 49% of the 2023 charge [P-1563]. IAE ended when the partners were not on the same new engine [P-1875]; here both are on NGSA.
- Rolls-Royce decides this round without seeing our order. The commitment therefore mainly puts P&W on the public record for Rolls-Royce's stated revisit. That is why S4 is weighted only 0.10.

5. Red-line flag (gear and core [P-1233]): REBUTTED, and its condition written into the terms
- The flag said 'joining RR's engine shares RR's, not yours'. That assumed we would join UltraFan. Here GTF2 folds in, so our technology is the one at stake.
- The order commits risk, investment and revenue sharing, not technology. The technology question sits in the terms, and the terms are on the record before Rolls-Royce can commit:
  - the gear and core stay one P&W module and one P&W team, with design authority through certification;
  - there is no technology licence.
- Counting the fold-in toward our $2B share transfers no intellectual property.
- Rolls-Royce rates its own geared IP [P-2004], so it has no claim on ours.
- Eddy confirms that module ownership works for a GTF2-based engine [P-1240]. If any design ever needs the gear or core handed over, his withdraw proposal stands, and we withdraw before formation.
- The flag is therefore rebutted on these terms. The terms are a public other move and part of the statement.

6. Mitchill's four grounds
- Ground 1 (dividend and debt): not engaged. The envelope passes, and the dividend and debt path are intact.
- Ground 2 (unselected engine): passes. GTF2 is selected.
- Ground 3 (terms): passes on the final text. Standard terms, no discounts, no subsidy of UltraFan, and the durability kit is sold.
- Ground 4 (unproven upside): passes. His round-1 objection named its own condition (a live Rolls-Royce code and a commit row never below hold), and both now hold. The booked number is -8.44. Nothing for S4 is booked, and no Joint Venture date beyond 2036 is given.
- His cancel objection rests on return, not on one of his grounds. I adopt it anyway, on my lever rule and the Board's would-veto list.

7. Tie-breaks
- The fleet beats a new engine [PX-0080]: the fleet and A320neo deliveries keep first call on parts, ahead of GTF2.
- 'Miss a cycle' [PX-0071] applies here on visible demand, a live selection. It is RTX-wide in my record; applying it to GTF2 is inference. It argues for holding, not cancelling.
- No rival's price entered the answer [PX-0055].

8. Shared source [P-0746]
NGSA's code 7 stays covered by the shared-source relief declared in round 1, as franchise defence, at 40-50% of a shared platform [P-1292][PX-0058]. No order of ours changes a code this round.

9. Capital order
The dividend first, then debt to pre-ASR levels, then buybacks [PX-0081][P-0686][P-0669]. GTF2's last $0.33B is 5.5% of the ~$6B envelope [PX-0268], and the commit adds nothing. No stretch is used.

10. Premium: 0
- Hold/commit (B) is the best expected PV at -10.14, against -10.69 for hold/hold, -29.42 for cancel and -26.94 for cancel/commit.
- No doctrine-favoured order trails the best, and no objective premium is needed. The total is $0 against the $1B cap.

11. Disclosure
- The statement leaves out the round-1 promise to launch in any round we are named. It keeps 2036 and 'proven before entry into service' as standards we set, not claims.
- It offers GTF2 only where we can be ready by an airframer's entry into service. It says nothing on Airbus's code review or on any airframer's dates [PX-0062]. It puts the fleet and A320neo deliveries ahead of GTF2.
- Its only lever on S2 is product, time on wing and delivery performance. The board prices none of that.

12. Incrementalism
My incrementalism leans me to S1. It does not move the answer: B weakly dominates A on any weights, and C needs P(S2) of about 0.98.
- Expected scenario: S1: rivals hold. Airbus keeps NGSA on code 7, Boeing keeps fps on CFM, and Rolls-Royce does not commit this round. Weight 0.67, on Mitchill's weights, which I adopt.. Best grid plan there: In S1, GTF2: hold + commit to JV reads -8.44. It ties with GTF2: hold (-8.44) and beats cancel (-29.42). It weakly dominates hold across all columns (0 / 0 / 0 / +5.51), so it is the best plan in S1 and the best expected PV overall (-10.14).. Premium +0.00 $B: No premium is needed.
- The chosen plan (hold + commit) has the best expected PV, -10.14 on Mitchill's weights.
- It weakly dominates hold/hold (-10.69), so no doctrine-favoured order trails the best.
- It also serves the objective: credibility stays met, and the commit narrows the Capitalise gap in S4.
Doctrine plus objective premium is $0, against the $1B team cap and the Board's $0.5B.
- Predictions: S1 0.67, S2 0.15, S3 0.08, S4 0.10 (sum 1.00). Expected column: S1. These are Mitchill's weights, adopted over my provisional 0.62/0.15/0.08/0.15.
- S2 (0.15): Airbus wants CFM plus a second source. GTF2 has a year of margin to NGSA's 2037 entry into service, and UltraFan NB has none. But A320 engine delivery performance is on Airbus's list, and that is our record.
- S3 (0.08): Boeing owns its design and runs a single development on CFM.
- S4 (0.10): Rolls-Royce's revisit is conditional on our commitment being on the record, and it decides this round sealed, without seeing it.
Expected PV by row on these weights:
- hold/hold: -10.69
- hold/commit: -10.14
- cancel: -29.42
- cancel/commit: -26.94
- Expected ΔPV −10.14 $B; board after the round −2.93 $B

- Other moves: GTF durability kit (Hot Section) for the installed fleet (public); Joint Venture terms, published with the commit (public); GTF2 configuration freeze through certification (private); Order of call on pacing parts: the fleet and A320neo deliveries first (private); GTF2 readiness report to the Board each round (private); Standing 2045 review of the Joint Venture commitment (private)

|---|---|---|---|---|
| Joint Venture with P&W | commit | approve | Every test that touches this item passes. We checked it against the brief's grid ('UltraFan NB: hold + commit to JV' minus 'UltraFan NB: hold', other orders at hold). S1-S5: 0 / 0 / 0 / +2.29 / 0. Expected value is +0.23 on McCabe's weights (0.55 / 0.20 / 0.10 / 0.10 / 0.05), +0.23 on our case (0.55 / 0.15 / 0.10 / 0.10 / 0.10) and +0.11 to +0.46 with S4 at 0.05-0.20. It is never below hold under any weights. (1) ExCo process: pass. McCabe co-signs the commit in her test memo and again in her step-4 veto check, with no withheld signature or binding veto on this field. Her binding withholdings cover the upgrade, UltraFan WB and a commit beside either; none is ordered, so all are respected. Watson invokes no Civil veto. The red-line flags both colleagues raised (upgrade, UltraFan WB, cancel) apply to orders not submitted and are struck in rationale item 7. `overrides` is empty. (2) Safety and maturity: pass. Watson's 'Ready by EIS' test passes against the final orders. Formed this round (S4), the Joint Venture engine takes the folded UltraFan's 2037 date, or 2036 with GTF2. That meets NGSA's 2037 EIS. A private move keeps the folded programme on its own test clock, with no new combined design for 2037. His three conditions, all checked against the final orders, are carried: no early or Joint Venture date in public; the engine counted only at the folded programme's bulletin date (otherwise 2041); and the support plan carried privately with its dates (contracts 2035, spares and field service 2036, EIS-readiness review 2036). The zero margin is advice, not a ground. (3) Partner alignment: pass. P&W's commitment is not on the record, but NGSA's live code 7 includes P&W, and the weighted value is at least 0. (4) Profit over share: pass, at +0.23 and at or above 0 under any weights, with no premium. (5) Declared premium: pass. `premium` is 0; the chosen row is best or tied-best in every column. (6) Reasonable worst case: pass. The worst plausible column is 0, against a limit of -$4B, and no column needs new cash. In S4 the +2.29 is the $8B x 2/7 not spent; the $1.71B beyond our $4B share is already spent. The off-grid S3+S4 case is not a grid column and books nothing. McCabe bounds it at about -0.09 against the S2+S4 hedge at about +0.21. (7) One big programme at a time: pass. Both widebody items are at hold, so strain is $0, and a formed Joint Venture folds the Solo, so there is no second narrowbody project. (8) Balance sheet and position weakening: not applicable. McCabe invokes no balance-sheet veto: the remaining £0.84bn a year comes from free cash flow, with no equity, no borrowing and leverage at or below about 1.5x. (9) Disclosure: pass. 'Ready in 2037, seven years from launch, and not before' is 2030 + 7. There is no Joint Venture ready date and no airframer date. | commit; hold |

- Decision rationale: So, the decision, by the team rule.

1. START FROM THE PROPOSAL. UltraFan NB Solo: hold, which keeps the programme to readiness with no cancel. Joint Venture with P&W: commit. UltraFan WB: hold. Trent 1000 upgrade: hold. Both memos recommend exactly these orders, and the Board says it would approve them.

2. BINDING VETO CHECKS.
- CFO. McCabe co-signs the commit against the hurdle. It is 0 / 0 / 0 / +2.29 / 0 against 'UltraFan NB: hold', +0.23 expected, worst column 0, with no new cash in any column. She withholds her signature on the upgrade, on UltraFan WB and on a commit beside either widebody item. None of these is ordered. She invokes no balance-sheet veto. The remaining $2.29B of Solo spend ($8B x 2/7, about $1.14B or £0.84bn a year in 2036-37, £1.68bn in total) comes from free cash flow, after the 2026-28 returns programme and inside a broadly flat R&D envelope. No equity, no borrowing, leverage at or below about 1.5x.
- Civil. Watson invokes no Civil veto. UltraFan NB, ready 2037 (2030 + 7), against NGSA's 2037 EIS is zero margin: a pass with a note. His concurrence rests on three conditions, and all three are met in these orders.
  (a) The public statement gives no UltraFan NB date before 2037, no Joint Venture ready date and no airframer date. 2037 is not placed next to NGSA, and the date is framed as a clock, 'not before'.
  (b) I count the Joint Venture engine only at a folded programme's bulletin date (UltraFan 2037, or GTF2 2036) and otherwise at formation + 6 (2041). I promise no new combined design for 2037: no P&W gear in UltraFan, no core change. The folded programme keeps its own test clock (private other move).
  (c) The support plan is carried as a private other move with its dates. Partner-shop agreements on the Singapore model and the castings, forgings and gearbox contracts are placed in 2035. The spares pool and field service stand up in 2036, and the EIS-readiness review is in 2036.
- No binding veto stands, and overrides is empty.

3. GRID, EACH ITEM AGAINST THE SAME ROW WITHOUT IT (S1 / S2 / S3 / S4 / S5). My weights, which McCabe confirmed: S1 0.55, S2 0.20, S3 0.10, S4 0.10, S5 0.05.
- S2 is a signal: Airbus's 2035 code review is on the record.
- S3 and S4 have nothing on the record.
- S5 sits below 'nothing': Boeing has said no second development, and Airbus says the A350 family is its focus.
Item by item:
- JV commit ('hold + commit to JV' minus 'hold'): 0 / 0 / 0 / +2.29 / 0. Expected +0.23 on my weights and on the Board's case (0.55 / 0.15 / 0.10 / 0.10 / 0.10); +0.11 to +0.46 with S4 at 0.05-0.20. Worst column 0, against a $4B limit. In S4 the whole +2.29 is spend not made: the $1.71B beyond our $4B share is already spent, sunk, with no new cash.
- Trent 1000 upgrade: -6.02 / -6.02 / -6.03 / -6.02 (-6.03 beside the JV) / -5.87. Expected -6.01, worst -6.03. That is beyond its own $2B bill and above the $3B cap, and it is a red line. Not ordered.
- UltraFan WB: -9.00 / -9.00 / -9.00 / -9.00 / -6.95. Expected -8.90, worst -9.00. There is no Re-engine on the record, it overlaps UltraFan NB, and it is far above the $1B sub-cap. Not ordered.
- Cancel UltraFan NB: -24.54 / -3.22 / -42.25 / -24.54 / -24.54. Expected -22.05 (-19.99 beside the JV, -4.00 in S4). Worst -42.25. NGSA's code 7 flies it. Not ordered.
Row EVs: 'hold + commit to JV' +15.99; hold +15.76; 'hold + JV + T1000' +9.98; 'hold + T1000' +9.75; 'hold + JV + UltraFan WB' +7.09; 'hold + UltraFan WB' +6.87; 'cancel + JV' -4.00; cancel -6.28.

4. EXPECTED SCENARIO AND BEST PLAN. I expect S1, rivals hold. Its best plan is 'UltraFan NB: hold' at +18.26, tied with 'hold + commit to JV' at +18.26. The commit is best or tied-best in every column and best outright in S4 (+20.55). Premium $0B.

5. OFF-GRID (inference, nothing booked). If S3 and S4 come in the same round, equal shares could hand P&W up to about $8.9B of the fps upside, at a joint weight of about 0.01. If S2 and S4 come together, the Joint Venture keeps us on NGSA and recovers about $10.7B, at a joint weight of about 0.02. Net about +0.12. The two roughly offset, the S2 hedge is the larger, and the sign of +0.23 does not change. Orders are sealed and simultaneous, so P&W cannot time its commit to Boeing's or Airbus's code.

6. WHY COMMIT, ON MY RECORD. 'We don't commit that easily' [RX-0124], so I do not take this lightly. The commitment costs nothing in any column, and an airframe can use it: NGSA's live code 7 includes both makers, and a 2035 formation is ready by 2037 from the folded UltraFan. So it is a choice, not options management [RX-0051]. Partnership is the preference, not a need [RX-0118]. The terms credit what we bring [RX-0072]: shared risk and investment, our technology protected as P&W protects its gear and core. It saves the last $2.29B if P&W comes in, which is cash as an input [RX-0042]. The test is binary [RX-0050]: P&W comes to the table, or we deliver alone. It is not open-ended. A sealed withdrawal is planned for 2045 unless that grid shows the commit at or above hold, and no widebody item goes beside it. The thresholds and the Joint Venture terms are doctrine and inference, not my record: my record is Low on Joint Venture terms.

7. RED LINES, CHECKED AGAINST THE FINAL ORDERS.
- No cancel of a flown programme: met.
- No widebody item beside UltraFan NB in development: met. Strain $0.
- Joint Venture with P&W commit: met on the partner test (NGSA's live code 7 includes P&W), co-signed, with no Civil veto and no widebody item beside it.
- No early date and no airframer date: met.
- No item loses more than its own bill: the commit's worst column is 0.
- Premium within cap: $0B.
- No price concessions.
No colleague flagged a red-line breach against these orders. The flags both raised (upgrade, UltraFan WB, cancel) apply to options that are not ordered.

8. CAPACITY AND THE FRANCHISE (advice). Narrowbody volume is 965 engines in 2037 and 990 at the 2039 peak, against 197 widebody a year. Work goes through partner shops and our best suppliers. Widebody is 58.0% to 2041 and 55.2% from 2042, so the franchise holds above 50%. The franchise test comes back in 2045.
- Expected scenario: S1: rivals hold (weight 0.55). Airbus keeps NGSA on code 7, Boeing keeps fps on CFM, Pratt & Whitney does not commit to the Joint Venture, and there is no Re-engine.. Best grid plan there: In S1: 'UltraFan NB: hold' at +18.26 (116.6%), tied with 'UltraFan NB: hold + commit to JV' at +18.26. The chosen row is best or tied-best in every column and best outright in S4 (+20.55). Its expected value is +15.99 on my weights, against +15.76 for plain hold.. Premium +0.00 $B: None. 'UltraFan NB: hold + commit to JV' is never below 'UltraFan NB: hold': 0 / 0 / 0 / +2.29 / 0, +0.23 expected. It is the best expected row and best or tied-best in every column, so no doctrine-favoured order costs PV. No widebody item is ordered, so no UltraFan WB sub-cap is used.
- Predictions: Weights: S1 0.55, S2 0.20, S3 0.10, S4 0.10, S5 0.05.
- Airbus keeps NGSA on code 7 (likely, 0.80). On its own published test, UltraFan's 2037 ready date meets the NGSA entry, GTF2 is ready in 2036 and CFM's ducted engine in 2036, and its stated aim is CFM plus at least one other maker.
- Boeing keeps fps on CFM (0.90). It is on the 10-year path to 2040 and has said it starts no second development.
- Pratt & Whitney does not commit to the Joint Venture this round (0.90). Its record says it has made no commitment, it protects its gear and core, and GTF2 is already on NGSA.
- No 787 or A350 Re-engine this round (0.95). Airbus says the A350 family is its focus.
- CFM continues the ducted engine and the Embraer partnership, with RISE as technology. GEnx stays in service.
- RR: UltraFan NB ready 2037 on its clock, with nothing compressed. The 2036 readiness review clears gearbox durability, or the engine slips.
Expected ΔPV of the chosen orders: +15.99 $B on my weights (+17.05 on the Board's case). S1 outcome +18.26.
- Expected ΔPV +15.99 $B; board after the round +20.55 $B

- Other moves: Joint Venture commitment to Pratt & Whitney on aligned terms (public); Joint Venture internal conditions (structure and maturity) (private); 2045 withdrawal review of the standing commitment (private); UltraFan NB test programme held to its seven-year clock (private); Narrowbody support build: 2035 contracting (Watson's plan, step 4 due) (private); Round-1 plan for failure retired (private); UltraFan demonstrator path and technology pull-through continue (public); Trent fleet durability (time on wing) continues as funded (public); Pricing discipline (private); Widebody franchise watch for 2045 (private)


## Round 3: decision year 2045

**2045: everyone holds; neither airframer re-engines its widebody**

- Both new narrowbodies are in service. The live levers were the Re-engines, the Open Fan and Rolls-Royce's widebody engines, and every company held.
- Airbus: the A350 Re-engine was worth +$0.89B if Boeing held, under its $1B bar, and no widebody engine would be ready for it.
- Boeing: its Board would have vetoed the 787 Re-engine on its business case: +$0.29B if Airbus held, −$1.26B if Airbus re-engined too.
- Rolls-Royce found the Trent 1000 upgrade still carrying a $5B strain charge while the Joint Venture ran.

### Orders (as adjudicated)

| Player | Orders | Public statement |
|---|---|---|
| Boeing | Do Nothing | Our new single-aisle airplane has been in service since 2040, on the date we gave you. That's five years of airplanes in customers' hands, and we're climbing the ramp the way we said we would. We take one step at a time, on our KPIs, agreed with the FAA, and only after the line has shown it's stable at the rate it's on. We've come a long way. There's still a lot of work in front of us, and we're not going to hurry the last part of the climb. We're not launching a new program today. We've looked hard at the 787's next step. It's a strong airplane, and it has room to grow from our Charleston capacity. We'll take that next step when the market, the technology and we are ready. Today all three aren't there. So I'm not going to give you a date for something we haven't launched. Paying down debt is far and away our priority. We're funding the business from our operations, with no new equity. I'm not going to put a year on the rating before the data supports it. You'll see it in the numbers. We said we wanted to hold half of the single-aisle market. We're at 43% this year, up from 38% when the airplane came into service, and still climbing. That's short of half, and I won't dress it up. We earn the rest one delivery at a time. A few things haven't changed. We'll certify before we build at rate. We won't take on fixed-price development. We'll keep our suppliers funded and healthy for the rates in front of us. We'll keep working with CFM, our engine partner, to support those rates. Others make their plans on their own clock. Ours rests on our own readiness. The financials will follow our production performance. |
| Airbus | Do Nothing | Our core principles of safety, quality, integrity, compliance and security will remain at the heart of all we do. Our next-generation single-aisle aircraft has been in service since the second half of the 2030s, with engines from CFM, Pratt & Whitney and Rolls-Royce. We continue its production in a disciplined and controlled way, alongside the A320neo family, and we put fleet safety and operational integrity above all. The people and suppliers who deliver both remain protected. On the widebody side, the A350 family, including the A350F, remains our focus. We keep studying the options for its future on our own technology and industrial clock. We will take decisions when the technology and the case are mature, and not before. We continue to strengthen a more sovereign European supply chain, with long-term agreements sized to our own programmes. We also continue to recruit the engineers who will build the next generations of Airbus aircraft. We fund this within our financial framework: disciplined investment in preparing our future portfolio, and shareholder returns while preserving financial flexibility. On trade, we keep working with industry partners on both sides of the Atlantic for a zero-for-zero tariff regime in civil aerospace. |
| CFM/GE | Do Nothing | Safety, quality, delivery and cost, always in that order. That doesn't change this year either. So let me tell you what we know and what we don't. What we know: both new single-aisle airplanes are in service, and there's a CFM engine on both. The ducted engine CFM and Safran launched in 2030 has been in service since 2036. Our work with Embraer has been in service since 2037, and the upgraded GEnx on the 787 since 2033. Those are dates on the record, not on slides. We didn't take those positions for granted then, and we don't now. We don't have a birthright on the next order, so we earn it the way we earned the last one: by delivering. That means LEAP and the new engine, and the spares behind them, on time to every customer we serve. Spares don't crowd out new engines. What we don't know yet is how these new engines hold up over years of service, especially in the hot and harsh places where engines work hardest. So we'll measure time on wing on the fleet, in cycles, against the yardstick our customers know, and we'll report it on field data, not on analysis. Where we find something, we fix it with Safran, the airframers and the regulators. We don't trade durability for delivery. The ducted engine is the right engine for the airplanes in front of us. It isn't our next-generation engine. That is still RISE. We're all in on that technology with Safran, and we're keeping it whole. When that architecture enters service is really not for us to say. The airframers and their airline customers will decide that. We're not starting anything new this year. We plan on the low case and keep the balance sheet and our capital returns whole. Any commitment on CFM capacity, we make together with Safran. You won't see us pointing fingers; you'll see us solving problems. We get paid in dollars, not in percentages. It's that simple, ain't that hard. |
| Pratt & Whitney | Do Nothing | Let me start with what is not where we want it. Our share of narrowbody engine deliveries was 23.2% in 2044, down from 24.8% in 2039. That is not where we want to be, and we will not buy it back with price. We earn it with time on wing, and with engines delivered when we promised them. What is done: our Joint Venture engine with Rolls-Royce, which carries GTF2's design, has been ready since 2036 and in service on NGSA since 2037. The Joint Venture formed in 2035 on the terms we published. It shares risk, investment and revenue. Pratt & Whitney's gear and core remain our own module, with no technology licence, and each partner's technology is protected. Control what you can control. The fleet is where this engine earns its place, and the fleet record is how we will be judged: time on wing, life-limited-part life and shop visits. We will report what that record shows, not what we hope it shows, and we give no dated recovery. If we find a durability or safety issue, we will say so early, with the plan. The GTF fleet, the new engine fleet and A320neo engine deliveries keep first call on our parts, our shops and our engineers. We have learned our lesson from the prior iterations of the GTF. The GTF Hot Section durability kit stays on offer at shop visits, on standard terms. There are no launch-style discounts. We're not in the early entry phase, the launch phase. We are not starting a new engine programme today. Any new engine we commit will follow an airframer's selection. It will be ready six years from launch, with durability proven before entry into service. Our capital priorities have not changed: the dividend first, then debt, then share repurchase. We will not speak for any airframer's plans, dates, rates or engine choices. |
| Rolls-Royce | Do Nothing | So, our narrowbody engine with Pratt & Whitney is in service, and our job now is the fleet. Safety is at the top. Then time on wing, full-life parts and support through our partner shops. We do not want to underestimate the risks. Widebody is our franchise. Durability work on the Trent fleet goes on, and there is no new widebody engine spend without a Re-engine. UltraFan technology continues as technology. We are not starting a new programme this year. Cash goes first to the balance sheet, then to the dividend, then to distributions from sustainable free cash flow. We are not after market share. We won't do anything not profitable. I will say that categorically. |

### How each company decided (private during the game)

**Boeing** (Kelly Ortberg, Jay Malave, Stephanie Pope; Boeing Board)

| Step | What happened |
|---|---|
| Board guidance | Boeing Board: risk appetite: 4.5 of 5, unchanged in our tests this round. We expect to ease toward 4 only once the Finance Committee sees debt back to a solidly investment-grade position; that is our inference. A launch must clear +$1B in the expected column, no Board item may fall below … |
| Frame | Kelly Ortberg: initial lean: **Orders I lean to:** Do Nothing. - fps: hold - fps engine code: null - 737 rate: hold - 787 Re-engine: hold Row: +0.66 / +0.34. Every order is a hold, so no item goes to the Board. I will still answer each of its five recommendations in board_response. **Why:** - … |
| Test (CFO) | Jay Malave recommends: Do Nothing. Options failing a test: Re-engine in the 2050 window, against the Board's bar (ask 5). Vetoes: B: fps hold + 787 Re-engine (row 'fps: hold + 787 Re-engine') (not binding) |
| Test (operating head) | Stephanie Pope recommends: Do Nothing. Options failing a test: On-time, predictable delivery: 787 Re-engine with EIS 2050, row B (record [BX-1331] by inference; doctrine slip prior); Technology readiness for a 787 Re-engine (doctrine; CEO ask 5; not priced on the board). Vetoes: 737 rate: increase (any combination) (not binding); fps: hold + 787 Re-engine: launch (row B) (not binding) |
| Decide | Kelly Ortberg: Do Nothing |
| Veto check (CFO) | Jay Malave concurs |
| Veto check (operating head) | Stephanie Pope concurs |
| Board review | Boeing Board: not called; every order is the default (a hold is never a Board item) |

CFO tests:

| Test | Threshold | Result | Numbers |
|---|---|---|---|
| Buffer test (slip leg), my binding veto (ask 1) | In the column the brief labels 'Risk case', the plan may sit no more than $2B below Do Nothing in the same column. If the brief has no risk-case column, the test is not applicable and I hold no veto this round. | not applicable | The round-3 grid has two columns, S1 (rivals hold) and S2 (Airbus launches an A350 Re-engine). Neither is labelled 'Risk case'. The fps has been in service since 2040, so it has no slip leg. My veto has nothing to bind this round, which confirms the CEO's reading. No row draws a binding veto. |
| Buffer proxy on S2, the Board's risk case: plan A, Do Nothing (advice; the Board's Rating first gate) (ask 1) | S2 may be no lower than Do Nothing's S2 less $2B: 0.34 - 2.00 = -1.66. | pass | Row 'fps: hold + 787: hold', S2 = +0.34, which is 2.00 above the -1.66 floor. Full row: +0.66 / +0.34 (S1 / S2). Its worst column is S2 at +0.34. Confirmed as the CEO stated. |
| Buffer proxy on S2: plan B, fps hold + 787 Re-engine (advice) (ask 1) | S2 at or above -1.66. | pass | Row 'fps: hold + 787 Re-engine', S2 = -0.92, which is 0.74 above the -1.66 floor. Full row: +0.96 / -0.92. Item against A: +0.30 / -1.26. Its worst column, S2 at -0.92, is $1.26B worse than Do Nothing's. That is advice under Meet and beat, not a veto. Break-even chance of S2: 0.30 / (0.30 + 1.26) = 19.2%. Confirmed. |
| Meet and beat (expected column S1, on Ortberg's provisional read; I have no Airbus evidence of my own and defer to his frame) | Among plans within ε ($1B) in the expected column, prefer the one with the higher worst column (inference). | pass | In S1, A scores +0.66 and B +0.96: 0.30 apart, inside ε. Worst columns: A +0.34 (S2), B -0.92 (S2). The rule prefers A by 1.26. If S2 is expected instead, A leads by 1.26, outside ε. A wins in either column, so the orders do not depend on the Airbus call. B fails the rule. |
| Independent estimate check: rebuilding B's item from the public rules (asks 1-2) | The grid governs. A rebuild that ties out to rounding confirms the estimate, and a residual is reported, not netted (inference). | pass | Inputs: - 787 at $180M: the brief's 2026 revenue of 56.4, less 800 x $48M, over 100 airplanes. - Margin 25.21% for a lone Re-engine, 15.77% if both re-engine (dashboard_game.md §2). - +1 pp of share a year from EIS, over a 20-year window from EIS 2050. Rebuilt item: - S1: operating gain +1.25 PV, bill -0.455, penalty -0.50, giving +0.29. - S2: -1.27. - The grid shows +0.30 / -1.26, so the rebuild ties to rounding. - My rebuild of Do Nothing's S2 loss (A350 alone, 1.5 pp a year) is -0.33, against the grid's -0.32. The estimate holds. The one correction is the penalty, under the next test. |
| Balance sheet first: debt penalty of B (ask 2) | The grid's ΔPV already nets each plan's discounted penalty (α 0.30 x debt x (debt/EV)^2): never subtract it again. Between plans within ε in the expected column, prefer the one that adds the smaller penalty (inference: threshold). | pass | A carries only the fps penalty, -4.73 PV: 0.30 x 105.32 x (105.32/130)^2 = 20.74, less 1.62 at $45B, gives $19.12B undiscounted at 2040. B, on the CEO's convention that bills add on the board's debt: - the plan's penalty becomes 0.30 x 110.32 x (110.32/130)^2 - 1.62 = $22.22B, which is +$3.10B undiscounted. The CEO's figure is confirmed. - A Re-engine alone would add $0.60B. Confirmed. - The bill itself is 5 / 10.98 = 0.455 PV at 2050. Confirmed. Correction on the PV: the grid behaves as if the combined penalty is spread across the bills pro rata (inference from my rebuild, which ties out): - 60.32/65.32 of it is booked at 2040: $20.52B, of which $1.40B is new; - 5/65.32 is booked at 2050: $1.70B. - So the Re-engine's penalty costs about 0.50 PV (0.34 + 0.155), not 0.28. On the board, debt/EV goes from 81% to 85%. All of it is inside B's +0.30 / -1.26 and is never subtracted again. Within ε in S1, A adds the smaller penalty, so A is preferred. |
| Cash path of each bill (ask 2) | When each bill lands, and whether two land together [BX-0547]. Real spend is set against operating profit (inference). | pass | A: no new bill. The fps's $60.32B was booked at EIS 2040 (the brief's 2040 non-recurring line is 60.3). B: - One bill, $5B at EIS 2050, the only one landing that year. No two bills land together. - Real spend is about $1.0B a year over 2045-49, against operating profit of 15.7 / 16.0 / 16.3 / 16.6 / 16.9 (81.5 in total). - That is 6.1% of operating profit and 10.4% of the $48.0B uplift over the status quo. - It is fundable from operations, with no new equity. Funding is not the ground for holding; the return is. |
| Yearly funding report to the Finance Committee: fps phasing, debt path, rating outlook, investment-grade year (ask 4) | Debt is the first call on cash and no new equity is issued. Solidly investment grade means debt back to the board's pre-launch $45B, 35% of the $130B EV (inference: threshold). Publish the conservative year and hold the upside. | pass | Phasing against my 2030-39 curve: - The brief gives no in-year actuals. The fps entered service in 2040 on its date, and its bill was booked at the launch estimate, $60.32B. - 2037-39 operating profit was 6.6 / 6.5 / 6.4: on my trigger line, not below it. - No trigger tripped: no slip past 2040, no peak overrun shown, no second development. - The curve stands as the record. 2030-34 built $8.77B of room, 2035-38 drew $3.66B, 2039 turned positive, and the decade ended about $6.1B down on debt (operating profit less fps spend, before tax, interest and other capex). Post-EIS debt path (board convention: $105.32B at EIS 2040, 81% of EV): - Operating profit was 74.4 over 2040-44 (40.9 above the status quo) and runs at 81.5 over 2045-49 (48.0 above). - Upper bound, the full uplift to debt before tax: $64.4B at end-2044; back to $45B during 2047; $16.4B at end-2049. - Conservative case, half the uplift after tax and interest (inference; the brief has no free-cash-flow line): $84.9B at end-2044, $60.9B at end-2049 (47% of EV), back to $45B in 2053. Rating outlook: - Peak leverage is behind us (2040), and investment grade is supported throughout. - I plan on solidly investment grade by 2053, with 2047 held as upside. - B adds $5B: 2054 on the conservative case, the upper bound unchanged. No new equity in any plan. No dividend or buyback proposal: that is reserved to the Board. |
| Full cost of a slip or cancel: one year of Re-engine certification slip, for scale (ask 3) | Cost a slip once and in full: concessions, rework, learning curve, carrying cost [B-2349]. - Decision rationale: **Decision: Do Nothing.** The orders are fps hold, fps engine code null, 737 rate hold, 787 Re-engine hold. The row is +0.66 / +0.34 (S1 / S2). Every order is the default, so no item goes to the Board.

**1. Team rule, applied.**
- **Malave's buffer test** is not applicable. The brief labels no column 'Risk case', and the fps has been in service since 2040, so it has no slip leg. He confirms he holds no veto this round, and no plan is out on this ground.
- **Board proxy on S2** (floor 0.34 - 2.00 = -1.66): A passes by 2.00 and B by 0.74.
- **KPI doctrine (Pope's seat):** not in play. 737 rate is offered only at hold, and the brief reports no live quality, FAA, certification or supply-chain problem. She checked the veto and did not invoke it.
- No colleague flagged a red line, and I override nothing. The rest is my call.

**2. The grid** (from the brief):
- A, Do Nothing: +0.66 / +0.34.
- B, fps hold + 787 Re-engine: +0.96 / -0.92.
- B's item: +0.30 / -1.26. It breaks even at a 19.2% chance of S2.
- Malave rebuilt the item from the rules and tied out to rounding (+0.29 / -1.27).

**3. My tests.**
- **KPI stability: pass.** No live problem in the brief. Pope rightly says delivery counts are not the KPI record, so the Chief Engineer and the Chief Aerospace Safety Officer report to Aerospace Safety [BX-1271, BX-1259].
- **Three work streams: not converged** [BX-1296].
  - *Market:* S2 is plausible. If both re-engined airplanes enter service in 2050, share does not move and our 787 margin falls to 15.77%.
  - *Technology:* Pope fails it, as advice. The upgraded GEnx has been on the 787 since 2033, so a Re-engine on it adds no new engine. A GEnx derivative has no durability record. UltraFan WB launched now is ready in 2051, after a 2050 EIS. My technology risk appetite is low [BX-1298]. This is unpriced, and it is not why I hold, but it means the work stream isn't there.
  - *Boeing:* fundable. About $1.0B a year is 6.1% of the 81.5 of operating profit in 2045-49. Readiness is not the issue; the return is.
- **Debt before the next airplane: B fails.** A second development must beat Do Nothing by more than ε in the expected column, and B does so in neither column [BX-1305, B-2326].
  - Malave corrects the penalty: +$3.10B undiscounted, which on the grid's pro-rata allocation is about 0.50 PV, not the 0.28 Pope and I used. The grid governs, and nothing is subtracted twice.
  - B would also move his conservative investment-grade year from 2053 to 2054.
- **Certification slip:** there is no risk-case column, so this is for scale only.
  - Malave: one year of slip costs about 0.15 all-in in S1.
  - Pope: doctrine's central +2-year prior takes 40-50% of the S1 item.
  - So B's +0.30 rests partly on a nominal date with no headroom. I won't plan that way [BX-1318, BX-1319].
- **Doing less, better:** +0.30 in S1 is inside ε, so the plan with less to execute wins [BX-1239]. It is also the higher-confidence plan, with a worst column higher by 1.26 [BX-0548]. In S2, A leads by 1.26, outside ε. A wins whichever column I expect.
- **Airbus does not set the date:** both directions apply.
  - I'm not launching to pre-empt an A350 Re-engine. That is doctrine's Chicken-game reading, which I did not use [B-2313].
  - I'm not holding because of Airbus either. The hold rests on ε, debt-first and readiness.

**4. Hard rules.**
- H1, H2, H6 and H7: not at issue.
- H3, second half: not triggered, since no A350 Re-engine is on the record. If Airbus launches one this round, it bars a 787 Re-engine from then on, and I accept that.
- H4: 0 years of overlap (the fps counted 2033-40).
- H5: the fps is in service and cannot be cancelled.
- H8: the premium below.

**5. The 2050 window.** Malave estimates that a 2050 launch scores about +0.04 in S1, about $0.96B short of the Board's bar. So holding now very likely forgoes the 787 Re-engine on this board. That is a loss we accept, not a wait. On an item inside ε, with a technology work stream that hasn't converged, I'd rather be a year late than a year early [BX-1322].

**6. The Board.** It would veto 787 Re-engine: launch on business case (+0.30 / -1.26 clears $1B in neither column) and on downside (-1.26 against its -$1B limit). It approves Do Nothing. Weighed: no conflict.

**7. Record versus doctrine.**
- My record drove: the tie-break [BX-1239, BX-0548], debt-first [BX-1305], the three work streams [BX-1296] and under-disclosure of the rating year [BX-1268].
- Doctrine drove: Pope's supplier binding order, the slip prior and the reading that the engine code is locked.
- Malave's investment-grade year is his inference (debt back to $45B). I keep it internal.
- Expected scenario: S1 (rivals hold), provisional. My confidence on Airbus is Low.

For S1:
- Airbus repeated that the A350 family, including the A350F, remains its focus, and it held the widebody in 2030 and 2035.
- Rolls-Royce's line, no new widebody engine spend without a Re-engine, is a condition on its own spending, not an Airbus commitment.
- A move is real only when it is launched.

S2 stays plausible:
- NGSA has been in service since 2037, which frees Airbus engineering.
- Doctrine expects an A350 Re-engine attempt.

The orders are the same in either column. Hold ties within ε in S1 and wins by 1.26 in S2.. Best grid plan there: In S1 the top row is fps: hold + 787 Re-engine, at +0.96. It is 0.30 above Do Nothing's +0.66, which is inside ε ($1B).

I order Do Nothing (fps: hold + 787: hold, +0.66 / +0.34). The tie goes to doing less, better [BX-1239] and the higher-confidence plan [BX-0548], whose worst column is higher by 1.26. Debt-first and the Board's business case also rule out the Re-engine row: it clears ε in neither column.

In S2, Do Nothing is the top row outright (+0.34 against -0.92).. Premium +0.30 $B: 0.30 is a doctrine premium, forgone in S1, the expected column, by holding the 787 Re-engine. B's +0.96 against A's +0.66 is inside ε, so doing less, better and the higher-confidence plan decide the tie.

- In S2 the premium would be 0: holding gains 1.26 there.
- Objective premium: 0. No order on offer moves the narrowbody path.

Running log:
- Round 1: 0.46 (10-year over 7-year).
- Round 2: 0.
- Round 3: 0.30.
- Cumulative: 0.76, within the $2B cap (H8).

Hard-rule costs are logged apart:
- Round 1, H3: 2.20.
- Round 2, H4: 0.65 (S1 and S3).
- Round 3: none, since H3 and H4 both pass.

Malave notes that holding now very likely forgoes the Re-engine on this board: a 2050 launch is about +0.04. That is a loss we accept, not a wait. It adds no further premium beyond this round's 0.30.
- Predictions: **Airbus** (Low confidence):
- Most likely holds the A350 again this round (S1).
- An A350 Re-engine (S2) is plausible. If it launches, H3's second half bars any 787 Re-engine from then on, and we stand aside.
- NGSA stays in service on code 7.

**Engine makers:**
- Rolls-Royce launches no UltraFan WB without a Re-engine.
- CFM holds RISE as technology, with no product date.
- The P&W-RR Joint Venture engine stays on NGSA.
- CFM ducted shows no durability flag.

**Boeing:**
- The Board is not called to review, since all four orders are holds.
- The fps ramp runs 860 to 940 on KPIs, with narrowbody share at 47% from 2049.
- No live problem is expected, but the CFM line and long-lead structures are the watch items.
- Debt falls on Malave's conservative path, solidly investment grade by 2053, with 2047 as upside.
- 2050 round: the 787 Re-engine likely shows about +0.04 in S1, below the Board's $1B bar, so it is not launched.

**Expected ΔPV:** +0.66 in S1, +0.34 in S2.
- Expected ΔPV +0.66 $B; board after the round +0.66 $B

- Other moves: fps ramp stays KPI-gated, step by step with the FAA (public); Chief Engineer and Chief Aerospace Safety Officer report directly to the Aerospace Safety Committee each year (public); Certify before building at rate on any derivative; no fixed-price development (public); Supply base funded and hot through 2049 (private); CFM allocation tracked by airframer; delivery commitments contracted for the ramp (private); Yearly funding report to the Finance Committee; debt first, no new equity (private); 787 grows from Charleston; no Re-engine launch this round (public); Independent estimate check and early-warning rule continue (private); Customer slots quoted only against the KPI-gated ramp (private)

- Decision rationale: **Decision: the company default.** NGSA hold, engine code none (the only code on the menu), A350 hold, Delay Tactics none.
- Grid: +23.37 in S1 and +23.26 in S2.
- No order differs from the default, so there is no Board item.

**1. Red-line and pillars screen (the team rule's first step)**
Neither option breaks a company red line or the five pillars.
- Rule 1 is met: NGSA entered service in 2037, after the 2035 technology-ready year.
- Rules 2 and 7: there is no NGSA cancel on the menu.
- Rule 3 is not binding: no 787 Re-engine is on the record.
- Rules 4 and 5: Delay Tactics are closed, and none is the only value.
- Rule 6 is met: a 2045 A350 Re-engine would be one launch with zero overlap with NGSA.
- Wagner confirms safety and quality pass on both options. The brief reports no safety, quality or supply problem and no inject.
Both options therefore go on to the colleagues' tests.

**2. Survivors**
- **Plan A (hold)** passes all of Toepfer's tests: peak cash $0, robustness +23.26 in the worst column, net cash and rating proxy. It passes all of Wagner's: safety and quality, ramp shield, engine code, integrity, rule 1.
- **Plan B (A350 Re-engine, entry into service 2050)** fails two binding soft vetoes:
  1. Toepfer's added-move gate, which is also the company's $1B launch test. The item is +0.89 in S1, $0.11B short of $1B, and -0.50 in S2, negative in the worst plausible column.
  2. Wagner's production-readiness test. No widebody engine on the record is ready by 2050: Rolls-Royce spends on a new widebody engine only with a Re-engine, an UltraFan WB launched in 2045 is ready in 2051, and there is no fallback.
  Neither failure is a safety, quality, ramp-shield or supply-crunch result. Both are soft vetoes.
- **Override.** None is available. B's margin over A, the best passing option, is +0.89 in S1 and -0.50 in S2: under $1B in both columns. The Board's override review would also fail it, as a Qualified Majority item that trails hold in its worst column. The game's single override stays unused. I concur on the merits in any case.

A is the only survivor. It is also the doctrine default: within $1B of the best grid row in my expected column, a $0.89B gap in S1.

**3. Tie-breaks** all point to A: less strain, a later capex start, and fewer Board items (0 against 1).

**4. My read of Boeing, and the expected column**
Expected column: S1, at about 60%. S2 at about 40%, within a 30-50% range.
- For S2: in 2035 Boeing said "We'll look at its next step once this airplane is in service". fps has been in service since 2040. Boeing also said the 787 "has room to grow from our Charleston capacity".
- Against S2: "We're not starting a second development alongside this one", "Doing less and doing it better", and debt reduction with a solid investment-grade rating as its priority.
S2 stays the worst plausible column.

At 40%, the launch is worth about +0.33 in expectation (+0.40 at 35%, +0.20 at 50%). It breaks even at 64%. Even at 0% for S2 it is only +0.89, under the $1B test. **The decision rests on our own tests, not on our read of Boeing.** That keeps us on our own clock and ties no widebody step to Boeing.

**5. The staged widebody case the Board asked for in 2035, gate by gate**
| Gate | Result |
|---|---|
| Rule 3 | Met. No 787 Re-engine is on the record. |
| A350F in service and Spirit A350 packages at rate | Passes on the game proxy only. The record is silent after 2025 and Wagner has no line data, so the Board's condition that he confirm both is not met. |
| Widebody engine ready by entry into service | Fails. The gap is built into the clocks: an engine launched alongside the airframe is ready at launch + 6, the airframe enters service at launch + 5. |
| Grid gate (at least $1B in the expected column, not negative in the 787 column) | Fails at +0.89 / -0.50. |
| Peak cash | Passes: $1.0B a year over 2045-49, $4.2B under the $5.2B line. |
| Qualified Majority | Required. |

The option to wait: on Toepfer's reconstruction (an inference), a like-for-like 2050 launch is worth about +0.6 in a rivals-hold column. It is still under $1B. A 787 Re-engine in 2045 would close it under rule 3, and the company already named 2035-37 as the last window. The case stays staged and is not launched.

**6. Board-gate line**
There is no Board item this round. For the record, the hold package:
- five pillars: pass;
- Toepfer: pass, with a peak programme cash of $0 against the $5.2B line;
- Wagner: pass on safety, quality, the ramp shield and integrity;
- grid: +23.37 in S1 and +23.26 in S2.
The Board says hold is never vetoed.

**7. Cost and objective**
- Holding gives up $0.89B in S1, about $0.33B in expectation at 40% for S2. It gains $0.50B against B in S2.
- Plan A's worst column (+23.26) is better than plan B's (+22.76), so it meets the robustness preference.
- No PV is traded for the 60/40 objective, which no order on this menu can move.

**8. Operating actions**
- Supply agreements are resized down to our own falling needs (Wagner).
- The Joint Venture engine is carried as a watch item, with CFM as backstop.
- Buybacks are sized after programme cash (Toepfer).
- Studies stay studies, below EUR 300m each until a Board gate.
- Expected scenario: S1 (rivals hold), at about 60%. S2 (Boeing launches a 787 Re-engine) is at about 40%, within a 30-50% range, and is the worst plausible column.
- For S2: Boeing said in 2035 it would look at the 787's next step once fps was in service. fps has been in service since 2040, and Boeing has said the 787 has room to grow.
- Against S2: Boeing's one-development-at-a-time discipline and its priority on debt reduction and investment grade.
The decision does not depend on this read: the launch fails the $1B test even at 0% for S2.. Best grid plan there: In S1, NGSA hold + A350 Re-engine scores +24.26, against +23.37 for NGSA hold + A350 hold.

The Re-engine row fails two binding soft vetoes: Toepfer's added-move gate (the company's $1B launch test) and Wagner's production readiness (no widebody engine ready by 2050). Its margin of +0.89 / -0.50 over hold allows no override.

The best surviving plan, and the doctrine default within $1B, is NGSA hold + A350 hold: +23.37 / +23.26. It is also the best plan against S2.. Premium +0.00 $B: No premium is paid. Hold is the only plan that survives both colleagues' tests, so it is the highest-grid survivor. It is also within the $1B doctrine default of the best S1 row: the gap is $0.89B, or about $0.33B in expectation at 40% for S2. Hold is the better plan in S2 (+0.50) and in the worst column. No PV is traded for the 60/40 objective, which no order on this menu can move.
- Predictions: **Boeing, this round**
- About 60%: it holds, with no 787 Re-engine.
- About 40%: it launches a 787 Re-engine (S2). That would close our A350 Re-engine under rule 3 for 2050.
- No public Boeing move aimed at NGSA, and no new narrowbody programme announced.

**Engines**
- No new widebody engine launched by Rolls-Royce without a Re-engine.
- CFM ducted and the P&W-RR Joint Venture engine keep delivering to the NGSA ramp, with no slip or shortfall reported.
- Narrowbody engine shares hold near CFM 54% / P&W 23% / RR 23%.

**Our board path if nobody moves**
- Narrowbody share 57% in 2045 and 53% from 2049. Widebody share flat at 41.2%.
- ΔPV +23.37 in S1 and +23.26 in S2.

**2050 round**
- If Boeing holds, the A350 Re-engine returns only as a staged case. I expect it to fail the $1B test (about +0.6 on Toepfer's discount) unless an engine is committed ahead of the airframe.
- If Boeing launches a 787 Re-engine, the case is closed.
- Expected ΔPV +23.33 $B; board after the round +23.37 $B

- Other moves: Resize the NGSA and A320 supply agreements down to our own needs (public); Twice-yearly safety review: the NGSA ramp report and the A320neo transition report, taken together (private); A350 Re-engine kept as a staged widebody case, with gates for any re-test (private); Staged studies outside the board: an NGSA derivative, an A220 variant, and research and technology for the generation after NGSA (private); ECSC integrity review of supply sizing, hiring and pricing (private); Capital allocation inside the policy, with buybacks sized after programme cash (private); Open European engineering recruitment continues (public); Zero-for-zero trade advocacy (public)

In 2023, fleet leaders ran 9,882 cycles against about 17,000 for the CFM56 in benign service, and about 4,800 against 7,000-8,000 in harsh service. The LEAP-1A HPT hardware was certified in December 2024. By July 2025 it was in all LEAP-1A deliveries and shop visits, with more than twice the time on wing, and by November 2025 new LEAP-1A engines performed at CFM56 levels. The LEAP-1B kit was in certification for 1H 2026, and its outcome is not in my evidence. Retrofit runs through shop visits: 'years, not months'. Its lineage is GE90/GEnx hot-section experience. - LEAP: as in the durability test. Its post-2026 state is not in my evidence. Where I look first (inference from the LEAP history, not a flag): the Ducted fleet in hot and harsh service, because that is where LEAP first fell short, through 'a small, handful of parts', in the Middle East and India. If a shortfall is found, the answer is the LEAP answer: root cause, inspection and a parts fix accepted by the airframer and the regulator, targeted or fleet-wide, with no redesign of the engine. Enhanced inspection of safety-critical parts stays in new-make shops and MRO. Any flag reaches the Board before orders. |
