- Decision rationale: DECISION: hold on every field. There is no Board item, $0 of new R&D, $0 of new strain and a premium of $0.

THE FRAME [CX-0467]. The narrowbody contest is decided. NGSA has been in service since 2037 with CFM, P&W and RR (code 7), and fps since 2040 with CFM alone (code 3). The Joint Venture engine has been in service since 2036. No code can move, no slot is open, and the brief has one column, S1. The game is the installed base we already hold [CX-0422]: LEAP on the 737 and A320neo, the Ducted on fps, NGSA and Embraer, and the GEnx on the 787. We win it on safety, quality, delivery and cost, in that order [CX-0658].

THE GRID (brief §3, S1 at weight 1.0, so the expected column is also the worst). Ghai reproduced every row.
- Hold everything: -38.29 (76.1%). This is the best row: engine value -32.29, R&D -4.00 and strain -2.00, the 2030 package, all booked.
- Open Fan: -68.85, or -30.56 against hold. That is the $8B bill plus $22.56B of 35 pp wait penalty from 2045.
- Open Fan + Lobby: -60.18, or -21.89 against hold. That is the $9B bill plus $12.89B of 20 pp penalty.
- Lobby beside an Open Fan: +8.67. Lobby alone: about -1.00 (not a grid row; it has no board effect on its own).
Nothing is positive against hold. Every alternative loses more than its own bill (3.8x and 2.4x).

CORRECTIONS TO MY FRAME, accepted:
- Ali: 2055 is 18 years after NGSA's entry and 15 after fps's, not 'eight and five'. That was my arithmetic slip. It never reached a statement or a move.
- Ghai: the penalty starts in 2045. He reproduces it within $0.03B on a 2045 start, and a 2046 start does not match. $0.645B per raw point and $0.678B per point of share are the same rate.
- Ghai: no new strain arises because the $2B is a one-time charge already in hold, not because it is 'one project'. Open Fan + Lobby would be two projects.
- Ghai: on renormalised shares, Dominate falls to 21.0% (2045) and 23.5% (2050) with the Open Fan alone, and to 35.2% and 37.8% with Lobby. My rough figures ran 1-2 points low; the conclusion stands.

MY TESTS.
- Additive and paid for the risk [CX-0301, CX-0609]. The new NBs are on the record, so S1 shows the launch's own effect, and it is all cost: the board gives the Open Fan no share gain. Nothing is ordered.
- H2. A 2045 launch is ready in 2055 at the earliest (launch + 10), 18 and 15 years after the two entries. Every route waits, so there is no launch. The RISE allowance cannot cover it: the cheapest route is $21.89B against $15B, $4.89B above the allowance plus the round cap and $2.89B above the allowance plus the full game cap.
- H3. The Ducted stands as the defence, triggered on the record and in service. It is never the next generation.
- H5. RISE continues whole; holding the Open Fan is not a cancel.
- My 20% and durability lines are unaffected. The Ducted is not called the next generation, and no durability margin is traded.
- Strain: no new project.
- Tie-break: not needed. The nearest alternative is $21.89B behind, far outside ε.

OBJECTIVE. Dominate is met (51.1% worst year, 2040; 54.3% in 2045; 56.8% in 2050). Introduce is unreachable. Launching an Open Fan would give up an objective we meet for one we can't reach. That is a net objective loss, not a premium.

LOW CASE FIRST [CX-0248, CX-0536]. I book -39.60: S1's -38.29 less $1.31B, the larger of the two exclusive widebody risks named by the Board and Ghai. Those risks are not put into any grid row or column. On Ghai's advice I go no lower: the narrowbody is locked, and booking below the low case misinforms too. I raise toward -38.29 only if the next bulletin shows no widebody move.

THE JOINT VENTURE LESSON. The record moved from -31.20 to -38.29, which is -$7.09B. The board took 20 raw points off us, not 15: the Joint Venture's 10 stacked on one solo-sized 10. That sits between Ghai's two readings (+$7.1B fold, -$14.2B stack), on the stacked side at half the size. My -56.00 booking covered it by $17.71B, so guiding low worked. But we named the risk and didn't book it, and we discounted a rival's stated sequencing. The lesson, adopted: a named risk with a negative midpoint gets its midpoint booked in the round we find it, and a rival's stated sequencing is not data.

SAFRAN GATE (H7). Hold orders no new CFM narrowbody move, so there is nothing to gate. RISE continues as a 50-50 decision [CX-0166]. An Open Fan with no airframer path would need a reason why Safran would agree, and there is none: a 35 pp loss cuts Safran's half of the same base. Lobby serves RISE, so consent is assumed, but the point is moot. Any CFM price, terms or capacity is joint [CX-0184], including any answer to the airframer's backstop rate request. GEnx is GE alone.

DOCTRINE, NOT MY RECORD, drove these parts:
- Lobby only beside a live Open Fan (no lobbying evidence in my record).
- Embraer held (my record is silent on it).
- No reaction to the Joint Venture (profile.md §5, §7).
- Supply and raw materials: Ali's supply record is my own description of his job, so I checked it against doctrine (OE first, spares never crowding out OE).

BIAS NAMED. The installed-base lens ('seed corn' [CX-0256], 'price of admission' [CX-0557]) pulls toward an Open Fan for the objective. But admission buys a seat, and both airframes are flying. There is no seat until an airframer wants one [CX-0469, CX-0205]. My ramp optimism [CX-0590, CX-0592] is checked by publishing no rate, and no capacity commitment on the backstop request without field data and Safran.

WHAT CHANGES MY MIND:
- A durability or safety flag. That means fleet action and a reserve in the round it is found; it lowers the booking, not the orders.
- A widebody move on the record. I re-price it in 2050.
- A firm airframer launch that wants RISE [CX-0469]. None is possible in the NB window.
Investors asking does not move me [CX-0444].

No veto stands. Ghai's binding vetoes and Ali's advice all fall on orders I don't make, so there are no overrides.
- Expected scenario: S1 (rivals hold), at weight 1.0. It is the brief's only column and the backlog on the record [CX-0248]. fps is on CFM (code 3, in service 2040) and NGSA on CFM, P&W and RR (code 7, in service 2037). The Joint Venture engine has been in service since 2036. Codes are locked, and the Delay Tactics window on fps closed at its 2040 entry.. Best grid plan there: Hold everything: -38.29 (76.1%) in S1, the only column. The alternatives against hold are Open Fan + Lobby -21.89 and Open Fan -30.56. My submitted plan is this plan.. Premium +0.00 $B: Premium this round: $0.00. Game to date: $0.00 of the $4.0B cap. The submitted plan is the best grid plan in the only column, so no doctrine or objective premium arises.

RISE allowance, stated apart: $0 of $15B, unused. It cannot be used this round. Every Open Fan route waits on an airframe already in service, so it fails the airframer-path test.

Objective premium declined for 'Introduce the Open Fan', against the best non-RISE plan (hold):
- $21.89B for the cheapest route (Open Fan + Lobby), which is $4.89B above the allowance plus the round cap and $2.89B above the allowance plus the full game cap;
- $30.56B for the Open Fan alone.
Neither buys anything toward the objective, since the engine is ready in 2055, after the 2050 test. Both would also break 'Dominate', so this is a net objective loss, not a premium.
- Predictions: COLUMN WEIGHTS: S1 1.0, the only column. Hold is -38.29 there. I book -39.60.

What I expect on the record: nothing moves on the narrowbody. The codes are locked, both airframes and the Joint Venture engine are in service, and our NB share follows the brief: 54.3% in 2045, rising to 56.8% from 2049. WB stays at 44.8% unless someone moves.

RISKS OUTSIDE THE COLUMN (named; in no grid row; only the largest sets the $1.31B guide-low cushion in my booking):
(a) Widebody, against GEnx's 44.8%. The two cases are exclusive.
- An RR Trent 1000 upgrade (+5 pp WB from 2048): about -$0.76B (Ghai) or -$0.8B (Board).
- An UltraFan WB re-engine (+10 pp once a re-engined 787 or A350 is in service): -$1.08B from a 2045 launch, -$1.22B from 2050 (Ghai), or about -$1.3B (Board).
The signals: Boeing will look at the 787's next step once fps is in service, and it now is; RR does no widebody spend without a Re-engine; Airbus's focus is the A350. I can't put a probability on it, because looking is not moving.
(b) A 787 or A350 re-engine shifting WB airframe share. Unpriced, and it could cut either way for GEnx.
(c) Ducted and Embraer in-service durability, hot and harsh service first. On LEAP, time-on-wing shortfalls became reserves in the quarter they were found. This lowers the booking, not the orders.
(d) Raw materials: titanium forgings and rare earths. Unquantified.
(e) The airframer's backstop rate call on the same supplier base. Any answer is joint with Safran, and OE comes first.
(f) Supplier-dependent volumes, and macro (recession, tariffs) that the board's fixed narrowbody market leaves out.

Upside is not booked. If the 2050 bulletin shows no widebody move, I raise toward -38.29, and not before.
- Expected ΔPV −39.60 $B; board after the round −38.29 $B

- Other moves: In-service safety and durability reporting on the Ducted, Embraer, LEAP and GEnx fleets, on field data (public); LEAP and Ducted OE engines and spares on time to every airframer (public); Answer to the airframer request for engine rate commitments with CFM as backstop: joint with Safran, on standard terms (private); Supplier and raw-materials review: titanium forgings and rare earths (private); RISE continues whole as technology, 50-50 with Safran (public); Private CFM offer to every airframer on standard terms, with Safran (private); No reaction to rival or Joint Venture news (private); GEnx 787 fleet support, GE alone; no move ahead of the airframer on the 787's next step (private)

| Standing 2050 test (not engaged this round) | Any 2050 engine order must be ready by the EIS of a live airframe, not yet in service, that could select P&W. | not applicable | No such airframe exists today: NGSA has been in service since 2037 and fps since 2040. A 2050 launch would be ready in 2056, the last NB year the board counts. My default proposal for 2050 is hold. |

- Decision rationale: THE DECISION: GTF2 Solo hold, Joint Venture with RR hold. premium 0. predictions S1 1.00. expected ΔPV -2.93. overrides empty. No Board item.

1. START FROM EDDY'S PROPOSAL. Eddy proposed hold/hold. It is the only row in brief §3.
- A cancel is gone: GTF2 folded into the Joint Venture in 2035 and has been ready since 2036, and the rules allow a cancel only until an engine is ready.
- A withdraw is gone: the Joint Venture formed in 2035, and the rules allow a withdraw only before formation.
- launch and launch if selected are not offered, and no live airframe is outside service (NGSA since 2037, fps since 2040).
I adopt the proposal unchanged.

2. STRIKE WHAT FAILS A BINDING VETO.
- Mitchill invoked one binding veto, on ground 3 (aggressive terms): any share-recovery or pricing campaign to close the Capitalise gap. I had already struck it in my frame, and it stays struck. It also fails my own rule, value not share [PX-0055][PX-0100]. The board does not price it, and it cannot reach the gap, which comes from the airframe split.
- Mitchill's ground 2 (on a 2050 launch) and ground 4 (on the final text) are standing conditions, not invoked. I checked the final statement against ground 4. It carries no share forecast, no dated recovery, no durability claim, no partner money, no look-back figure and no programme cost figure.
- Eddy invoked neither ground. Ground 1 passes: no order puts an engine on an airframe or compresses a programme, and the engine was ready in 2036 for NGSA's 2037 entry into service. Ground 2 passes: the brief shows no live fleet, quality or supply problem.
- No colleague flagged a company red-line breach.

3. CONSTRAINT FIRST, THEN OPTIMISE [PX-0035].
- The safety and durability constraint now applies to the fleet, not to a launch. We claim no durability that the fleet record does not show, and the brief holds no record. Any finding goes to Governance and Public Policy and the full Board before the 2050 orders.
- Inside the constraint there is one row, so the optimum is hold/hold: -2.93 in S1, worst column -2.93, nothing against hold.

4. TIE-BREAKS.
- The fleet beats a new engine [PX-0080], so I adopt Eddy's order of call. Steps 2 to 4 are weighed jointly with Airbus [PX-0099].
- 'Miss a cycle' [PX-0071] asks for nothing. The cycle was answered in 2030 and 2035, and no live airframe can still select us.
- A rival's discount never moves the answer.

5. PREMIUM AND OBJECTIVE.
- premium 0. No row differs from hold, so no doctrine or objective premium can arise, and the $1B cap is unused.
- Restore credibility: MET. The Joint Venture engine has been in service on NGSA, a new airframe, since 2037.
- Capitalise: NOT MET at -2.93. No order on the grid reaches it, and I will not chase it with price or terms.
- PV given up for either: $0.

6. ANNUAL PROGRAMME NPV REVIEW (Mitchill's numbers, which I trust over my own operating dates).
- ΔPV -2.93 = engine value -0.93 + R&D -2.00. The R&D is undiscounted, booked once in 2035, with no fresh share and no write-off since.
- Engine value against the status quo: small gains in 2038-40 (7.6 against 7.5, 7.9 against 7.6, 7.9 against 7.8), worth +0.15 PV. Losses from 2043 (8.1 against 8.3), reaching -1.0 a year by 2049-53, worth -1.12 PV. Net -0.97 on rounded figures against -0.93.
- The drift tracks Airbus's NB share at 0.317 pp per point. That attribution stays private; in public we speak for no airframer [PX-0062].
- Look-backs, inference and private. About +24.8 against being left off NGSA, which is the top of the range, not a point estimate. +5.51 for the 2035 commit, for $0 of new R&D.
- Answer to the shareowner: the $2B defended the franchise, and the residual gap is the airframe split.

7. WHAT CHANGED FROM MY FRAME. Two wording fixes to the statement, both advice and both taken:
- Eddy's and Mitchill's: 'our Joint Venture engine with Rolls-Royce, which carries GTF2's design', in place of 'the engine we developed with Rolls-Royce'. It keeps point (c) exact while the derivative suits are open [PG-0039].
- Mitchill's: 'formed on the terms we published' is printed only now that Eddy has confirmed points (a) to (c).
I also added the conditional, dated offer for any future engine: after an airframer's selection, ready six years from launch, durability proven before entry into service.

8. BIASES NAMED.
- Optimism on operating dates: the statement and the fleet report carry no dated recovery.
- Architecture loyalty: the airframe-split reading is supported by the bulletin, but we hold no fleet data, so I do not use it to say the fleet is fine.
- Partner discipline: Rolls-Royce is now a partner under strain, and its technology is protected on the same terms as ours [PX-0038].

9. RECORD VERSUS DOCTRINE.
- My own record grounds fleet first, value not share, the capital order and fast disclosure.
- The Joint Venture close-out and the derivative rule rest on company doctrine [P-1233][P-1240][P-1563] and on Eddy's engineering, not on my record (Low).

10. 2050. The standing rule is in other_moves. A 2050 unconditional launch would be ready in 2056 and fails Mitchill's ground 2 and the Board's tests. My 2050 default is hold unless a live airframe not yet in service selects P&W.
- Expected scenario: S1 (rivals hold), the brief's only column, probability 1.00.. Best grid plan there: GTF2: hold (GTF2 Solo hold, Joint Venture with RR hold) at -2.93 $B (97.3%) in S1. It is the only row on the grid.. Premium +0.00 $B: No row differs from hold/hold, the brief's only legal row, so no doctrine or objective premium can arise. No PV is given up against the best expected PV, and the $1B team cap and the Board's $0.5B limit are both unused. The Capitalise gap (-2.93) is not chased with spend, discounts or terms. No grid order reaches it, and a pricing campaign fails Mitchill's ground 3 and my own value-not-share rule [PX-0055].
- Predictions: S1 (rivals hold): 1.00. It is the brief's only column. Expected column: S1. Expected PV of hold/hold: -2.93 $B.
- Expected ΔPV −2.93 $B; board after the round −2.93 $B

- Other moves: GTF durability kit (Hot Section) for the installed fleet (public); Fleet report to the Board each round (replaces the GTF2 readiness report) (private); Order of call on pacing parts, shops and engineers (private); Joint Venture close-out note to the Board, and the derivative rule (private); Annual programme NPV review of GTF2 and the Joint Venture (Mitchill) (private); Capital order report (private); Standing 2050 rule (private)

For an engine in service, support scales with the fleet (advice). | pass | The Joint Venture engine's support was carried in the 2035 orders, before NGSA's 2037 EIS: partner-shop agreements on our Singapore model and the castings, forgings and gearbox supply contracts placed in 2035; the spares pool and field service stood up in 2036; the EIS-readiness review in 2036. It carried over to the Joint Venture unchanged and does not count on P&W's shops, because the GTF fleet keeps first call on them. No new narrowbody EIS lies ahead, so ground (c) has nothing to bite on this round. The in-service gap is the gear and core. Under P&W's design authority and with no technology licence, our partner shops cannot repair that module without P&W's repair data. Without that data, the module goes to P&W's shops, where the GTF fleet has first call (inference). Plan: (1) repair data, or a gear and core module-exchange pool, under the shared-risk terms; (2) a spares pool sized to the fleet: 914 RR-share engines in 2045, about 8,600 delivered 2037-2045; (3) field service near NGSA operators; (4) RR's own tracking of time on wing and life-limited parts, reported to the Board each round. Widebody options: the upgrade would be retrofitted at shop visits through the Trent network and partner shops. UltraFan WB would need its support built before 2051; that is not a ground now, because it is not ordered. |
| Engineering load | A narrowbody project beside a widebody project costs $5B of strain; flag the years of overlap. Red lines: no UltraFan WB beside an UltraFan NB Solo unless both are selected; no upgrade while an UltraFan is in development (seat and company rule, inference). | pass | All four at hold: no widebody project, strain $0, 0 years of overlap. Confirmed: the board treats the Joint Venture as a running narrowbody project even with its engine in service. The grid splits exactly: - upgrade: -2 bill - 5 strain + 0.52 = -6.48 (S1); + 0.54 = -6.46 (S2); - UltraFan WB: -4 - 5 + 0 = -9.00 (S1); + 0.98 = -8.02 (S2). Overlap if ordered: the upgrade 2045-48 (3 years) and UltraFan WB 2045-51 (6 years), each beside the Joint Venture. The $5B is charged once either way. The Joint Venture runs to the board's end (narrowbody is counted to 2056), so I expect the strain on any widebody item in 2050 too (inference; the 2050 grid will confirm). Red lines as written are not engaged: no UltraFan is in development and no NB Solo is live, so neither option would be a red-line breach. The strain is the cost. My 2035 plan (bring the upgrade back in 2045, without strain) assumed the strain would lift. It does not, so I withdraw it. The 2035 fold question is moot: the Joint Venture formed in 2035. |
| Capacity and suppliers | State RR's engines a year from the brief. The board has no capacity limit, so the result is not applicable unless the brief reports a constraint. Partner MRO and supplier needs are advice. | not applicable | Brief, on hold: 914 narrowbody + 188 widebody = 1,102 engines in 2045; 863 + 188 = 1,051 a year from 2049; 990 + 197 = 1,187 at the 2039 peak. Narrowbody is 4.9 times widebody in 2045 and 4.6 times from 2049. RR-share narrowbody engines delivered: about 8,600 by the end of 2045 and about 13,000 by 2050. No constraint is reported. Advice: partner shops on the Singapore model for the narrowbody shop-visit load; high-performing suppliers for castings, forgings and life-limited parts; a gear and core module pool. Shop visits: I use my CMD figure of 1,100-1,200 a year (widebody era), not Helen's 1,400-1,500; the two were never reconciled. The narrowbody fleet adds a load that neither figure covers and the board does not model. |
| Widebody dominance | RR widebody engine share of at least 50% in 2040, 2045 and 2050. Time on wing is the lever (inference). | pass | S1 on hold: 58.0% in 2040, 55.2% in 2045 and 55.2% in 2050. The worst year is 55.2%, 5.2 pp of headroom. S2: the brief gives no shares by year. S2 moves only the widebody airframes, the widebody engine market is fixed at 340 a year, and the hold row is +0.25 above S1. So RR's widebody value is at least as high, and its share at or above S1's 55.2%, above the 50% line (inference). I confirm the CEO's reading. The GEnx upgrade's 2.8 pp is 197 to 188 engines, 9 a year, worth about $0.53B of PV at 2026 by my arithmetic on the dashboard economics (inference). It is already in the hold row. The upgrade would win back about 17 engines a year from 2048 for +0.52, at $7B of bill and strain. Time on wing is worth $0 on this grid, because it moves no share on the board. Off the grid it keeps the Trent fleet flying and earning, and it is what wins the next campaign (inference). A further CFM widebody move the size of the GEnx upgrade would leave about 52.4%, still above 50% (inference). |
| Technology pull-through | An idle or folded UltraFan still feeds the Trent fleet. Noted qualitatively only; no grid value is adjusted. | not applicable | UltraFan NB folded into the Joint Venture in 2035. Its technology is protected under our terms and keeps feeding the Trent fleet for time on wing, with public R&D money where available. It is no argument for any widebody launch. No grid value is changed. |

- Decision rationale: So, frankly, nothing on this grid adds cash, and we buy nothing. All four fields stay at hold. No Board item. Premium $0B.

1. Proposal. My framing lean was all four at hold. Both memos recommend the same orders.

2. Grid, each item against 'UltraFan NB: hold' (S1 / S2):
- Hold row (the submitted orders): +20.55 / +20.80. Best in both columns. Worst column +20.55.
- 'UltraFan NB: hold + T1000 upgrade': +14.07 / +14.34. Item -6.48 / -6.46, which is $2B of bill + $5B of strain against +0.52 / +0.54 of engine value. Without strain it would still be about -1.48.
- 'UltraFan NB: hold + UltraFan WB': +11.55 / +12.78. Item -9.00 / -8.02, which is $4B + $5B against 0 / +0.98 of engine value. Ready in 2051 (2045 + 6), a year after S2's 2050 Re-engines enter service.
- The upgrade and UltraFan WB are exclusive, so no combined row.

3. Weights and expected scenario. S1 0.90 / S2 0.10, confirmed by McCabe. S2 needs both Re-engines and can be no likelier than its weaker leg. Boeing's '787 next step once this airplane is in service' is a signal (0.2). Airbus's 'the A350 family, including the A350F, remains our focus' is nothing (0.1).
- Expected scenario: S1, rivals hold.
- Hold EV: +20.58 (+20.59 on the Board's 0.85 / 0.15; +20.56 on 0.95 / 0.05).
- Upgrade item EV: -6.48 under any weights; row EV +14.10.
- UltraFan WB item EV: -8.90 (-8.85 on the Board's case); row EV +11.67.
- No sign changes under any weights: both widebody items are negative in both columns.

4. Binding vetoes.
- McCabe withholds her co-signature on `Trent 1000 upgrade: launch` and `UltraFan WB: launch`, on hurdle and unselected downside. Both are binding. Both are respected: neither is ordered.
- She invokes no balance-sheet veto. All-hold needs no funding. No narrowbody R&D remains: our $4.00B Joint Venture share and the $1.71B write-off were booked in 2035. We hold net cash, and leverage stays at or below about 1.5x.
- Watson invokes no Civil veto. His two conditions are met in this package. (a) The statement and the public other moves carry no new-engine date, no Joint Venture date and no date for any next build standard. (b) The in-service support plan and his report to the Board are carried as private other moves.
- `overrides` is empty.

5. Doctrine-favoured orders and premium.
- My record favours the Trent 1000 upgrade: 'do we believe we can regain market share? Absolutely' and 787 share 'Why not?' [RX-0101, RX-0117]. My rule keeps it only as a declared premium within $3B. Its shortfall is $6.48B, beyond the cap and over three times its own bill. Struck. Not at any price: we are not after market share [RX-0054, R-1582].
- UltraFan WB fails airframers first [RX-0058]. No A350 Re-engine is on the record, and Boeing has named no UltraFan on a 787 Re-engine. Its $8.90B shortfall is far above both the $1B sub-cap and the $3B cap. Struck.
- Premium: $0B. The chosen row is the best row in both columns.

6. Red lines.
- No colleague flags a breach on the final orders.
- Watson notes that the red lines as written (no upgrade while an UltraFan is in development; no UltraFan WB beside an NB Solo) are not engaged by either widebody option, because no UltraFan is in development and no Solo is live. I accept that. On this board the strain is the economic form of 'one big programme at a time' [R-0929, R-1127]. It is priced in the grid, not deducted again.
- No item may lose more than its own bill in a plausible column. Both widebody items fail that test in both columns, so both stay at hold. That test is Board doctrine [RG-0033], not my record.
- Nothing is cancelled: no programme is in development and no cancel is offered.

7. Housekeeping, plainly.
- The 2045 withdrawal review is moot and closed: the Joint Venture formed in 2035.
- The 2035 widebody plan assumed the strain would lift once UltraFan NB left development. It did not: on this board the formed Joint Venture is a running narrowbody project. That assumption was wrong, and I say so.
- What the miss costs: without strain the upgrade would read about -1.48, a premium we could have chosen to declare inside $3B. With strain it is off the table.
- What the Joint Venture brought: 24.1% of NGSA engines from entry, both legs of the objective met, and the $2.29B of Solo spend not needed in 2036-37.

8. Off-grid, as the Board asked.
- Watson: the gearbox that flies is P&W's, under P&W's design authority, on GTF2's clock. It was ready in 2036 with no slip, RR was fitted at NGSA's entry (965 engines, 24.1%), and there is no durability or safety flag on the record. The board does not model time on wing or full-life parts, so he gives no number, and neither do I.
- His oversight note becomes a private move: RR technical review rights on P&W's test, certification and fleet data, and gear-and-core repair data or a module pool, so that our partner shops can support that module without P&W's shops.
- McCabe sizes our share of a reasonable-worst-case in-service charge at about £1.2-2.9bn, with a peak year of £0.3-0.7bn. Twice that still fits without equity, without leverage above about 1.5x and with the dividend untouched; extra distributions flex first. It books nothing.

9. Where my record is thin. Joint Venture terms and P&W are Low confidence for me (erginbilgic.md §6). On the gear and core, support and shop capacity I defer to Watson. On the shared-risk sizing I take McCabe's test as the evidence. The in-service oversight and the worst-case sizing are company doctrine and Board asks (profile.md §4; RG-0027, RG-0033), not my own words.

'Actually, I've seen this movie before': two programmes in parallel, and the strain eats the return. We control what we control: the fleet, durability and cash.
- Expected scenario: S1: rivals hold (weight 0.90). No 787 or A350 Re-engine this round. Boeing has only signalled a look at the 787's next step once fps is in service, and Airbus keeps the A350 family as its focus. S2 (both Re-engines) is weighted 0.10, capped by its weaker leg.. Best grid plan there: 'UltraFan NB: hold' (all four fields at hold): +20.55 in S1, and also the best row in S2 at +20.80. EV +20.58 on 0.90 / 0.10 (+20.59 on the Board's 0.85 / 0.15). It beats 'UltraFan NB: hold + T1000 upgrade' (+14.07 / +14.34) by 6.48 / 6.46, and 'UltraFan NB: hold + UltraFan WB' (+11.55 / +12.78) by 9.00 / 8.02.. Premium +0.00 $B: None. The submitted orders are the best grid row in both columns, so no doctrine-favoured order is kept against PV. Item by item: `UltraFan NB Solo` hold, $0; `Joint Venture with P&W` hold, $0; `UltraFan WB` hold, $0; `Trent 1000 upgrade` hold, $0. For the record, choosing the upgrade would have cost $6.48B under any weights, and UltraFan WB $8.90B ($8.85B on the Board's case). Both are above the $3B cap, and UltraFan WB is also above its $1B sub-cap, so neither could be carried as a declared premium.
- Predictions: Weights: S1 0.90, S2 0.10.
- No 787 Re-engine this round (0.80). Boeing has only said it will look at the 787's next step once fps is in service, and has named no engine.
- No A350 Re-engine (0.90). Airbus says the A350 family, including the A350F, remains its focus.
- Both Re-engines together (S2): 0.10 at most.
- CFM/GE holds. The ducted engine and Embraer stay in service, RISE stays as technology, and the GEnx upgrade keeps its 2.8 pp of widebody.
- P&W stays in the Joint Venture on its published terms. The GTF fleet keeps first call on its shops.
- The Joint Venture engine stays in service with no durability or safety flag on the record. Watson's in-service report reaches the Board before the 2050 orders.
- RR on hold: narrowbody share 21.6% from 2049; widebody 55.2% in 2045 and 2050. Both legs of the objective are met.
- The $5B strain stays on any widebody item in 2050, to be confirmed from that grid.
Expected ΔPV of the chosen orders: +20.58 $B on my weights (+20.59 on the Board's case). S1 outcome +20.55; S2 outcome +20.80.
- Expected ΔPV +20.58 $B; board after the round +20.55 $B

- Other moves: Joint Venture engine in service: safety, durability and support first (public); Trent fleet durability (time on wing) continues as funded (public); UltraFan technology continues as technology (public); Joint Venture in-service support plan (Watson) (private); Technical review rights and in-service reporting to the Board (private); Off-grid shared-risk sizing on the Joint Venture engine (McCabe) (private); Capital frame held: no new programme spend (private); Pricing discipline (private); Housekeeping: two 2035 plans formally closed (private); 2050 widebody watch (private)


## Round 4: decision year 2050

**2050: everyone holds again, and the final state is the 2035 state**

Every company held, and no value has changed since 2035. A Re-engine ordered in 2050 would enter service only in 2055. Airbus's A350 Re-engine was worth +$0.57B if Boeing held and −$0.42B if not; Boeing's 787 Re-engine +$0.04B or −$1.00B.

### Orders (as adjudicated)

| Player | Orders | Public statement |
|---|---|---|
| Boeing | Do Nothing | Our new single-aisle airplane has been in service for ten years now. It came in on the date we gave you. The line is at 940 airplanes a year, and we got there one step at a time: on our KPIs, agreed with the FAA, and only after the line had shown it was stable at the rate it was on. If we take another step, it works the same way. We said we wanted to hold half of the single-aisle market. We're at 47%. That's up from 38% when the airplane came into service, and it's still short of half. I won't dress it up. We earn every point one delivery at a time, and that includes supporting the airplanes our customers already fly. We're not launching a new program today. We've looked hard at the 787's next step again. It's a strong airplane, and it has room to grow from our Charleston capacity. We'll take that next step when the market, the technology and we are ready, and today all three aren't there. So I'm not going to give you a date for something we haven't launched. Paying down debt is still far and away our priority. We fund the business from our operations, with no new equity, and I'm not going to put a year on the rating before the data supports it. You'll see it in the numbers. Some things don't change. We certify before we build at rate. We won't take on fixed-price development. We keep our suppliers funded and healthy for the rates in front of us, and we keep working with CFM, our engine partner, on time on wing and support for the fleet. A while back I said we were turning this big ship, but it hadn't turned yet. We've come a long way since then. There's still work to do, and we'll keep doing it the same way: do less, do it better, and let our own readiness set the pace. The financials will follow our production performance. |
| Airbus | Do Nothing | Our core principles of safety, quality, integrity, compliance and security will remain at the heart of all we do. Our next-generation single-aisle aircraft has been in service since the second half of the 2030s, with engines from CFM, Pratt & Whitney and Rolls-Royce. Our first task now is the fleet in service. We put fleet safety and operational integrity above all, and with our engine partners we follow how these engines perform in service, on field data. We continue production in a disciplined and controlled way, alongside the A320neo family. The people and suppliers who deliver both remain protected. On the widebody side, the A350 family, including the A350F, remains our focus. We continue to study the options for its future on our own technology and industrial clock, and we will take decisions when the technology and the case are mature, and not before. We also continue to prepare the technologies for the generations of aircraft that will follow. We continue to strengthen a more sovereign European supply chain, with long-term agreements sized to our own programmes, and we keep recruiting the engineers who will build the next generations of Airbus aircraft. We fund this within our financial framework: disciplined investment in preparing our future portfolio, and shareholder returns while preserving financial flexibility. On trade, we keep working with industry partners on both sides of the Atlantic for a zero-for-zero tariff regime in civil aerospace. |
| CFM/GE | Do Nothing | Safety, quality, delivery and cost, always in that order. That's how we started this, and it's how we'll finish it. So let me tell you what we know and what we don't. What we know: there's a CFM engine on both new single-aisle airplanes. The ducted engine CFM and Safran launched in 2030 was ready in 2036. It has flown on NGSA since 2037 and on fps since 2040. Our work with Embraer has been in service since 2037, and the upgraded GEnx on the 787 since 2033. Those are dates on the record, not on slides. We still don't have a birthright on the next order. We earn it by delivering: LEAP and the ducted engine, and the spares behind them, on time to every customer we serve. Spares don't crowd out new engines. What we don't know yet is how the newest of these engines hold up over the long haul, especially in the hot and harsh places where engines work hardest. We're not raising a flag today. That isn't a claim that the job is done. The field data decides that, not us. So we measure time on wing on the fleet, in cycles, against the yardstick our customers know, and we share it with our airframers and regulators through one team, with Safran's engineers alongside. When a customer's safety review looks hard at our engines, we welcome it. Where we find something, we fix it, with Safran, the airframers and the regulators. We don't trade durability for delivery. The ducted engine is the right engine for the airplanes in front of us. It isn't our next-generation engine. That is still RISE. We're all in on that technology with Safran, 50-50, and we're keeping it whole. When that architecture enters service is really not for us to say. The airframers and their airline customers will decide that. We're not starting anything new this year. We plan on the low case and keep the balance sheet and our capital returns whole. We size our own supply base to our own needs, and we keep the shop and inspection capacity our fleet relies on. Anything on CFM capacity or terms, we work through together with Safran, and with our customers in private. You won't see us pointing fingers. You'll see us solving problems. We get paid in dollars, not in percentages. It's that simple, ain't that hard. |
| Pratt & Whitney | Do Nothing | Let me start with what is not where we want it. Our share of narrowbody engine deliveries was 21.6% in 2049, down from 24.8% in 2039. That is not where we want to be, and we will not buy it back with price. We earn it with time on wing, and with engines delivered when we promised them. What is done: our Joint Venture engine with Rolls-Royce, which carries GTF2's design, has been ready since 2036 and in service on NGSA since 2037. The Joint Venture formed in 2035 on the terms we published. Pratt & Whitney's gear and core remain our own module, with no technology licence, and each partner's technology is protected. Control what you can control. The fleet is where this engine earns its place, and the fleet record is how we will be judged: time on wing, life-limited-part life and shop visits. We will report what that record shows, measured on the fleet, not what we hope it shows, and we give no dated recovery. If we find a durability or safety issue, we will say so early, with the plan. The GTF fleet, the new engine fleet and A320neo engine deliveries keep first call on our parts, our shops and our engineers. We have learned our lesson from the prior iterations of the GTF. The GTF Hot Section durability kit stays on offer at shop visits, sold on standard terms. There are no launch-style discounts and no free upgrades. We're not in the early entry phase, the launch phase. We are not starting a new engine programme today. Any new engine we commit will follow an airframer's selection. It will be ready not before six years from launch, and it will enter service only once durability is proven. Our capital priorities have not changed: the dividend first, then debt, then share repurchase. We will not speak for any airframer's plans, dates, rates or engine choices. |
| Rolls-Royce | Do Nothing | So, our narrowbody engine with Pratt & Whitney is in service, and the fleet is our job. Safety is at the top. Then time on wing, full-life parts and support through our partner shops. If we find an issue, we will say so early, with the plan. We do not want to underestimate the risks. Widebody is our franchise. Durability work on the Trent fleet goes on, and there is no new widebody engine spend without a Re-engine. UltraFan technology continues as technology. We are not starting a new programme this year. Cash goes first to the balance sheet, then to the dividend, then to distributions from sustainable free cash flow. We are not after market share. We won't do anything not profitable. I will say that categorically. |

### How each company decided (private during the game)

**Boeing** (Kelly Ortberg, Jay Malave, Stephanie Pope; Boeing Board)

| Step | What happened |
|---|---|
| Board guidance | Boeing Board: risk appetite: 4.5 of 5, unchanged in our tests for this final round. On Malave's conservative case, debt is not back to $45B until 2053, so we do not yet ease toward 4 (inference). The tests stand: a launch must clear +$1B in the expected column, and no Board item may fall … |
| Frame | Kelly Ortberg: initial lean: **Orders I lean to: Do Nothing.** - fps: hold - fps engine code: null - 737 rate: hold - 787 Re-engine: hold Row: +0.66 / +0.57. Every order is a hold, so no item goes to the Board. I'll still answer every one of its recommendations in board_response, including those … |
| Test (CFO) | Jay Malave recommends: Do Nothing. Options failing a test: Full cost of a slip: one year of certification slip on plan B, for scale (ask 3); Re-engine against the Board's business-case bar (advice to the Board). Vetoes: B: fps hold + 787 Re-engine (row 'fps: hold + 787 Re-engine', +0.71 / -0.43) (not binding) |
| Test (operating head) | Stephanie Pope recommends: Do Nothing. Options failing a test: On-time, predictable delivery: 787 Re-engine with EIS 2055, row B (record [BX-1331] by inference; doctrine slip prior); Technology readiness for a 2055 EIS (doctrine; CEO ask 6; advice, not a veto ground, not priced on the board). Vetoes: 737 rate: increase (any combination) (not binding); fps: hold + 787 Re-engine: launch (row B) (not binding) |
| Decide | Kelly Ortberg: Do Nothing |
| Veto check (CFO) | Jay Malave concurs |
| Veto check (operating head) | Stephanie Pope concurs |
| Board review | Boeing Board: not called; every order is the default (a hold is never a Board item) |

CFO tests:

| Test | Threshold | Result | Numbers |
|---|---|---|---|
| Buffer test (slip leg), my binding veto (ask 1) | In the column the brief labels 'Risk case', the plan may sit no more than $2B below Do Nothing in the same column. With no risk-case column, the test is not applicable and I hold no veto this round (inference: threshold and no-column case). | not applicable | The round-4 grid (round4.md §3) has two columns: S1, rivals hold, and S2, Airbus launches an A350 Re-engine. Neither is labelled 'Risk case'. The fps has been in service since 2040, so it has no slip leg. My veto has nothing to bind this round, and no row draws a binding veto. Confirmed as the CEO stated. |
| Buffer proxy on S2, the Board's risk case: plan A, Do Nothing (advice; Board's Rating-first reading) (ask 1) | S2 no lower than Do Nothing's S2 less $2B: 0.57 - 2.00 = -1.43. | pass | Row 'fps: hold + 787: hold', S2 = +0.57, which is 2.00 above the -1.43 floor. Full row: +0.66 / +0.57 (S1 / S2). Its worst column is S2, +0.57. Confirmed. |
| Buffer proxy on S2: plan B, fps hold + 787 Re-engine (advice) (ask 1) | S2 at or above -1.43. | pass | Row 'fps: hold + 787 Re-engine', S2 = -0.43, which is 1.00 above the -1.43 floor. Full row: +0.71 / -0.43. Item against A: +0.05 / -1.00. Its worst column is 1.00 below Do Nothing's. That is advice under Meet and beat, not a veto. Break-even chance of S2: 0.05 / (0.05 + 1.00) = 4.76%, which is 4.8%. Confirmed. |
| Meet and beat: worst-column advice (expected column S1 on Ortberg's provisional read; I have no Airbus evidence and defer to his frame) (ask 1) | Among plans within ε ($1B) in the expected column, prefer the one with the higher worst column (inference). | pass | In S1, A scores +0.66 and B +0.71: 0.05 apart, well inside ε. Worst columns: A +0.57 (S2), B -0.43 (S2). The rule prefers A by 1.00. If S2 is expected, A leads outright by 1.00 (+0.57 against -0.43). A wins in either column, so the orders do not depend on the Airbus call. B fails the rule. |
| Independent estimate check: grid tie-out to my round-3 build of a 2050 launch (ask 1) | The grid governs. A rebuild that ties out to rounding confirms the estimate, and any residual is reported, not netted (inference). | pass | Inputs, from the brief and dashboard_game.md §2: - 787 at $0.18B: 2026 revenue of 56.4, less 800 x $48M, over 100 airplanes. - Margin 20% today, 25.21% for a lone Re-engine and 15.77% if both re-engine. - +1 pp a year from EIS 2055, over the window 2055-74. S1: - operating gain +0.758 PV; - bill -0.276; - penalty -0.439; - item +0.042, against the grid's +0.05. That ties out to rounding and to my round-3 estimate of about +0.04 (+0.76 / -0.28 / -0.44). Confirmed. S2: - margin squeeze of -0.761 a year nominal over 2055-74, -0.383 PV; - less the bill and the penalty; - less Do Nothing's own S2 loss. An A350 Re-engine alone from 2055, counted to 2061 on the board's Re-engine snapshot window, comes to -0.097 against the grid's -0.09. Item -1.001, row -0.431, against the grid's -1.00 and -0.43. The estimate holds. |
| Balance sheet first: debt penalty of B (ask 2) | The grid's ΔPV already nets each plan's discounted penalty (α 0.30 x debt x (debt/EV)^2), so it is never subtracted again. Between plans within ε in the expected column, prefer the one that adds the smaller penalty (inference: threshold). | pass | A carries only the fps penalty: - 0.30 x 105.32 x (105.32/130)^2 - 1.62 = $19.12B undiscounted at 2040; - -4.73 PV in the brief, which my figure of 4.725 ties. B: - +$3.10B undiscounted, taking debt from $105.32B to $110.32B and debt/EV from 81.0% to 84.9%. Confirmed. - A Re-engine alone would add $0.60B. Confirmed. - One wording correction: the $3.10B sits on top of $105.32B of debt, not on the penalty. The fps's own penalty is $19.12B. On the grid's pro-rata allocation, B's penalty is 0.439 PV: - $1.40B re-allocated to the 2040 book, 0.345 PV; - $1.70B booked at EIS 2055, 0.094 PV. The CEO's 0.44 is confirmed. All of it is inside B's +0.05 / -1.00 and is never subtracted again. Within ε in S1, A adds the smaller penalty (zero), so A is preferred. |
| Cash path of each bill (ask 2) | When each bill lands, and whether two land together [BX-0547]. Real spend is set against operating profit (inference). | pass | A has no new bill. The fps's $60.32B was booked at EIS 2040 (brief 2040 non-recurring 60.3). B: - One bill, $5B at EIS 2055, the only bill that year. No two bills land together. - Real spend is about $1.0B a year over 2050-54. That is 5.9% of the 16.9 a year of operating profit, and 9.8% of the 10.2 a year of uplift over the status quo. - Fundable from operations, with no new equity. Confirmed. Does B move the investment-grade year? On my conservative case (half the uplift to debt, 5.1 a year), debt is: / Year-end / A (Do Nothing) / B (Re-engine) / /---/---:/---:/ / 2049 / $60.87B / $60.87B / / 2050 / $55.77B / $56.77B / / 2051 / $50.67B / $52.67B / / 2052 / $45.57B / $48.57B / / 2053 / $40.47B / $44.47B / - Both reach $45B during 2053, so B does not move the year. - B cuts the room at end-2053 from $4.53B to $0.53B. - B also gives up the 2052 upside: A misses 2052 by only $0.57B. - What breaks 2053 with B: about $1.06B of operating-profit shortfall over 2050-53, roughly $0.27B a year or 1.6% of 16.9, or more than $0.5B of Re-engine overrun spent before end-2053. Funding is not the ground for holding. The return is. |
| Close-out funding report to the Finance Committee (ask 4; the Board's recommendation 2) | Debt is the first call on cash, with no new equity. Solidly investment grade means debt back to the board's pre-launch $45B, 35% of the $130B EV (inference: threshold). Publish the conservative year and hold the upside. - Decision rationale: **Decision: Do Nothing.**
- Orders: fps hold, fps engine code null, 737 rate hold, 787 Re-engine hold.
- Row: +0.66 / +0.57 (S1 / S2).
- Every order is the default, so no item goes to the Board.

**1. The team rule, applied.**
- **Malave's buffer test: not applicable.** The brief labels no column 'Risk case', and the fps has been in service since 2040, so it has no slip leg. He confirms he holds no veto, and no plan is out on this ground.
- **The Board's S2 proxy** (floor 0.57 - 2.00 = -1.43): A passes by 2.00 and B by 1.00.
- **KPI doctrine (Pope's seat): not in play.** 737 rate is offered only at hold, and the brief reports no live quality, FAA, certification or supply-chain problem. She checked her veto and did not invoke it.
- Neither colleague flagged a red line. I override nothing. The rest is my call.

**2. The grid** (round4.md §3):
- A, Do Nothing: +0.66 / +0.57.
- B, fps hold + 787 Re-engine: +0.71 / -0.43.
- B's item is +0.05 / -1.00. Holding is the better call whenever the chance of S2 is above 4.8% (0.05 / 1.05).
- Malave's independent rebuild gives +0.042 / -1.001 (operating gain +0.758, bill -0.276, penalty -0.439). That ties to the grid and to his round-3 estimate of about +0.04. Pope's rebuild of the operating gain ties as well.

**3. My tests.**
- **KPI stability: pass.** No live problem in the brief, and no dip in the numbers: 860 to 940 over 2045-49, exactly on the path. Delivery counts are not KPIs, so the record comes from the Chief Engineer and the Chief Aerospace Safety Officer [BX-1271, BX-1259].
- **Three work streams: not converged** [BX-1296].
  - *Market:* S2 is plausible. If both re-engined airplanes enter service in 2055, share doesn't move and our 787 margin falls from 20% to 15.77%, about $0.76B a year.
  - *Technology:* Pope fails it, as advice. The upgraded GEnx has been the 787's engine since 2033. A GEnx derivative has no durability record. UltraFan WB launched now would be ready in 2056, after a 2055 EIS. RISE has no product date. My technology risk appetite is Low [BX-1298]. This isn't priced on the board and it isn't why I hold, but it means that work stream isn't there.
  - *Boeing:* fundable. About $1.0B a year is 5.9% of operating profit, with no new equity. Funding isn't the issue; the return is.
- **Debt before the next airplane: B fails.** A second development has to beat Do Nothing by more than ε in the expected column, and B does it in neither column [BX-1305, B-2326].
  - B adds +$3.10B of undiscounted penalty on top of $105.32B of debt (I accept Malave's wording fix). That's about 0.44 PV, already inside the grid and never subtracted again. For scale, the fps 10-year's own undiscounted penalty is $19.1B.
  - B would keep the conservative investment-grade year at 2053, but it cuts the room from $4.5B to $0.5B and gives up the 2052 upside.
- **FAA process: pass.** No certification trouble is reported [BX-1309].
- **Certification slip:** there's no risk-case column, so this is for scale only.
  - Malave: one year of slip costs about 0.09 priced, which takes B's S1 item to about -0.05 before concessions and rework.
  - Pope: one year defers about 0.07 of gain, more than the whole item.
  - So all of B's S1 value rests on a nominal 2055 date with no headroom. I don't plan that way [BX-1318, BX-1319, BX-1275].
- **Doing less, better:** +0.05 in S1 is inside ε, so the plan with less to execute wins [BX-1239]. A is also the higher-confidence plan: its worst column is higher by 1.00 [BX-0548]. In S2, A leads outright by 1.00.
- **Airbus doesn't set the date.** I'm not holding because of Airbus, and I wouldn't launch to pre-empt it either [B-2313]. On the grid, entering service in the same year costs us 1.00, so B is no hedge. The hold rests on ε, debt first and readiness.
- **Premium cap:** 0.05 this round, 0.81 cumulative, against $2B.

**4. Hard rules and red lines.**
- H1: does not apply. The fps is in service; I lifted H1 in Round 1, with evidence.
- H2: cannot bind.
- H3, first half: lapsed after Round 1.
- H3, second half: not triggered, since no A350 Re-engine is on the public record. If Airbus launches one this round, we stand aside (company doctrine [B-0917, B-0858], not my record).
- H4: 0 years of overlap (fps 2033-40, a Re-engine 2050-55). It would pass, so it isn't binding.
- H5: the fps can't be cancelled. A launched Re-engine is never cancelled, so in this last round S2's -1.00 would have been locked in.
- H6: not in play. H7: no trigger.
- H8: 0.81 of $2B, logged.
- My own lines: none is tested by a hold. Certify before rate, no fixed-price development, no blame on the FAA, and no attribution all stand.

**5. Board risks.** The Board would veto 787 Re-engine: launch on its business case: 0.95 short of $1B in S1 and 2.00 short in S2, with S2 sitting exactly at its -$1B limit. With the hold, no item goes to it. board_response answers all five of its recommendations and closes out those carried from Rounds 1-3.

**6. Where doctrine, not my record, drove the choice.**
- The H3 stand-aside, the KPI veto, Pope's +2-year slip prior and the four supplier categories come from profile.md.
- The tie-break [BX-1239, BX-0548] and debt first [BX-1305] are my record. The ε threshold on debt first is an inference.
- The engine code is null because None is the brief's only option. CFM has been locked in since 2040.

**7. What it costs.** 0.05 in S1, logged as a doctrine premium. The 787 Re-engine is forgone on this board: a loss we accept, not a wait. In the real company the readiness work goes on. "I think that we're turning it. I don't think it's turned." [BX-1300] We're further along now, and the same discipline holds.
- Expected scenario: S1 (rivals hold), provisional. My confidence on Airbus is Low.

For S1:
- Airbus repeats that the A350 family, including the A350F, remains its focus, and that it will decide 'when the technology and the case are mature, and not before'.
- It held its widebody in 2030, 2035 and 2045.
- Rolls-Royce's line, no new widebody engine spend without a Re-engine, is a condition on its own spending, not an Airbus commitment.
- A move is real only when it is launched.

S2 stays plausible:
- NGSA has been in service since 2037, which frees Airbus engineering.
- Doctrine expects an A350 Re-engine attempt.

The orders are the same in either column. Hold ties within ε in S1 and wins by 1.00 in S2.. Best grid plan there: In S1 the top row is fps: hold + 787 Re-engine, at +0.71. That's 0.05 above Do Nothing's +0.66, well inside ε ($1B).

I order Do Nothing (fps: hold + 787: hold, +0.66 / +0.57):
- The tie goes to doing less, better [BX-1239] and to the higher-confidence plan [BX-0548], whose worst column is higher by 1.00.
- Debt first and the Board's business case also rule out the Re-engine row. It clears ε in neither column.

In S2, Do Nothing is the top row outright (+0.57 against -0.43).. Premium +0.05 $B: 0.05 is a doctrine premium, forgone in S1 (the expected column) by holding the 787 Re-engine. B's +0.71 against A's +0.66 is inside ε, so doing less, better and the higher-confidence plan decide the tie.

- In S2 the premium would be 0, since holding gains 1.00 there. Holding is the better call whenever the chance of S2 is above 4.8%.
- Objective premium: 0. No order on offer moves the narrowbody path.

Final log:
- Round 1: 0.46 (the 10-year ramp over the 7-year).
- Round 2: 0.
- Round 3: 0.30.
- Round 4: 0.05.
- Cumulative: 0.81, within the $2B cap (H8). Nothing carries forward.

Hard-rule costs are logged apart:
- Round 1, H3: 2.20.
- Round 2, H4: 0.65 (S1 and S3).
- Rounds 3 and 4: none.
- Predictions: **Airbus** (Low confidence):
- Most likely holds the A350 again (S1).
- An A350 Re-engine (S2) is plausible. If it launches, H3's second half means we stand aside, and our hold avoids the -1.00.
- NGSA stays in service on code 7.

**Engine makers:**
- Rolls-Royce launches no UltraFan WB without a Re-engine.
- CFM holds RISE as technology, with no product date, and raises no durability flag on the Ducted.
- The P&W-RR Joint Venture engine stays on NGSA.

**Boeing:**
- The Board isn't called to review, since all four orders are holds.
- The fps holds 940 a year, with narrowbody share at 47% through 2060. Widebody stays at 100 a year (58.8%).
- No live problem is expected. The CFM line and the in-service tail are the watch items.
- Debt falls on Malave's conservative path, solidly investment grade during 2053, with 2052 as upside.

**Final ΔPV:** +0.66 in S1, +0.57 in S2.
- Expected ΔPV +0.66 $B; board after the round +0.66 $B

- Other moves: fps line holds at 940 a year; any step above it is KPI-gated and agreed with the FAA (public); Chief Engineer and Chief Aerospace Safety Officer report directly to the Aerospace Safety Committee each year (public); Certify before building at rate on any derivative; no fixed-price development (public); Supply base funded and hot for 940 plus spares, not contracted above it (private); CFM allocation tracked by airframer (private); In-service support for the fps fleet (private); Close-out funding report to the Finance Committee; debt first, no new equity (private); 787 grows from Charleston; no Re-engine launch (public); Independent estimate check and early-warning rule continue (private); Customer slots quoted only against the KPI-gated line (private)

- Decision rationale: **Decision: plan A, the company default.** NGSA hold, engine code none, A350 hold, Delay Tactics none. Grid +23.37 in S1 and +23.33 in S2; expected PV about $23.36B at my 30% for S2. There is no Board item.

**Team rule, step by step**
1. **Delete red-line and pillars breaches.** Neither plan breaks a company red line:
   - Rule 1 is met: NGSA entered service in 2037.
   - Rules 2 and 7: no cancel is on the menu.
   - Rule 3 is not binding: no 787 Re-engine is on the record.
   - Rules 4 and 5: Delay Tactics are none, the only value.
   - Rule 6 is met: plan B would be one launch with 0 years of overlap.
   - Both plans stand on the five pillars on the record. Wagner adds that plan B could do so only with a public window the engine can meet; 2055 would overstate a 2056 engine.
2. **Survivors.**
   - Plan A passes both colleagues' tests.
   - Plan B fails Toepfer's added-move gate, which is also the company's $1B A350 Re-engine launch test (profile section 6): +0.57 in S1, $0.43B short, and -0.41 in S2.
   - Plan B also fails Wagner's production-readiness test. No widebody engine is on the record for a 2055 entry into service: an UltraFan WB launched in 2050 is ready in 2056, and no fallback exists.
   - Both are soft vetoes, and both colleagues mark them binding. Plan A is the only survivor.
3. **Highest survivor, and the doctrine default.** In my expected column (S1) the best grid row is plan B at +23.94. Hold is $0.57B below it, inside the $1B doctrine default, so I take the default even before the vetoes. The tie-breaks all point the same way: no added entry into service, no strain, no capex start, and fewer Board items.
4. **Override.** None is available, and none is used. Plan B is not $1B better than the best passing option: it leads by +0.57 in S1 and trails by -0.41 in S2. The Board's override review would also fail it. The game's one override closes unused.

**Read of Boeing (the Board's recommendation 1).** I put S2 at about 30%, within a range of 20-40%, down from 40% in 2045.
- Against S2, Boeing said "We're not launching a new program today" and "Today all three aren't there", gave no date, and put debt reduction "far and away" first. Its fps ramp is still KPI-gated, and Rolls-Royce spends nothing on a new widebody engine without a Re-engine.
- For S2: Charleston has headroom, readiness work on the 787's next step continues, and this is the last decision year.
- S2 therefore stays the worst plausible column.
- At 30% the item is worth about +0.28. It is +0.33 at 25%, +0.18 at 40% and +0.08 at 50%, and it breaks even at 58%.
- My answer does not rest on this read: the item's ceiling is +0.57 at 0% for S2, which is below $1B. We decide on our own clock and our own tests.

**Last decision year.** No later round exists. A launch could not be gated, re-tested or cancelled on the board. The cancel condition cannot be reached either, because a 787 Re-engine launched in 2050 cannot enter service before 2055. Proof comes before commitment [AX-0007, AX-0008, A-0364], and the proof is missing on engine, value and line data. Toepfer's check of the option to wait shows that waiting bought the same case, smaller (+0.61 estimated against +0.57 on the grid) and with a heavier relative downside: break-even fell from 64% to 58%.

**In the order I am paid to protect**
- **Pillars.** The task is a fleet in service, with two new engine families in hot and harsh service and no durability claim yet from any maker. Hold adds nothing that could draw on the people, shops or supplier slots behind that work [AX-0073, AX-0081, AX-0015].
- **Ramp.** The NGSA line runs at 1,060 a year with the A320neo transition, under a disciplined and controlled ramp [AX-0010].
- **The Airbus of the 2030s.** That aircraft was delivered, NGSA on its clock in 2037. My instinct favours the widebody derivative, but this case clears neither our engine test nor our value test. The long view continues as staged studies.
- **The Board.** It approves hold as readily as a launch.

**Board-gate lines.** No order differs from the default, so no Board item is submitted. For the record, plan B would have read:
- five pillars: pass, conditional on its window;
- Toepfer: fail (soft veto, binding);
- Wagner: fail on production readiness (soft veto, binding);
- grid ΔPV: +0.57 / -0.41 against hold.
It would also have met the Board's would-veto ground on an unresolved binding ExCo veto. The Board would have nothing to vote, so plan B is not submitted.

**Cost of the decision.** Holding gives up $0.57B in S1, about $0.28B in expectation, and gains $0.41B in S2. Its worst column (+23.33) beats plan B's (+22.92). No PV is traded for the 60/40 objective.
- Expected scenario: S1 (rivals hold), at about 70%. I put S2 (Boeing launches a 787 Re-engine) at about 30%, within a range of 20-40%.
- Against S2: Boeing said "We're not launching a new program today" and "Today all three aren't there", gave no date, and put debt first. Its fps ramp is still KPI-gated, and no widebody engine maker has committed new money.
- For S2: Charleston has headroom, readiness work continues, and this is the last round.
S2 stays the worst plausible column. The decision is the same at any probability, because the item's ceiling is +0.57.. Best grid plan there: NGSA hold + A350 Re-engine, at +23.94 in S1. It fails Toepfer's added-move gate and the company's $1B launch test (+0.57 over hold), and it fails Wagner's production-readiness test (no engine for 2055). Hold is +23.37, $0.57B below and within the $1B doctrine default. Hold is also the only plan that passes both colleagues' tests, and it is better in S2 (+23.33 against +22.92).. Premium +0.00 $B: No premium is paid. Hold is the company default and lies within $1B of the best grid row in my expected column ($0.57B below in S1, about $0.28B in expectation at 30% for S2). It is also the only plan that passes Toepfer's and Wagner's tests and the company's $1B launch test, so it is the highest-grid survivor in its own right. No PV is traded for the 60/40 objective.
- Predictions: **Boeing**
- About 70%: it holds the 787 in 2050 with no Re-engine launch, keeps the fps ramp KPI-gated toward the long-run 47% narrowbody share, and keeps debt reduction first.
- About 30% (S2): it launches a 787 Re-engine. That could enter service no earlier than 2055, and it costs us only about $0.04B on the grid under hold.

**Engine makers**
- None starts a new widebody engine programme.
- Rolls-Royce keeps UltraFan as technology and its Trent durability work as funded.
- CFM and P&W report durability on field data only.
- I expect no durability flag on NGSA before the Board's next review. If one comes, it is answered through supply and fleet action.

**Our position**
- Narrowbody share holds at 53% and widebody at 41.2% (70 a year). Operating profit is $15.9B a year.
- ΔPV is +23.37 in S1 and +23.33 in S2.
- The Board confirms hold with no item to vote.
- Expected ΔPV +23.36 $B; board after the round +23.37 $B

- Other moves: Twice-yearly safety review: the NGSA line, the A320neo transition and the in-service durability record of both NGSA engine families, taken as one package (private); Supply agreements sized to our own needs: the ECSC reports the outcome of the 2045 resizing (public); Closing record of the staged A350 Re-engine case (private); Staged studies for the long view, outside the board (private); ECSC integrity review: supply sizing, hiring and pricing (private); Capital allocation inside the policy (private); Open European engineering recruitment continues (public); Zero-for-zero trade advocacy (public)

What we can share: - the in-service years; - the method: time on wing in cycles on field data against the CFM56 yardstick; - the fleet-action process: root cause, inspection and a parts fix turned on and off in test, accepted by the airframer and the regulator; - enhanced inspection of safety-critical parts in new-make shops and MRO; - the LEAP record above. Airbus's 1,060 and fps's 940 in 2049 are 53% and 47% of the 2,000 NB market, so they are already in the hold row. On the board, the base carries no OE volume above what it carried to 2036. Spares are not on the board. My last supplier data is 2024: 80% of shortages came from 9 suppliers at 15 sites. Doctrine: - OE on time to every airframer; - spares never crowd out OE; - our own supplier, titanium-forging and rare-earth contracts sized to our LEAP, Ducted and GEnx needs, never over-contracted, while keeping shop-visit and inspection capacity whole; - any CFM capacity answer to Airbus's resize or Boeing's rate support made jointly with Safran; - no rate published; - field data from the 9 suppliers before any capacity commitment. |
| Fix, don't redesign | On the widebody, prefer the GEnx upgrade on a proven architecture to a new engine. On the narrowbody, keep RISE technology flowing into the fleet while RISE matures. This does not argue against the Open Fan as the next generation (inference). | pass | GEnx: hold, the only value offered. The upgrade has been in service since 2033 on the GE90/GEnx hot-section lineage and counts +5 pp WB from 2042 (44.8%). RISE technology keeps flowing into the LEAP and Ducted fleets. Any in-service finding gets a parts fix, targeted or fleet-wide, not an engine redesign. |
| Safety call | If a problem in the brief or an order touches safety or durability, it needs a cross-functional team, not one person. | pass | Boeing's review of 'CFM ducted durability in service' touches durability. The frame's red line puts every in-service gate call, and the answer to that review, with one cross-functional supply, engineering and quality team, with Safran's engineers on CFM engines. Enhanced inspection of safety-critical parts stays in new-make shops and MRO. |

- Decision rationale: DECISION: hold on every field (ducted, Open Fan, Embraer partnership, emissions lobbying and genx all hold). There is no Board item, $0 of new R&D, $0 of new strain and a premium of $0.

WHAT GAME, HOW WE WIN [CX-0467]. The narrowbody contest was decided years ago.
- NGSA has been in service since 2037 with CFM, P&W and RR on it.
- fps has been in service since 2040 with CFM alone.
- The Joint Venture engine was ready in 2036 and has been on NGSA since 2037. Ali corrected my frame, which had it 'in service since 2036'. It stays out of everything we publish.
The codes are locked and no slot is open. The game is the installed base we hold [CX-0422], and we win it on safety, quality, delivery and cost, in that order [CX-0658]. Not on share for its own sake [CX-0493].

THE GRID (one column, S1, rivals hold; Ghai reproduced it).
- Hold everything: -38.29 (76.1%). This is the best row.
- Open Fan: -57.46, which is -19.17 against hold: the $8B bill plus $11.17B of 35 pp wait penalty.
- Open Fan + Lobby: -53.67, which is -15.38 against hold: the $9B bill plus $6.38B of 20 pp penalty.
- Lobby beside an Open Fan: +3.79. Lobby alone: about -1.00 (not a grid row).
- Neither Open Fan row adds strain; the $2B is already in hold.
- Ghai confirms the penalty starts in 2050, at about $0.32B a raw point. A 2051 start leaves $1.88B and $1.07B unexplained.
- Nothing comes within the $1B epsilon of hold, so no tie-break is needed.

MY TESTS.
- Additive and paid for the risk [CX-0301]: every launch row loses more than its own bill (2.4x and 1.7x). On this board the Open Fan has no share gain, only cost. It fails.
- Low case first [CX-0248, CX-0536]: the expected scenario is S1, the backlog on the record.
- H2, the airframer sets the date [CX-0205, CX-0469]: a 2050 launch is ready in 2060 at the earliest. That is 23 years after NGSA, 20 after fps and 4 after the narrowbody count closes. It is barred, and the RISE allowance needs a committed airframer, and there is none.
- H3: the Ducted is in service. Nothing to order.
- Suppliers: no new project. Ali: the board's CFM narrowbody count sits below the 2036 level.
- Next generation: no fuel-burn claim is made. The wording check passes: the Ducted is not called the next generation [CX-0217, CX-0183].

SAFRAN GATE (H7).
- Hold orders nothing new on CFM, so there is nothing to gate.
- RISE continues as a 50-50 decision.
- An Open Fan with no airframer path would sit outside joint practice. Its 35 pp loss cuts Safran's half of the same base, so there is no stated reason Safran would agree.
- Lobby serves RISE; the point is moot.
- Any answer on Airbus's long-term-agreement resize, or on support for Boeing's rates, is joint with Safran on standard terms [CX-0184].
- GEnx is GE alone.

SAFETY FIRST. Ali reported against our round-3 promise: no flag raised, and none cleared. The brief carries shares, not cycles; I don't call that a durable fleet [CX-0436]. Boeing's safety committee gets field data through the one cross-functional team with Safran's engineers. We say what we can't yet show. No flag means nothing lowers the booking on evidence.

BOOKING: -38.80 (75.75%). That is S1 less $0.51B, the larger of the two exclusive same-round widebody cases: an UltraFan WB with a re-engine, about -$0.50-0.53B, or a Trent 1000 upgrade, about -$0.41-0.43B.
- It is a raise of $0.81B from -39.60. That is earned: the 2050 bulletin shows no widebody move, the condition I set last round.
- It stays below the record, because the round is still sealed and a stated stance is not data.
- The solo-relaunch corner (-$0.19B) fits inside the cushion.
- No upside is booked.

BIAS NAMED. The installed-base lens ('seed corn' [CX-0256], 'price of admission' [CX-0557]) and the last-round itch both pull toward a launch for the objective. Admission buys a seat. Both airplanes have been flying for a decade, and the seat isn't for sale. Paying $15.38B for a 2060 engine that also breaks 'Dominate' in 2050 is a net objective loss.

Doctrine, not my record, drove these moves:
- Lobby only beside a live Open Fan.
- Supplier sizing to our own needs.
- The allowance-needs-an-airframer reading (profile.md §6 G1).

We close the game the way we played it: hold, deliver, keep RISE whole, book low.
- Expected scenario: S1 (rivals hold), weight 1.0. It is the brief's only column and the only plausible one: both new narrowbodies and the Joint Venture engine are in service, the codes are locked, and no widebody move is on the record.. Best grid plan there: Hold everything: -38.29 (76.1%) in S1. The next best, Open Fan + Lobby, is -53.67, which is $15.38B behind. The submitted plan is the best grid plan.. Premium +0.00 $B: Premium this round is $0.00, and $0.00 of the $4.0B cap is used for the game. The submitted plan is the best grid plan in the only column, so no doctrine or objective premium arises.

RISE allowance, stated apart: $0 of $15B. It closes the game unused.

Objective premium declined, against the best non-RISE plan (hold):
- $15.38B for the cheapest route (Open Fan + Lobby);
- $19.17B for the Open Fan alone.

The allowance cannot carry the $15.38B route, even though $15.38B sits under $15B plus the $2.0B round cap:
(a) The allowance is declared in full against the best non-RISE plan, and $15.38B is $0.38B over $15B. The allowance is doctrine's one exception to the cap, not a base to stack the round cap on. The $17.0B sum is arithmetic, not doctrine (Ghai).
(b) There is no airframer path. Doctrine's gate G1 needs a committed airframer, and both new narrowbodies have been flying since 2037 and 2040.
(c) It buys nothing toward 'Introduce': the engine is ready in 2060, after the 2056 count.
(d) It would break 'Dominate' in 2050, taking it to 37.8% with Lobby or 23.5% alone.
So it is a net objective loss, not a premium.
- Predictions: COLUMN WEIGHTS: S1 at 1.0, the only column. Hold is -38.29 there. I book -38.80 (75.75%).

What I expect on the record: nothing moves on the narrowbody. NB share stays at 56.8% from 2049 to 2056, and WB at 44.8% to 2061, unless someone moves on the widebody in this sealed round.

RISKS OUTSIDE THE COLUMN (named; in no grid row):
(a) A same-round widebody move against GEnx's 44.8%. The two cases are exclusive.
- An UltraFan WB with a 2050 re-engine: about -$0.50B (Ghai) to -$0.53B (mine).
- A Trent 1000 upgrade: about -$0.41B to -$0.43B.
The larger of the two sets the $0.51B cushion. The signals point to none this round: no Re-engine launch, and no widebody engine spend without one.
(b) A rival NB solo relaunched to be ready in 2056: about -$0.19B for one year, on a reading of the rules Ghai can't confirm. It fits inside the cushion if it comes alone.
(c) A 787 or A350 re-engine shifting WB airframe share. Unpriced, and it could cut either way for GEnx.
(d) Ducted and Embraer durability in hot and harsh service, now under an airframer's safety committee review. No flag has been raised, so it is named and unsized; there is no field data to size it. A flag lowers the booking in the round it is found.
(e) Raw materials (titanium forgings, rare earths) as the supplier base is resized. Unsized.
(f) Supplier-dependent volumes, and macro factors (recession, tariffs) that the board's fixed narrowbody market leaves out.

No upside is booked.
- Expected ΔPV −38.80 $B; board after the round −38.29 $B

- Other moves: In-service safety and durability reporting continues on field data: Ducted, Embraer, GEnx and LEAP fleets (public); Ali's in-service report to the Board, as promised in round 3: no flag raised, none cleared (private); Field data to Boeing's Aerospace Safety Committee review of CFM ducted durability in service (private); LEAP and Ducted OE engines and spares on time to every airframer (public); Answer to the airframers' supply signals (resized long-term engine agreements; support for rates): joint with Safran, on standard terms (private); Supplier and raw-materials contracts (titanium forgings, rare earths) sized to our own needs (private); RISE continues whole as technology, 50-50 with Safran, beyond the game window (public); GEnx 787 fleet support, GE alone (public); No reaction to rival or Joint Venture news (private)
