- Decision rationale: DECISION: GTF2 Solo hold, Joint Venture with RR hold. These are the only values brief §3 offers. Neither differs from the default, so no Board item arises. premium is 0, overrides is empty, the expected PV is -2.93 (S1, rivals hold), and the worst column is also -2.93.

TEAM RULE, STEP BY STEP
1. Start from Eddy's proposal: hold/hold.
2. Strike plans that fail a binding veto.
- Mitchill invokes one binding veto, ground 3, against any pricing, share-recovery or free-upgrade campaign. I accept it. I had already struck that campaign in the frame on value, not share [PX-0055][PX-0100].
- His ground 2 (an unconditional 2050 launch) and ground 4 (the final text) are standing conditions. Neither is invoked.
- Eddy's grounds 1 and 2 both pass, with no veto. None of the vetoes bears on hold/hold.
3. Set the safety and durability constraint first [PX-0035][PX-0073].
- No order puts an engine on an airframe. The Joint Venture engine was ready in 2036 for NGSA's 2037 entry into service.
- The constraint now bites on the fleet and on the words. We claim no durability the record does not show, and the brief shows none for the thirteenth year.
4. Optimise inside the constraint. There is one row and one column, so there is nothing to optimise on the grid. What I can still decide is the fleet record, the close-out and the last word.

TIE-BREAKS
- The fleet beats a new engine [PX-0080]: fleet lift sits ahead of new builds in the order of call.
- 'Miss a cycle' [PX-0071] asks for nothing. No live airframe is outside service: NGSA since 2037, fps since 2040. This cycle was answered in 2030 and 2035.
- A rival's discount never moves the answer [PX-0055].

STRUCK FOR THE RECORD: AN UNCONDITIONAL 2050 LAUNCH
It is not a legal value. It would be ready in 2056, the last NB year the board counts, with no airframe to serve. On Mitchill's numbers it is worth at most +0.26 PV against $2.00B, an edge of -1.74, with no cancel round. It would fail:
- the committed-airframer red line [P-0746];
- my visible-demand test [PX-0109];
- Mitchill's ground 2;
- Eddy's readiness test;
- the Board's return hurdle.

CLOSING PROGRAMME NPV REVIEW (the Board's last annual review; Mitchill's figures, which I re-checked in work/r4_decide_check.py)
ΔPV -2.93 = engine value -0.93 + R&D -2.00.
- The R&D is undiscounted, one line in 2035 against cash of about $0.33B a year in 2030-35. There has been no fresh $2B and no write-off since.
- Gains: +0.145 PV in 2038-40.
- Losses: -1.115 PV in 2043-56, of which -0.553 falls in 2043-49 and -0.563 (-7.3 nominal) is still to come in 2050-56.
- That nets to -0.97 on rounded rows, against the brief's -0.93. The gap is rounding.
Look-backs, labelled as INFERENCE and private:
- +5.51 for the 2035 commit at $0 of new R&D;
- about +24.8 against being left off NGSA, the top of the range and not a point estimate.
Answer to shareowners: the $2B defended the narrowbody franchise. The residual gap is the airframe split after fps's 2040 entry into service, which no P&W order reached. I do not present -0.93 as an operational number without the $2B.

CAPITAL ORDER CLOSE
- $0 of new R&D against the ~$6B envelope.
- The dividend is covered and declared by the Board [PX-0081].
- Debt goes back to pre-ASR levels next, with 'no date' because the brief has no path [P-0686].
- Buybacks are the fill-in [P-0669].
- The envelope stretch was unused for the whole game.
- A buyback at ΔPV 0 is the benchmark for any spend.

DISCLOSURE
I took all four of the Board's fixes and both colleagues' advice.
(a) The share line is 21.6% in 2049, down from 24.8% in 2039. It has no forecast and attributes nothing to an airframer.
(b) The public Hot Section text is stripped of every ExCo internal: the ground passes, the slipped-dates line, 'the brief has no count' and 'no effect on the game board'. I accept that the 2045 text went out unchanged when both colleagues had advised the strip. That was my miss, and it is corrected.
(c) The six-year line is split, so that the date attaches to readiness rather than to the commitment. Mitchill's wording and Eddy's are merged: 'ready not before six years from launch, and it will enter service only once durability is proven.'
(d) The Joint Venture is 'on the terms we published'. The restated revenue line is gone.
I also added Eddy's 'measured on the fleet', to meet CFM's field-data bar. The text has no programme cost figure and no dated recovery, and nothing on Airbus's resizing.

RED-LINE FLAGS
- Neither colleague flagged a company red-line breach on the orders, the other moves or the draft.
- Mitchill's binding ground-3 veto stands against an option I had already struck.
- No flag needs rebutting.

BIASES I NAME
- Optimism on operating dates [PX-0017][PX-0098][PX-0104]. The six-year line now reads 'not before', and there is no dated AOG recovery.
- Architecture loyalty [PX-0029]. The airframe-split reading of the gap is supported by the bulletin. But we hold no fleet data, so I do not use it to say the fleet is fine. Eddy names the same risk from his 'not a surprise' remark [PX-0112].
- Incrementalism carries no cost this round.

RECORD VERSUS DOCTRINE
- My own record grounds fleet first, value not share, the capital order and early disclosure.
- The Joint Venture and derivative discipline is company doctrine [P-1233][P-1240][P-1563][P-1875] and Eddy's engineering. My own record there is Low.
- Applying 'miss a cycle' and demand visibility to engines is inference from RTX-wide statements.
- Expected scenario: S1 (rivals hold), the only column in brief §3, probability 1.00. Nobody launched anything in 2045, both new narrowbodies are in service (NGSA since 2037 on code 7, fps since 2040 on code 3), and neither code can change.. Best grid plan there: GTF2 Solo hold, Joint Venture with RR hold: -2.93 $B (97.3%) in S1. It is the only row on the grid.. Premium +0.00 $B: No premium. There is one legal row, so no doctrine-favoured or objective-serving order differs from the best expected PV. No grid order reaches the Capitalise gap (-2.93), and chasing it with price fails Mitchill's binding ground 3. The $1B team cap and the Board's $0.5B limit both close the game unused.
- Predictions: S1 (rivals hold): 1.00. It is the only column in brief §3. Expected column: S1. Expected PV of hold/hold: -2.93.

Qualitative expectations, which the board does not price:
- P&W NB engine share holds at 21.6% to 2056, as the brief carries it.
- No airframe code changes.
- No new narrowbody, Re-engine or Joint Venture build standard is launched in 2050.
- Expected ΔPV −2.93 $B; board after the round −2.93 $B

- Other moves: GTF Hot Section durability kit for the installed fleet (public); Final fleet report to the Board: Joint Venture engine fleet on NGSA and the GTF fleet (Eddy's report, adopted) (private); Order of call on pacing parts, shops and engineers, and the 2050 parts result (private); Closing programme NPV review to the Board (Mitchill's review, adopted) (private); Capital-order close, and the after-game technology rule (private); Joint Venture and derivative discipline, standing after the game (private)

No narrowbody airframe is still live and no selection is open, so no further narrowbody readiness test applies. |

Any safety or durability flag goes to the Board in this package with its fleet action. | not applicable | Fleet exposure, summed from the brief's year table: 12,139 RR-share narrowbody engines delivered 2037-2049, 13,002 by the end of 2050. Of these, 3,530 were delivered 2046-2049, after my 2045 report. Time on wing: the board does not model it and the brief gives no figure, so I give none. Full-life (life-limited) parts: no figure in the brief, so I give none. Shop visits: no narrowbody figure in the brief, so I give none. The GTF fleet, the new engine fleet and A320neo deliveries keep first call on P&W's parts, shops and engineers. Reading for the Board: until (a) and (b) are secured, our view of the gear and core rests on P&W's own fleet reporting. This is an oversight note. It is not a flag and not a veto. |

A fail is advice, not a veto. | fail | Launched 2050, it is ready in 2056. - S1: no Re-engine is on the record, so it never counts. That gives about +0.26 to +0.29, close to the grid's +0.27. Strain: 2050-53, three years beside the Joint Venture. No date of any kind on a new engine, a Joint Venture build standard or widebody. No grid value is changed. |

- Decision rationale: Decision by the team rule: all four fields at hold. No Board item.

1. Start from my proposal: all four at hold. Both memos recommend the same orders.

2. Binding vetoes. McCabe withholds her co-signature on `Trent 1000 upgrade: launch` and on `UltraFan WB: launch`. Her grounds are the hurdle, the unselected downside, and no round left in which to recover either loss. Both vetoes are binding and both plans are struck. She invokes no balance-sheet veto. Watson invokes no Civil veto. His concurrence rests on two conditions: no date of any kind in public, and the in-service support and report carried in this package. Both are met. overrides is empty.

3. Scenario and weights. S1 0.90 / S2 0.10, confirmed by McCabe. S2 needs both Re-engines, so it can be no likelier than its weaker leg. Boeing ('Today all three aren't there', no date, no matured widebody engine for a 2050 EIS) is close to nothing. Airbus ('keeps studying the options') is at most a signal. Expected scenario: S1, rivals hold.

4. Grid (brief), each item against 'UltraFan NB: hold', S1 / S2:
- All-hold: +20.55 / +20.90. EV 20.585 (+20.59); +20.60 on 0.85/0.15, +20.57 on 0.95/0.05. Worst column +20.55. Best row in both columns.
- T1000 upgrade: row +13.82 / +14.27, item -6.73 / -6.63, EV -6.72 under any weights. Split: +0.27 / +0.37 of engine value, counting from 2053, against a $2B bill and $5B of strain. It would still be -1.73 without strain. The worst column is 3.4 times its own bill.
- UltraFan WB: row +11.55 / +12.57, item -9.00 / -8.33, EV -8.93. Split: 0 / +0.67 against $4B and $5B. It would be ready in 2056, a year after S2's 2055 Re-engines. It fails airframers first: no Re-engine is on the record.
- Neither item can move a measured year. Neither can be cancelled later, because 2050 is the last decision year.
- Watson and McCabe both read S1's -9.00 = -4 - 5 exactly, so the $5B strain stands beside the Joint Venture as a running narrowbody project.

5. Doctrine premium. None is kept or needed. The upgrade gap ($6.72B) and the UltraFan WB gap ($8.93B) are both above the $3B cap, and UltraFan WB is also above its $1B sub-cap. My own 787 line ('Why not?' [RX-0101, RX-0117]) does not survive the numbers. Premium $0B, item by item.

6. Red-line check on the final orders. No UltraFan launch, no date of any kind, no overlap, no cancel. The 'no upgrade while an UltraFan is in development' line is not engaged as written; the strain is its priced cost. No colleague flagged a breach. Nothing to strike.

7. Safety and support (doctrine and the Board's ask, not my record). Watson's in-service report goes to the Board with these orders.
- Fleet: 12,139 RR-share engines delivered 2037-2049, 13,002 by the end of 2050.
- Time on wing, life-limited parts and shop visits: the brief gives no figure, so none is reported. I will not put a number in public that fleet data does not support.
- No safety or durability flag is on the record.
- Oversight gap, said plainly. RR technical review rights and gear-and-core repair data or a module pool were asked for in 2045 and are not shown as secured. Until they are, our view of that module rests on P&W's own fleet reporting, and our partner shops cannot repair it. Both asks stay live as private moves. RR keeps its own fleet tracking.
- McCabe holds the shared-risk sizing at £1.2-2.9bn (peak £0.3-0.7bn). The installed-fleet top of about £4.3bn sits inside the twice case of about £5.7bn, which fits without equity, above about 1.5x leverage, or a dividend cut. It books nothing.

Closing account (R4-7).
- 2030, airframers first [RX-0058]. `launch if selected` spent nothing until NGSA chose us. It triggered on NGSA's code 7.
- 2035, partner first once P&W had committed. `Joint Venture with P&W: commit` halved the bill. The engine was ready in 2036 on GTF2's clock, with nothing compressed. RR was fitted at NGSA's entry in 2037 at 24.1% (965 engines), peaked at 990 in 2039, and held level with P&W through 2049.
- Arithmetic (McCabe): +26.26 engine value - 4.00 R&D - 1.71 write-off = +20.55 on hold. The $2.29B of remaining Solo spend was never needed.
- Declared expected PV: 2030 +3.37, 2035 +15.99, 2045 +20.58. Outcome: +20.55 / +20.90, +20.59 expected. Guide low, then beat.
- Objective met: narrowbody 22.9% (2045) and 21.6% (2050); widebody 58.0%, 55.2% and 55.2%.
- What we missed, said plainly:
  - The 2035 widebody plan assumed the strain would lift once UltraFan NB left development. It did not. On this board the formed Joint Venture is a running project, and that closed the Trent 1000 upgrade for good. My 2035 condition, nothing widebody beside it, meant no cash was lost to it, but I did not regain 787 share.
  - The outcome sits below the 2030 code-7 column of +32.60. That column held rivals still; their moves since took the difference (McCabe's inference).
  - The narrowbody drift from 24.8% to 21.6% is airframe mix, with fps on CFM. It is not a lever we held.

'A milestone on our journey, not our final destination' [RX-0062].
- Expected scenario: S1: rivals hold (weight 0.90). No 787 or A350 Re-engine is launched in 2050. S2 (both Re-engines, weight 0.10) is capped by its weaker leg, Boeing's 'Today all three aren't there'.. Best grid plan there: 'UltraFan NB: hold' (all four fields at hold): +20.55 in S1 and +20.90 in S2, the best row in both columns. EV +20.59 on 0.90 / 0.10 (20.585).. Premium +0.00 $B: The orders are the best grid plan in both columns, so the premium is $0B item by item: UltraFan NB Solo 0, Joint Venture with P&W 0, UltraFan WB 0, Trent 1000 upgrade 0. The doctrine-favoured upgrade would have cost $6.72B and UltraFan WB $8.93B. Both are above the $3B cap, and UltraFan WB is also above its $1B sub-cap, so neither could be kept as a declared premium.
- Predictions: - Rivals hold in 2050 (S1, 0.90).
- Boeing launches no 787 Re-engine and gives no date. Airbus keeps studying A350 options on its own clock, with no launch. If either launches, RR's widebody share still stays above 50% to the board's end on the hold row (inference).
- P&W keeps its published Joint Venture terms: the gear and core stay its own module, with no technology licence, and its fleet keeps first call on its shops. Review rights and a module pool stay unsecured this round (inference).
- No safety or durability flag on the Joint Venture engine reaches the record in 2050.
- CFM keeps reporting durability on field data. The GEnx upgrade's 2.8 pp is already in the hold row.
- Outcome: ΔPV +20.55 (S1) / +20.90 (S2). Both parts of the objective are met.
- Expected ΔPV +20.59 $B; board after the round +20.55 $B

- Other moves: Joint Venture engine in service: safety, durability and support first (public); Trent fleet durability (time on wing) continues as funded (public); UltraFan technology continues as technology (public); Watson's in-service report on the Joint Venture engine to the Board, with this package (private); Oversight under the shared-risk terms: technical review rights and gear-and-core module pool (private); Partner-shop support for the Joint Venture fleet (private); Supplier contracts sized to our own year table (private); Off-grid shared-risk sizing held (McCabe) (private); Capital frame held (private); Pricing discipline and widebody policy (private)


## Against the earlier game

The orders differ in only four places, all in 2030 and 2035; in 2045 and 2050 every company held in both games. The earlier game had one strategist agent per company, deciding as its leadership team, on the earlier version of the dashboard. This game had four agents per company on the new version. Both changed at once, so the comparison alone cannot say what the ExCo-and-Board structure did. The decision records show why each order differs. The table below values each game's final state on both versions of the dashboard.

| Round, company | Earlier game (one strategist agent, earlier board) | This game (ExCo and Board, new board) |
|---|---|---|
| R1 Boeing | Launch fps, 7-year ramp-up (engines requested: CFM); Increase 737 rate | Launch fps, 10-year ramp-up (engines requested: CFM) |
| R1 Airbus | Launch NGSA (engines requested: RR, P&W and CFM) | Launch NGSA (engines requested: RR, P&W and CFM) |
| R1 CFM/GE | Launch ducted engine; Partner with Embraer; GEnx upgrade | Launch ducted engine; Partner with Embraer; GEnx upgrade |
| R1 Pratt & Whitney | Launch GTF2 if selected | Launch GTF2 if selected |
| R1 Rolls-Royce | Launch UltraFan NB if selected | Launch UltraFan NB if selected |
| R2 Boeing | Do Nothing | Do Nothing |
| R2 Airbus | Delay Tactics: supply-chain bottleneck (covert) | Do Nothing |
| R2 CFM/GE | Do Nothing | Do Nothing |
| R2 Pratt & Whitney | Commit to Joint Venture with RR | Commit to Joint Venture with RR |
| R2 Rolls-Royce | Do Nothing | Commit to Joint Venture with P&W |
| R3 Boeing | Do Nothing | Do Nothing |
| R3 Airbus | Do Nothing | Do Nothing |
| R3 CFM/GE | Do Nothing | Do Nothing |
| R3 Pratt & Whitney | Do Nothing | Do Nothing |
| R3 Rolls-Royce | Do Nothing | Do Nothing |
| R4 Boeing | Do Nothing | Do Nothing |
| R4 Airbus | Do Nothing | Do Nothing |
| R4 CFM/GE | Do Nothing | Do Nothing |
| R4 Pratt & Whitney | Do Nothing | Do Nothing |
| R4 Rolls-Royce | Do Nothing | Do Nothing |

Final ΔPV, $B, each game's final state on both boards:

| Company | Earlier game, earlier board (as played) | Earlier game's state, new board | This game, new board (as played) | This game's state, earlier board |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Boeing | +1.85 | −1.57 | +0.66 | −0.66 |
| Airbus | +15.61 | +17.58 | +23.37 | +32.09 |
| CFM/GE | −25.17 | −28.63 | −38.29 | −42.29 |
| Pratt & Whitney | −10.64 | −9.40 | −2.93 | −1.20 |
| Rolls-Royce | +15.68 | +17.30 | +20.55 | +21.90 |

**Four orders differ.**
- 2030, Boeing: the 10-year ramp here, the 7-year before; and no 737 rate increase here, an increase before.
- 2035, Airbus: no Delay Tactics here, a covert bottleneck before.
- 2035, Rolls-Royce: a Joint Venture commitment here, a hold before. P&W committed in both games.
- Everything else matches: NGSA on all three makers, fps on CFM alone, CFM/GE's three 2030 moves, and P&W's and Rolls-Royce's conditional launches.

**In value, the dashboard change repriced one decision: Boeing's ramp.**
- The earlier version gave the 7-year ramp a 5-point first-mover bonus. On it the 7-year led in every case, by $5.20B where Airbus launched NGSA alone. The earlier strategist: the 10-year "costs $5.2B against NGSA, beyond the $2B cap".
- The new version drops the bonus: the first new narrowbody in service gains 1 point a year while alone, and keeps it. In Boeing's expected case the two ramps were $0.46B apart, inside the $1B margin its rules treat as a tie, and the CEO's stated preferences took the 10-year.
- The 2030 stage game agrees: its pure equilibria have the 7-year ramp on the earlier version and the 10-year on the new one.
- A replay of all 16 combinations of the four differences on both versions (data/replay16.json) finds the rate hold, the absent Delay Tactics move and the Joint Venture worth the same on either, within $0.01B. Each order's effect quoted below is averaged over every combination of the other three; the ramp and the Delay Tactics move interact, by about ±$0.25B.
- All of the dashboard change's effect on the value of the four differences sits in the ramp. It also cut Airbus's cash headroom (see Delay Tactics).

**The 737 rate increase: a test declared in advance, and kept.**
- The grid was the same on both versions: beside an fps launch the increase cost $0.74B in every case.
- The earlier strategist took it anyway, as a $0.74B premium under its team rule to raise the rate in the first round.
- Here the Boeing Board listed it among the orders it would veto ("Its item value is −0.74 in every column"), and Ortberg's profile allows the increase only if it adds value. The CEO held; his CFO and operating head advised the same.
- Effect of holding the rate, on either version: +$0.74B for Boeing and +$1.86B for Airbus. Boeing stays at 40% in 2032-36 instead of 45%, and Airbus meets its A320 objective (60% against 55%).

**Delay Tactics: integrity read two ways, a stricter condition and a cash line.**
- One move was worth less here (+$1.65B against +$2.16B). The replay puts that on Boeing's 10-year ramp, not the dashboard: against a 7-year fps the move is worth $2.15B to $2.16B on either version.
- The earlier strategist read the bottleneck as Airbus's "first claim on scarce capacity", the only form inside its integrity pillar. It read the team's stricter condition (the move must change who enters service first) as met, and passed its $5.2B cash guardrail at $4.8B of peak spend.
- Here the Airbus Board, its CFO and its operating head barred the bottleneck on integrity. Faury kept the team's stricter condition, which on its own keeps poaching off, and the Board's cash line also ruled poaching out. NGSA's bill is larger on the new version ($35.52B against $30.07B), which left only $0.13B of headroom.
- Effect of no Delay Tactics move: Boeing +$1.69B, Airbus −$1.90B, CFM/GE +$1.61B, P&W and Rolls-Royce −$0.66B each.

**The Joint Venture: a red-line clause taken from the Board's test.**
- In both games a Rolls-Royce commitment in 2035 was worth +$2.29B, but only if P&W committed in the same round, which it did in both.
- The earlier strategist's rule was to commit only after P&W's commitment was public, so with sealed orders it held. By 2045 joining was worth nothing.
- Here the CEO's red line also allowed a commitment once a live airframe's engine selection included P&W, the Board's partner test, and NGSA met it. For Rolls-Royce, the governance allowed the commitment; no test required it. For P&W, the RTX Board recommended it.
- Worth the same on both versions: Rolls-Royce +$2.29B, P&W +$5.51B, CFM/GE −$7.09B.

**Company by company.**
Each change splits into the dashboard change (the earlier game's state on both versions) and the order change (both states on the new version).
- Boeing −$1.18B: dashboard change −$3.42B, order change +$2.23B.
- Airbus +$7.76B: dashboard change +$1.98B, order change +$5.79B, mostly Boeing's ramp (+$5.83B) and rate hold (+$1.86B), less Airbus's own forgone Delay Tactics move (−$1.90B).
- CFM/GE −$13.11B, with the same orders in both games: dashboard change −$3.45B, others' orders −$9.66B, mostly the Joint Venture (−$7.09B).
- P&W +$7.71B, also with the same orders: dashboard change +$1.24B, others' orders +$6.47B, mostly the Joint Venture (+$5.51B).
- Rolls-Royce +$4.87B: dashboard change +$1.62B, order change +$3.25B, of which its own commitment +$2.29B.
- Objectives: five of ten met in each game, but different ones. Boeing kept incumbency before and lost it here, a cost the CEO and Board recorded when choosing the 10-year ramp; Airbus lost A320 protection before and kept it here.

**What the comparison cannot say.**
- The table values fixed end states. It replays no game, so it says nothing about what the companies would have chosen on the other version of the dashboard.
- An order change swaps every company's orders at once. CFM/GE's and P&W's own orders were the same in both games.
- Where the records show a governance mechanism (the rate increase, Delay Tactics, the Joint Venture), it is a test declared in advance and complied with, never a veto exercised. The ramp changed with the dashboard, and the ExCo chose it against its Board's advice.

## Method

How the game was run
- Dashboard. Your combined_dash.py, run headless (never in Streamlit) at the Game_v3.txt snapshot. Solved by the
game master (GM), it reproduces the snapshot: airframers 4 Pure Nash and 12 Near-Nash, engines 1 Pure Nash and 11
Near-Nash, with every rounded yield matching. Changes from the earlier version of the dashboard:
- The second new narrowbody no longer climbs to 50/50. The first in service gains 1 point a year while alone and keeps
that edge: the long-run split is 50/50 shifted 1 point per year of lead, with the leader capped at 80%.
- No 7-year fps share bonus. The two-front strain is overlap-aware and built in. Re-engines default to 2042.
- Bills: fps $65.00B (7-year) and $60.32B (10-year), NGSA $35.52B. Narrowbody obsolescence 30%.
- Players. 20 AI agents. In each company, the CEO, CFO and operating head of the default 2026 executive
committee, each profiled from that person's own public words (transcripts and filings), and the Board, profiled from
transcripts, filings and web search, with a culture score (risk aversion, time horizon). Where an executive's record was
thin, the seat's tests were derived from the role or company doctrine and labelled low confidence (Pope, Toepfer,
Wagner).
- Boeing: Kelly Ortberg, Jay Malave, Stephanie Pope and the Boeing Board.
- Airbus: Guillaume Faury, Thomas Toepfer, Lars Wagner and the Airbus Board.
- CFM/GE: Larry Culp, Rahul Ghai, Mohamed Ali and the GE Aerospace Board, with Safran's consent on CFM
programmes.
- P&W: Chris Calio, Neil Mitchill, Shane Eddy and the RTX Board.
- Rolls-Royce: Tufan Erginbilgic, Helen McCabe, Rob Watson and the Rolls-Royce Board.
- They are AI agents, not the people.
- Each round, in each company.
- The Board gives guidance. The CEO frames the choice. The CFO and the operating head test it, independently.
- The CEO decides. The CFO and operating head run a veto check, and the CEO revises once if a binding veto or a
red-line flag stands.
- Each Board item (any order other than hold) goes to Board review: approve, or veto with acceptable alternatives.
The CEO revises once within a Board veto. The Board confirms, and a still-vetoed item reverts to the default. A missing
Board review fails closed.
- Each company's decision rule and veto grounds come from its team profile.
- Each company's chain ran as its own workflow, in parallel, in every round; nothing passed between companies.
Within a company the GM passed only that company's own memos between seats.
- Information. As in the earlier game: the public bulletin plus a private brief per company, with its own
position, projection and objective status, and its own payoff grid against a few public scenarios. Only the GM saw the
dashboard.
- Isolation. The hardened path hook from the earlier game, extended to the new dashboard file and snapshot. It
denies other companies' files and briefs, the dashboard file, the snapshot, GM code, earlier reports and runs, and the
GM's scratch files and transcripts. From 2035 each company had its own scratch folder.
- An audit of every tool call found all 20 company-rounds clean.
- In 2030 the agents tried 13 times to write drafts to the session scratchpad their environment advertises. The
hook denied every attempt, and nothing was written.
- Other denials were the hook's conservative rules (command substitution, wildcards) applied to the companies' own
files.
- Out-of-date figures. The agents' standing instructions load when the session starts, before the Boeing and
Airbus files were updated for the new dashboard. So the running agents still quoted the earlier dashboard's fps and NGSA
bills, and figures derived from them (NGSA's yearly spend, Boeing's debt penalties), in eleven lines.
- Everything they read during play (brief, public rules, game guide) used the new dashboard.
- In 2030 both airframers' agents noticed. Airbus used the current $35.52B NGSA bill; its CFO noted the difference
moves headroom, not any result. Malave flagged Boeing's old debt-penalty figures as stale, so the current ones were
used.
- From 2035 each company's prompts stated its own current figures. The engine makers' files had no changed
figures.
- Rounds. Four sealed, simultaneous rounds: 2030, 2035, 2045, 2050. Each round's value is the GM valuation of
the state after that round, assuming nobody moves again. The final round's value is the game outcome.
Rules the GM added to the static dashboard (as in the earlier game)
- Time. A programme enters service, or an engine is ready, after its development time from the decision year.
- Airframes: fps 7 or 10 years, fps via Embraer 10, NGSA 7, Re-engines 5.
- Engines: ducted 6, GTF2 6, UltraFan NB 7, UltraFan WB 6, Trent and GEnx upgrades 3, the Embraer partnership 7, the
Joint Venture 6 or an inherited schedule. Open Fan takes 10 years and is never ready before 2045.
- Cancellations. Airframes write off the bill × years elapsed ÷ development years, with the dashboard's debt
penalty. Engines write off R&D × years elapsed ÷ development years, undiscounted like the dashboard's R&D.
- Delay Tactics are covert: the order and its cause are never published. None was ordered in this game.
- Engines. The dashboard's engine game is applied year by year, splitting each airframer's narrowbody share
equally among the engines fitted to it.
- Each narrowbody engine move counts from the later of its ready year and the first new narrowbody's entry into service (2037 here).
- One change from the earlier game: widebody engine upgrades now count from the year the dashboard starts its
widebody changes (the first Re-engine's entry into service, or 2042), as the dashboard does. On the earlier version that
year was always 2035, so the earlier game is unchanged.
- Joint Venture. It forms when both makers commit, without an airframer selecting the Joint Venture engine. It
folds in solo programmes, writing off spend beyond the maker's share, and inherits the most advanced folded
schedule.
- Tests. The model tests, on both versions of the dashboard: dashboard reproduction, 648 engine move
combinations and the snapshot equilibria. The replay tests: both games replay exactly, and no played year changes. The
isolation tests: 292 cases.
What the numbers are
- ΔPV and yield. ΔPV is the change in present value at 2026 against the status quo (nobody launches anything),
at each company's dashboard WACC: Boeing 10.5%, Airbus 8%, CFM/GE 8.5%, P&W and Rolls-Royce 10%. Yield = 100 + 100 ×
ΔPV ÷ enterprise value.
- Dashboard conventions. Airframe programme spend is charged in full at entry into service. Engine R&D is
undiscounted. Engine value is the lifetime value per engine delivered: price × 1.6, rising 2.2% a year.
- Regret. The final ΔPV a company would have had with its best alternative orders in that round, holding every
other order in the game as played (later rounds re-run), minus its actual final ΔPV.
- Your dashboard alone. The table below sets each final value next to your dashboard alone. The airframers
match exactly. For the engine makers the dashboard fixes a 50/50 airframer split and locks P&W to GTF2 Solo for the
engine selections in this game; the GM's Joint Venture and year-by-year split account for the difference.

- Boeing: No difference. The airframer values are your dashboard's own, at the game's dates.
- Airbus: No difference. The airframer values are your dashboard's own, at the game's dates.
- CFM/GE: Dashboard alone −$23.83B. Both partners' Joint Venture moves −$7.09B: the dashboard locks P&W to GTF2 Solo for the engine selections in this game, so there only Rolls-Royce's Joint Venture move applies. The year-by-year split −$7.37B: Boeing has 37-47% of narrowbodies, not 50%, and fps carries only CFM engines.
- Pratt & Whitney: Dashboard alone −$8.65B. Both Joint Venture moves +$2.75B. The year-by-year split +$2.96B.
- Rolls-Royce: Dashboard alone +$16.55B. The fold-in write-off −$1.71B. Both Joint Venture moves +$2.75B. The year-by-year split +$2.96B.

With a fixed 50/50 split and the board's own engine moves, the GM engine model gives the board's numbers exactly.
